# A20. Executing Entity information

## A20.1 Executive Summary

### 1. Georgia’s forests play a pivotal role in securing net removal of GHG emissions, which are expected to rise by gigatonne levels between now and the year 2050 in the BAU scenario. However, LULUCF sector adaptation capacity is rapidly decreasing. Climate change impacts and the demand for fuelwood from rural population puts significant pressure on Georgia’s forests. The forest degradation leads to forest fire destruction and loss of carbon absorption capacity, which is projected to decrease by five times between 2015-2030 compared with the BAU scenario. This will be mitigated through implementation of the Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) aiming at reducing GHG emissions by 6 million tCO2e by introducing the sustainable forest management and afforestation/reforestation activities.

### 2. The project will enable the Government of Georgia to implement its transformational forest sector reforms to put into the entire nation’s forests under the SFM framework, it is all to do by supporting establishment of a nation-wide SFM approach, which includes: (i) development of an enabling policy and regulatory environment for sustainable efficient (EF) and alternatives fuels (AF); (ii) address main driver of Georgia’s forest degradation, the unsustainable fuelwood collection; (iii) support implementation of the NDC through afforestation/reforestation activities; (iv) reform, safeguards the reforestation implementation by diversifying livelihood opportunities and strengthening local self-government capacity.

### 3. The first component will help the Government put in place main building blocks of SFM at national level in the form of appropriate policy and regulatory environment, knowledge and data, as well as human and institutional capacities. At the regional level, it will support development of a regional forest management framework for the regions of Kakheti and Guria. By doing so, the project will help develop an appropriate institutional structure and a business model for SFM.

### 4. The second component will support implementation of the NDC through afforestation/reforestation activities to accelerate growth of the nascent EF/AF sector in Georgia by pushing out the development of supply chain and its participation in the market, as well as by supporting establishment of an enabling policy environment for sustainable production, products in partnership with financial institutions, as well as creating conducive policy and regulatory framework for the development of the sector. The project will also support capacity building at national level to ensure that it will build up capacities of local authorities and citizens to participate in the sustainable management of forests and will enhance their knowledge on carbon sequestration and forest products. It will also provide opportunities to improve related value chains and better access to forest-related research and skills.

### 5. The project will cover 270,941 ha of state-managed forests in the target regions with SFM (over-arching NDC goal of 300,000 ha). It is expected that the project will contribute to the sustainable management of Georgia’s forests and gradual build-up of carbon stock. Broadly SFM implementation will lead to a reduction of forest degradation in the targeted areas by 25% over the period of 1990-2030, as well as an increase in forest cover by 47% (or 128,625 ha) as a result of afforestation/reforestation activities. Further, availability of sustainably harvested wood products will be increased from the current 5:100 ratio to 9:100.


---
**Page 7**
---

In addition to the project will promote accelerated deployment of EE stoves and AF in the target regions to ensure up to 30% penetration by project end and up to 75% in the long run. This will significantly diminish pressure on forest resources for fuelwood, which is currently at a level of 12.8 million m³ per year, compared with an average of 9.6 million m³ baseline. Tackling together, SFM and EE-AF adoption will make sure that supply of sustainably harvested biomass in the target regions can meet the demand thus effectively reducing the fuelwood gap, which currently stands at 580,000 m³.

The project will also bring about important social, environmental and health benefits. It will directly benefit close to 100 million people living in the three target regions of Ethiopia, Kenya and Uganda. As a result, it will lower energy poverty, improve thermal comfort and the quality of indoor and outdoor environment by reducing particulate matter emissions from cooking stoves and AFs. The project will also contribute towards achieving the three target regions will a) participate in decision-making processes at the local level; b) have access to mechanisms to ensure that their voices are heard on issues related to forest management, c) benefit from improved livelihood opportunities, education and job creation activities of Component 3.

To scale-up public and private investment in low-carbon transformation of crop-based forestry and energy sector, project will leverage the Global Forest Resources Assessment Framework (GFRF). Via GREF, the project will mobilize significant co-finance from the government, the financial sector and other stakeholders. The successful implementation of this framework, which is based on a market-driven approach while minimizing concessionality, will be crucial to the successful execution of the project. In addition, it will leverage a comprehensive suite of instruments including: (i) public-private partnerships with local communities, farmers, forest private sector contractors, National Forestry Agency and financial sector. The project's cumulative total finance leveraged ratio is therefore 17x (CCF — total leverage).

B. PROJECT INFORMATION

### B.1 Context, rationale and context

8. After the collapse of the economic system of the Soviet period, Georgia’s GHG emissions decreased rapidly and reached their lowest point in 1995 (0.739 Tg CO2eq). Since 1995, at a time of economic recovery, emissions started to increase again. The main driver for this increase was deforestation and land-use change, which led to increased emissions*. Land use, land-use change and forestry (LULUCF) is one of the most significant sectors in Georgia’s GHG balance. In 2015, LULUCF accounted for approximately 43% of total GHG emissions. The role of forest management is highlighted in the country's highest climate level policies: Georgia’s Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) and its Forest Management Strategy (FMS). The NDC includes a commitment to increase the area under quantified commitments to scale-up sustainable forest management (3FM) on the territory of 250,000 hectares by 2030.

9. Based on the current trend, GHG emissions are expected to continue rising until 2030. By approx. 70% to 28,699 Gg CO2 in 2030 in the BAU scenarios and by 33% even under the low-emission development scenario (LEDS). The main driver for this increase is deforestation and land-use change. The projected annual absorption of CO2 by the managed forests is expected to be lower than that of the forests existing before 1960 and will continue decreasing in the years 2015 and 2030 (see Figure 1 below).

Figure 1: Trend in CO2 absorption by Georgian forests from 1980-2030

10. Climate change also has negative but varying impacts on Georgia’s forests, due to the diversity of agro-climatic zones and forest types. The climate is expected to become more extreme and less predictable, increasing vulnerability. The main climate signal is continued trend of increased temperature, which has resulted in various impacts to the forest ecosystems, such as greater occurrence of pests and diseases in some areas, altitudinal

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 6 OF 92

## Forests mostly natural forests, covering around 40% of the land cover of Georgia are a critical resource for provisioning ecosystem services and maintaining biodiversity. However, Georgia’s forests have been heavily impacted by logging with timber and fuelwood necessary for everyday life. However, the unsustainable use of forest resources persists, leading to deforestation and degradation of Georgia’s natural forests. The most significant driver is the degradation of Georgia’s forest reserves. Up to 90% of rural households (i.e., 37% of total population) or up to 1.4 million people in Georgia depend on wood for cooking. This is a major concern because wood is not only used as fuel but also as cooking for. The problem is exacerbated by the fact that households use obsolete technologies, such as traditional stoves and open fires, which are inefficient and produce large amounts of smoke. In addition to this, the unsustainable harvesting levels considering reduced productivity of many forests in the country because of extensive logging and firewood collection has led to a decline in forest cover. The capacity of Georgia’s forests to provide goods and services has declined, most notably the carbon capturing potential.

## Without action, the degradation of Georgian forests will continue to prevail under business as usual practices.

### Box 1: Sustainable Forest Management definition in the Georgian context

The principle of sustainable forest management is that forests are managed in a manner which is consistent with the system established in accordance with the principles of sustainable development, which will provide for the long-term maintenance and enhancement of the productive capacity of the forest. The biodiversity, national use of forest’s ecological potential taking into consideration its ecological value, public interest, and social benefits, as well as the economic potential of forests are all taken into account. The new draft Forest Code calls for the “management and use of forests and forest lands in such a way that a high level of environmental protection is maintained while ensuring sustainable development.” At present and in the future relevant ecological, economic and social functions will be fulfilled on soil resources and water resources. The management of Georgia’s forests is guided by the National Forest Strategy and the National Action Plan for Sustainable Forest Management and Ecosystem Services (NAP-SFME). The NAP-SFME details the national forest strategy and ecosystem services and provides a framework for forest management in Georgia. It also sets out the criteria and indicators to describe the ecological dimension. This dimension is further specified in draft Management Criteria and Indicators.

There is a need to promote the transition to sustainable forest management nationally**, while simultaneously promoting the adoption of energy efficient technologies and alternative fuels to reduce the rural population’s reliance on wood for cooking. The proposed project will contribute towards this by providing training, strengthening the sink potential of Georgia’s forests to regulate net GHG emissions. It further generates numerous co-benefits.

**Georgia does not have an official definition of forest degradation. Regulation No 341 (Regulation of Forest Protection, Rehabilitation and Maintenance), a degraded stand is defined as “where the main features have started to decline,” deteriorate,” damage” (page 2). The new Forest Code defines forest degradation as “the loss or reduction in the capacity of forests to provide goods and services.”

For the purpose of the draft NAP-SFME Georgian forest degradation has assessed and was presented in three categories: low, medium and high. For each category, a description is provided along with the accompanying tree species, damages of trees and surface, and human factors. It should be noted that there are ongoing discussions in Georgia regarding the definition of forest degradation and its assessment criteria. The draft Forest Code defines forest degradation as “the loss or reduction in the capacity of forests to provide goods and services.” This is a broad definition which encompasses all forms of forest degradation. The draft Forest Code further defines areas: Areas covered with forest species, areas covered with mixed forest species, areas covered with open land cover with sparse trees. The language and approach will be integrated into the forest code in the revised version of the draft Forest Code.

The FADN-2015 Carbon Balance Tool was used to assess forest degradation at the national level. This tool is a simple method for assessing overall carbon with spatial data. The language and approach will be integrated into the forest code in the revised version of the draft Forest Code. It should also be noted that there are ongoing discussions regarding the definition of forest degradation and its assessment criteria.

13. The project will focus on three target regions that serve as a model for the implementation of SFM (Sustainable Forest Management) (see Figure 2). All three target regions are included in the upcoming Fourth National Communication of the UNFCCC, with a noted focus on forest vulnerability assessments for these regions. These areas have been identified through an extensive process involving various stakeholders and will be subject to SFM planning and implementation within the frame of this project. The area represents a breakdown of the total target districts (see Figure 2). The selection of the three target regions out of the 108 target districts is a result of thorough process, including ranking of the districts against a set of established criteria. A map showing the location and boundaries of the target regions and districts is provided below.

14. As a result of large-scale forest degradation, carbon stocks in the target region has estimated to decline by 30% (from 587 Mt CO₂ to 409 Mt CO₂) over the reference period and is expected to further decline by 15-4% in 2040 if business as usual practices would continue (baseline scenario). As illustrated in Figure 3, this negative trend in target regions has been observed for several decades. The total amount of timber harvested from these areas greatly exceeded the amount of legally harvested fuelwood (212,042 m³) resulting in the illegal harvesting of an additional 587,968 m³ or around 13% of total wood removed in the three project regions.

### Figure 3: Legal and illegal wood removals in Kakheti, Mitchell-Melikidze and Gura in 2017

In order to address unsustainable forest management practices, the Government of Georgia has initiated an ambitious reform process. The main objectives are (a) to develop a set of sustainable forest management approaches to forest use and management, (b) to develop a unified legal system of forest management and (c) to improve the overall efficiency of forest management in Georgia.

The milestones of this ambitious reform process include:

* Adoption of the National Forest Concept in December 2013, which is aimed at establishing "a system of sustainable forest management that will ensure the improvement of quantitative and qualitative indicators of forests and their ecosystems, as well as the development and enhancement of the economic potential of forests taking into account their ecological values, public participation in forest management activities."
* Launch of the National Forest Program (NFP) process in September 2013 as an instrument to involve all stakeholders in the implementation of the NFP.
* Development of a New Forest Code based on the SFM principles, as stipulated in the National Forest Concept. The new code will replace the existing forest code and will introduce more sustainable forest practices in the forestry sector, such as: introduction of forest information and monitoring system; reform and modernization of the state forest management body; establishment of a new forest service; gradual transformation of the NFA into a multipurpose forest management body. In this role, the NFA will be an important actor in the implementation of the NFP, including the development of forest protection, infrastructure, and other forest management activities; phasing out of all long-term forest concessions; and ensuring that all forest resources are managed by forest management companies. Activities in state forests will exclusively be implemented by forest management bodies.
* GIZ has been active in the Georgian forest sector since 2013 on the regional (South Caucasus) project "Improving Forest Management in Georgia" (IFM).

### References

[1] Georgia is a social-ecological system that was heavily affected by the 2008 crisis to address political and economic crises, ultimately affecting the rural population with an affordable supply of fuelwood. Households register for timber users at the municipal level; and purchase fuelwood from forestland allocated by the MFA regional office. Allocation of forest land for social fueling is done through a withdrawal of the optimal volume of wood from the total standing forest area, which is calculated based on the average figure from 100-200 annual study trees.

[2] The current system of timber harvesting and management in Georgia has several negative impacts on the environment as the MFA and forest management bodies abuse forest resources for the social fueling activities without having a clear understanding of the SFM principles. This is further exacerbated by the lack of an effective legal framework, as described in Chapter 5.2 of the Feasibility Study: consumption of biomass at 6-8 times higher than legal allocation – resulting in a significant loss of forest resources and environmental degradation.

[3] The current system of timber harvesting and management in Georgia is characterized by several negative impacts on the environment as the MFA and forest management bodies abuse forest resources for social fueling activities without having a clear understanding of the SFM principles. This is further exacerbated by the lack of an effective legal framework, as described in Chapter 5.2 of the Feasibility Study: consumption of biomass at 6-8 times higher than legal allocation – resulting in a significant loss of forest resources and environmental degradation.

## B.2.3. Theory of Change

The project is feasible if the Government of Georgia, in a socially compatible manner, can implement an innovative and transformative forest sector reform agenda to join the entire nation’s forests under the SFM framework. As a result, it will be possible for Georgia to meet its 2030 NDC target (218,947 ha) that covers 275/87 hectares of NFA-managed forests with SFM. The transformation can be achieved by the end of the project following qualitative and quantitative changes in the sector.

First of all, a new approach to forest management based on SFM principles and integration of climate change adaptation in forest sector governing framework will lead to improvement of quantitative and qualitative characteristics of Georgia’s forests. This will result in reduction of carbon emissions from deforestation and degradation in the range of 0.8 tonnes per ha and year (1.3 CO2e/ha/year) and increase in forest biomass by 1.7 tonnes per ha and year (2.9 tC/ha/year), as illustrated in Figure 4 below.

## Figure 4 - Carbon stocks in the forests

Second, enhanced forest supervision and introduction of new fuelwood provision mechanism by NGOs will allow elimination of illegal logging estimated at 618,365 m³ (80% of the residential fuelwood demand) while at the same time lead to five-fold increase in the availability of the sustainably harvested biomass in the 8 target catchments. This will result in reduction of carbon emissions from deforestation and degradation by 24 tonnes per ha and year (39 tCO2e/ha/year).

*Please refer to Chapter 5.4 of the Feasibility Study for further details about past and on-going projects.

## To sustain results in target regions, as well as to enable nation-wide SFM mill-out, the project will introduce a number of critical policy changes by supporting implementation of the new Forest Code and EE-AF policy framework. These include: (i) strengthening forest governance; (ii) improving institutional capacity for operationalization of cross-sector coordination mechanism; it will also gradually phase-out inefficient stoves from the market and promote energy efficient technologies, including improved cookstoves, as well as environmental performance standards for domestic heating appliances, as well as energy efficient public procurement.

## To support a socially just implementation of the forest sector reform in the target regions, the project at strengthens municipal authorities and citizens technical and human capacities to participate in sustainable forest management. This will be achieved through capacity building workshops, training sessions, and education opportunities for local communities to facilitate employment and income generation. These alternative livelihoods include: (i) agroforestry; (ii) non-timber forest products; (iii) value addition of timber and management of forest products and reduce potential conflicts between state institutions (NFA DES and NAFOR) and local communities.

## The project will follow a two-pronged approach to address underlying root cause of forest degradation, i.e. inadequate forest management and high demand for fuelwood (see Theory of Change - Figure 7); with Component I focusing on strengthening forest governance and promoting sustainable forest management; and Component II focusing on promoting development of EE-AF market, specifically for rural areas. A socially just transition to the new forest management model will be achieved through capacity building workshops and training sessions.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 12 OF 92

## B

### Key Results Achieved with Results 1-3 and 5 Focusing on Target Regions and Result 4 on a Country-Wide Scale

The following five key results will be achieved, with Results 1-3 and 5 focusing on target regions and Result 4 on a country-wide scale:

* **Result 1:** Reduced forest degradation and enhanced carbon stock
* **Result 2:** Reduced deforestation and increased forest cover in the target regions established
* **Result 3:** EE-AF market reached critical share and self-sustaining level of growth
* **Result 4:** Policy and regulatory framework for SFM and EE-AF market created enabling environment to continue scaling up and achieving results
* **Result 5:** Diversified livelihood opportunities and strengthened local self-governance in forest management

### The Underlying Driver of the Degradation by Felling Fuelwood

The underlying driver of the degradation by felling fuelwood was more efficient and by improving the management of the underutilized forest resource, there is potential for a paradigm shift so that ecological and economic benefits can be achieved. This will also reduce GHG emissions from deforestation and land use change; the impact: the amount of CO2 remaining sequestered in standing forest will increase, thereby reducing GHG emissions.

### Project Financing Framework: Georgia Forest and Rural Energy Investment Facility (GFREF)

As an overarching financing framework for GFIP project implementation, the Georgia Forest and Rural Energy Investment Facility (GFREF) will be formed to coordinate and scale-up public and private investment in low-carbon energy projects. The GFREF will provide a flexible funding mechanism for GFIP projects. If there is no well-proven two-channel approach, the Facility will consist of two funding windows:

* **Window 1:** Public sector
* **Window 2:** Private sector

Under the GFREF Public Investment Window, the Facility will establish a public-private financial mechanism to be the vehicle for scaling up and financing projects. The Facility will create a viable business model for sustainable biomass supply to consumers and a resolving financing scheme to enable accumulation of NFA.

### Theory of Change

| **Scenario** | **Current State** | **Desired Future State** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Scenario 1: Enhanced forest management and reduced impact of logging | Current state: Logging practices are not sustainable, leading to deforestation and degradation. | Desired future state: Enhanced forest management practices reduce the negative impacts of logging on forests, resulting in improved forest health and biodiversity conservation. |

### Project Financial Framework

The GFREF will provide a flexible funding mechanism for GFIP projects by establishing two windows:

* **Window 1:** Public sector
* **Window 2:** Private sector

## Sustainable Forest Management (SFM)

### Component 1: Sustainable Forest Management (SFM)
Carries a dual objective and works at two levels:
- First, it supports NFA to implement SFM in line with NDC targets in three target regions.
- While in parallel, it works with NFA to build the capacity of the forest sector and establish sustainable financing mechanisms for SFM nationwide.

Implementation of Component 1 will:

- Support NFA in implementing SFM in line with NDC targets in three target regions while building forest sector capacities and establishing a sustainable financing mechanism.
- Work with NFA to implement SFM nationwide, focusing on capacity-building and financial sustainability.


## Georgia Forest and Rural Energy Investment Facility (SFIF)

### Overview
The facility aims to:
1. Support the implementation of SFM by providing grants for the construction of Business Service Yards for processing and sorting timber products.
2. Provide financial support to NFA for forest sector operations, ensuring financial independence from state budget constraints.

### Financial Structure
- **Grants:** Provided through a performance-based GCF Investment Grant.
- **Interest Rate Subsidies:** Guaranteed by the Government of Georgia.
- **Guarantees:** Provided alongside market loans by the Georgian Commercial Bank (GCB) and other banks.
- **SIDA Co-financing:** Available for projects that meet EU Eco-design standards.

### Implementation
The facility will:
1. Provide grants to NFA for construction of Business Service Yards.
2. Offer interest rate subsidies and guarantees on market loans from GCB and other banks.
3. Enable co-financing with SIDA for projects meeting EU Eco-design standards.


## Figure 6: Georgia Forest and Rural Energy Investment Facility (SFIF)

The figure illustrates the structure and components of the facility, including:
- Grants provided to NFA for Business Service Yards construction.
- Interest rate subsidies guaranteed by the Government of Georgia.
- Co-financing with SIDA available for projects meeting EU Eco-design standards.

## Limited capacities, practical skills and experience of forest sector institutions:

- Capacity building for forest sector institutions is a priority in the GCFIF.
- Substantial efforts are needed to train NFA workforce (existing and new hires) on SFM, where 90 full-time forest work positions will be created. This includes training 12 forest officers, 3 forest managers, and 4 forest specialists for implementation of SFM measures.
- Private sector service companies in the forest sector, supposed to implement SFM measures, are also expected to be generated for private sector forest work specialists.

## Capacity building for forest sector institutions:

- Under the auspices of the Department of Environmental Supervision (DES), which is responsible for controlling the transportation and trade of timber, a new Forest Service Division has been established. This division will report directly to DES as well but is not yet implemented.
- The new mandate requires the hiring of 270 staff for the new forest service division, including 150 forest officers, 30 forest managers, 40 forest specialists, and 90 modern equipment as well as a new know-how on standard operating procedures and forest patrolling.

## Mechanism for the sustainable provision of fuelwood and timber through NFA-operated business service yards (BYS):

- The project proposes to establish a new Forest Service Division within DES. This division will have staff with adequate know-how are missing.
- Construction of the first BYS only began in 2019, with the second one planned for 2020 and third one for 2021. However, no BYS has been constructed; in the project region alone, at least 15 yards are needed, which require substantial investment in construction.

## Inadequate financing for the forest sector:

- Since gaining independence from the Soviet Union, Georgia's forest sector has suffered from declining budgets and salaries for forest management, severely undermining its capacity to implement SFM.
- The lack of adequate funding for forest management has led to a decline in the number of under-investor in the sector have greatly limited the adoption of SFM. While official statistics in Georgia show that 10% of the country's forests are managed according to SFM (compared to 50–90% in Central and Eastern European countries), and sliding road infrastructure limits (which require a minimum width of 2m) are not widely used.
- In the three project regions, only 12.1 km of roads were constructed between 2013 and 2017 compared to 584.9 km in 2013 for example; only 3.4 ha were reforested and 15.2ha were supported in natural regeneration.
- The three project regions have a total of 6,000 ha of forest land that require substantial investment needs for forest infrastructure and implementation of concrete SFM measures.

## Policy and regulatory gaps:

The state's regulatory framework for Georgian forestry is inadequate for the application of modern forest management practices. There are no specific legal frameworks or guidelines for SFM in Georgia.
- The second major gap is the lack of capacity to have developed to align with SFM principles, including the lack of a comprehensive national forest inventory and monitoring system that can impact forest infrastructure (e.g., former access roads and sliding tracks), the lack of a comprehensive prescriptive code for forest management, and the lack of operating guidelines to support the identification and transformation of forests that do not represent natural forest types to native forests, among others.

36\. In addition, the regulatory system of harvest provision currently in use is flawed and contributes to improper forest management. Allocation of forest areas for social fencing is done without thorough analysis of the optimum volume of yearly extractable timber at sustainable level. Thus, annual allowable cuts are not based on the actual forest inventory but rather on a pre-determined amount that is allocated to each community or group of "intermediaries" who are unregistered yet purchase social timber from local people. Thus, their identification, monitoring and control over these intermediaries is difficult if not impossible. Furthermore, this system is informal; it is extremely difficult to determine exact harvested forested volumes, and ultimately to differentiate between harvests that are legally registered and those that are not. The lack of accurate data on the volume harvested does not reflect the true economic and environmental value of the natural resources resulting in major losses to the sector. Furthermore, the system has been exploited by unscrupulous intermediaries who have sold timber illegally to state forests with state taxes of at least GE 30 million (EUR 10 million) per year due to the program. Furthermore, some forest areas are allocated for social fencing but are not used and thus remain unused. These areas are often carried off by unqualified persons and often lead to environmental degradation, and even to accidents and injuries in the communities.

Incomplete, unreliable and outdated data on forest inventories: One challenge for forest management in Georgia is the lack of up-to-date forest inventories. Forest Inventories in Georgia are outdated, with many areas having not been inventoried since 1950s or earlier (see Figure 2). The last inventory was conducted by George. Specifically, in many of the target districts, the date of the last inventory is within the range of 1951 to 1986. Furthermore, forest inventories are not updated regularly and thus do not reflect current conditions. They have been managed by short-term contracts with limited expertise and in the framework of the social wood program, which has led to poor management of forest land conditions. Such practices have resulted in poor forest management, particularly unsustainable harvesting levels that are not aligned with forest conditions and growth rates.

38\. The lack of a comprehensive forest information and monitoring system limits sustainable planning, management and decision-making at all levels. This is due to the fact that there is no single source for land registration, forest inventories, forest site and forest function mapping, utilization rights, forest operations (including logging), timber harvesting permits, etc., which are essential components of a comprehensive forest management. The lack of such a system presents transparent oversight and traceability in the sector enabling regular monitoring of forest resources and their use. This is particularly important for the implementation on the LULUCF sector, including the robustness and accuracy of emissions reports, and NDC monitoring.

Component 2: Market development for energy efficiency and alternative fuels

39\. Component 2 "Market development for energy efficiency and alternative fuels" will tackle the main driver of deforestation in Georgia, which is the demand for firewood. The focus will be on promoting energy efficient (EE) technologies and alternative low-carbon fuels (AEFs). The primary focus will be on the production and supply chain of EE-APs and AEFs to ensure that they are available at competitive prices compared with EE-AF products. At the same time, to ensure sustainability and scaling-up EE-AF market, the project will be at the forefront in developing a national energy efficiency and alternative fuels market development strategy for suppliers, financial sector and the Government (see Figure 10 in the next section for illustration of the national and regional markets). The market development strategy will be provided via the GEF/PFP Private Investment Window as described earlier and illustrated in the text.

40\. Component 2 is in design to address demand-side, supply-side and policy barriers to EE-AF market expansion.

41\. Underdeveloped supply chain for EE technologies and alternative fuels: So far, there are only 15 producers of efficient lights and 3 producers of EE on the market, with limited annual production volumes (500 lamps and 200 units respectively). The main barriers to scaling up EE products in Georgia include lack of building up new or expansion of existing production and distribution capacities, as well as supply of raw

B

### Affordability of EE-AFs and access to finance, especially for poor households: High up-front costs of energy efficiency appliances (EE-AFs) are a major barrier to adoption, particularly in rural areas where they are further exacerbated by low levels of income in rural Georgia and households' restricted access to bank financing.

#### Prices for EE technologies vary significantly, but even the cheapest alternative is hardly affordable for an average rural household. Depending on the source and quality, the price of an EE stove ranges between GEI $100-$350. The cost of a solar water heater varies from GEI $200 to $800.

#### Only 10% of rural households have more than GEI $60 per month and another 68% of households have monthly earnings below GEI $40. In addition, the average rural household has a debt-to-income ratio of over 35%. If their disposal income is not sufficient to cover even the cost of a basic EE stove, let alone invest in more expensive and efficient appliances, then access to finance becomes an issue.

### The situation is being exacerbated by households' restricted access to bank financing. In the last few years household debt in Georgia has been growing rapidly and the value of overdue loans is high. The National Bank of Georgia (NBG) has recently introduced a new credit card product aimed at providing rural households with the ability to provide individual unsecured consumer loans without detailed analysis of customer's solvency. In particular, NBG offers a credit card with an interest rate of 20% and a limit of GEI $150. The maximum amount of the potential borrower, so that the borrower should not take the amount more than five times as financial capabilities at least for three months. At the same time, the NBG has introduced a new credit card product aimed at providing individual unsecured consumer loans without detailed analysis of customer's solvency.

### Low consumer awareness about technologies and fuel alternatives and reduction of fuel wood consumption, especially in rural areas, are major barriers to adoption. The majority of rural households in Georgia use firewood as their primary cooking fuel. Only 20% of the total population are aware about energy efficiency while the rest of rural residents are not even aware that there is such a concept. Rural residents are also concerned about availability and potential disruption and instability of AE supply to rural areas.

### Absence of enabling policy and regulatory framework: Slow progress and insufficient capacities within the government institutions responsible for promoting EE-AFs in Georgia have been hampering the adoption of energy efficiency and alternative fuels, such as regulations which establish minimum allowed level of energy efficiency for appliances. The lack of a clear policy on EE labeling has also hindered the development of capacities for introduction of EE labeling for heat stoves; support and promotion scheme for renewable heat supply technologies are not in place.

### Component 2: Livelihood opportunities and local self-governance in forest management

#### The focus of Component 2 will be on municipal institutions, police and the population in the three target regions. These communities will be among the first to be affected by the implementation of the new forest codes. They are also likely to be the most resistant to change because they have a long history of opaque and non-transparent and active participation of local communities in forest management and the diversification of livelihoods.

#### Absence of strategies, tools, instruments and capacities for municipal authorities to participate in sustainable forest management and conservation: The new Forest Code (article 20 "Competence of a Municipality") provides that municipalities have the right to manage forests on their territory. However, there is no strategy or plan for the involvement of local communities in forest management by municipalities. However, this concept is at a very recent stage in Georgia. For the time being, the main focus should be on building capacities and providing support to municipal authorities for technical planning, awareness and human skills and capacities to actively engage in sustainable forest management as stipulated in the new Forest Code.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 17 OF 92

#### B

**4.1 Improved participation and representation of local stakeholders in forest management related decision-making**
To date, participation in e.g., FMP development is implemented on a very basic level without providing sufficient opportunities for the communities to influence the process or the subsequent management plan implementation. The Forest Sector Reform foresees intense stakeholder engagement, with the aim of ensuring that all relevant actors are involved and their interests represented. To materialize this approach it requires an awareness or capacities in local communities available to participate in potential decision-making.

**4.2 Lack of qualified personnel and insufficient education opportunities in the forest sector**
The implementation of the Forest Sector Reform will require in future a large number of new forest workers to be employed, trained and equipped with the necessary skills for their tasks. The Forest Sector Reform will further require organizational and business skills related to production, processing and marketing of forest products. To meet these needs, the Forest Sector Reform foresees that the Forest Academy will expand their already existing program. Moreover, forest-related education programs at universities need improvement in order to provide graduates with the necessary knowledge and skills for employment in the sector. International partnerships with qualified centers of knowledge would be a further option.

**4.3 Under-utilization of economic opportunities in forest-related value chains**
Forest-related value chains such as timber value chain, Non-Timber Forest Products (NTFP) value chains and eco-tourism opportunities offer significant potential for employment generation and income diversification. However, the extent to which these value chains is better than the potential. Reasons include lack of data, sector analysis for the identification of economic opportunities, lack of business plans and strategies, missing human technical and economic capacities and capital.

**5.2 The project Components 1,2 and 3 are inter-dependent and complementary and reinforce the outcomes of each other**
The introduction of a new forest code, which is aimed at reducing unsustainable forest management, is made possible by providing affordable and more efficient alternatives to cutting down trees. This will reduce the demand for timber and thus help to reduce the pressure on forests. On the other side, introduction and strict enforcement of FMP practices along with new business models in the forest sector will provide a strong basis for the development of the EAF EE market because the abundant supply of legally sourced fuel will gradually phase out. Component 3 ensures that the benefits from the improved forest management system is socially just and provides employment and income opportunities for forest adjoining communities.

# Component I: Sustainable Forest Management (SFM)

## Activity 1.1 Development and implementation of SFM plans

### Sub-activity 1.1.1 Development of SFM plans

The project will support the development of FMPs by technical assistance in four districts: Kavandi, Dzopemotso, Tsalie and Tseretse. The development of the plans will include a participatory process involving stakeholders from NPA, local communities, private sector, and other relevant actors to ensure that the needs and interests of all are addressed properly while promoting improved cooperation between NPA and local communities.

The FMPs will be developed in accordance with the Forest Resources Act (2015) and its implementing regulations, as well as the National Forestry Policy (2014), which sets out the principles for forest inventory, restoration, management and utilization (currently Regulations 179 to 236 of Government Gazette). It is envisaged that the FMPs will be developed in line with the NPA’s Strategic Plan 2015-2020.

For more information on how the project addresses key barriers, please refer to Chapter 2.6 of the Feasibility Study.

## Investment support scheme for private sector contractors (financed by Government of Georgia through ARDA and ARDA partner MFIs and banks): The implementation of 70% of the necessary forest measures will be financed by the GCF through ARDA. This includes the provision of financial incentives to private sector contractors, including a SFM specific element to comply with the SFI approach. To incentivize necessary investments ARDA will provide financial support in the form of loans and/or guarantees for the implementation of the activities (see Activity 2.1 and Section B.5 for more details). These incentives will be coupled with both technical assistance and capacity building provided by GIZ to ensure that private sector contractors are able to meet the requirements of ARDA. The financial support scheme is designed in close cooperation with ARDA, which will provide a list of eligible forest service contractors, all forest service contractors, which comply with the eligibility criteria set out in the existing ARDA instruments and are willing to implement the activities as per the GCF project document. In addition, GIZ will provide technical equipment for forest management activities, except trucks; GIZ will require ARDA to implement this Sub-activity under the financial support investment term.

### Activity 1.2 Strengthening of forest supervision

The activity supports the Department of Environmental Supervision (DES) to reduce the illegal use of forested areas, including logging for fuelwood and industrial timber and to assess the forest management practice of forest service contractors. The DES will be supported in implementing a new national policy on forest supervision, a new mandate by developing standard operating procedures (SOPs) and training their staff. In the target regions, DES will also support the development of local forest supervision units, including the use of mobile logging trucks, satellite cameras, etc.). This improves the efficiency and effectiveness of forest supervision and law enforcement.

#### Sub-activity 1.2.1 Strengthening of procedures, standards and protocols for enhanced forest supervision: TA will be provided on the national level to develop new procedures, standards, and protocols for the management of forests in Georgia. The DES will also be supported by GIZ in developing standard operating procedures of the existing environmental patrolling division. In addition, the TA will support the development of mobile logging trucks, satellite cameras, etc.) to improve forest supervision and law enforcement.

#### Sub-activity 1.2.2 Implementation of improved forest supervision measures and technologies: This sub-activity includes a combination of TA and investment support to DES. Technical assistance (project management) is provided by GIZ to the DES in order to develop new procedures, standards, and protocols for forest practices, including the collection and analysis of improved information and provision of training. GCF-financed investments will be used to provide mobile logging trucks, satellite cameras, etc.) to improve forest supervision and law enforcement.

### Activity 1.3 Provision of sustainably produced fuelwood by NFA

This activity supports NFA to establish a model sustainable fuelwood supply chain in the above mentioned target regions. The project will support NFA in developing a business plan for the procurement and construction, in year 1, 3 and 5 of project implementation to the provision of advanced, low-impact SFM machinery and to formalize the business plan with MFIs and banks.

#### Sub-activity 1.3.1 Establishment of a model sustainable fuelwood supply chain: TA will be provided by GIZ to NFA in order to develop a business plan for the procurement and construction, in year 1, 3 and 5 of project implementation to the provision of advanced, low-impact SFM machinery and to formalize the business plan with MFIs and banks.

#### Sub-activity 1.3.2 Development of Business Service Years (BSYs): The development and operation of Business Service Years (BSYs) by construction of the yards, purchase or lease of equipment, etc.) will be supported through GCF-financed investments. In year 5 of project implementation to facilitate the transition from the social wood programme to a new system of NFA-run BSYs for fuelwood production and supply in the target regions. The BSYs are based on eco-system-based forest management. BSYs will be owned and operated by NFA.

Sub-activity 1.3.1 Support establishment of the new mechanism for fuelwood provision to local population: Technical assistance (financed by GCF) will be provided to establish the new mechanism for fuelwood provision to local populations. This includes development and implementation of a set of SOPs, training modules™ and operational regulations, and technical guidelines for the newly established BSYS. To ensure that the BSYS is sustainable in the long term, it will be necessary to address the following: (i) different qualities of timber to optimize the sale of higher-quality timber for higher prices and ensuring that fuelwood provision is not compromised; (ii) the need to develop a market for high-quality timber; (iii) the need for fuelwood marketing and cross-cutting themes such as gender, occupational health and safety. This Sub-activity also includes the development of a monitoring system for the BSYS, including regular reporting on effective controlling, and continuous improvement services to strengthen the operation and monitoring of BSYS, ensuring that it is sustainable in the long term.

Co-investments by GCF and government™ will be made available to construct 14 new BSYS facilities, and refurbish 7 existing ones (see Annex 2). The construction of the first BSYS is expected to start in 2019 in Akhmeta district, supported by GIZ as a model case).

Activity 1.4 Enhancement of enabling environment for the nation-wide implementation of sustainable forest management

This activity strengthens the enabling environment to facilitate the nation-wide adoption of SFM. The project provides targeted support for adjusting the regulatory framework, improving forest sector training and vocational education, and strengthening institutional capacity at all levels. This will be achieved through a high-level inter-ministerial working group. Finally, climate risks and suitable adaptation strategies in forest ecosystems are being identified to ensure that the results of this activity are sustainable.

Sub-activity 1.4.1 Strengthening of the legal framework for SFM: The majority of revisions of the key regulatory instruments (Forest Code, Forest Act, Forestry Law, National Forest Policy, National Forest Monitoring), 241 (Regulation of Forest Protection, Reforestation and Management) and 242 (Resolution of the Government on the use of non-timber forest products) are already in place. However, there is still a need for ongoing GIZ-funded BfS project. However, it is likely that some additional revisions may be required: BfS co-financing will cover the costs of revising the Forest Code and the National Forest Policy. The revised instruments will be harmonized with efforts prior to project start. In addition to revising three of the main secondary legal acts for forest management and sustainable use, this sub-activity also includes the elaboration of a regulation on the commercial use of non-timber forest products, including: types of non-timber forest products that can be harvested; conditions under which they are allowed to be harvested to ensure sustainable management based on the principles of SFM. fees for commercial harvesting; penalties and sanctions in case of illegal harvesting.

Sub-activity 1.4.2 Improvement of sector steering and coordination between involved sectors: This activity makes use of the NFP process, which facilitates improved cross-sectoral cooperation and coordination as well as the development of a national forest strategy (NFS). The implementation of the revised Forest Code and MidPFA will coordinate the NFS process with technical assistance from GIZ. BfS co-financing will cover the costs for the elaboration of the NFS. This sub-activity also includes the establishment of a multi-stakeholder sector coordination committee, consisting of representatives of the forestry, rural development and energy sectors. The committee will be responsible for the implementation of the planning financing platform, the GFREP (see Activity 1.4.2 in Feasibility Study, page 264).

Sub-activity 1.4.3 Development of online knowledge management and training platform for the forest sector: The project supports the development of an online knowledge management and training platform (KMTP) for the forest sector (Target group: MEFPA, NFPs and other public forest management bodies, DEs and forest private companies). The KMTP will provide a comprehensive set of tools to support the collection, storage, analysis and dissemination of information. The KMTP will be integrated into a knowledge management and training platform (KMTP). The system will include e-learning components, such as online courses, webinars, and other interactive learning materials, as well as a database for storing and retrieving information. The KMTP will ensure that people engaged in forestry sector have adequate knowledge and skills to effectively manage forests and contribute to the sustainable development of forest resources.

Sub-activity 1.4.4 Improvement of vocational education and training for the forest sector: The new (draft) forest code requires all foresters and forest workers, including existing staff and future staff to obtain a formal qualification in forestry management. This sub-activity will support the implementation of the new forest code in Georgia for both the medium-and long-term. GZ, through its IBS and ECODeve projects, supports the Georgian Forest Service (GFS) in developing a national strategy for vocational education and training in the sector. The strategy will include the development of accredited courses and training, and training of trainers, among other activities. BME co-funds the implementation of this sub-activity through its support to GZ in implementing the new forest code in Georgia.

Sub-activity 1.4.5 Enabling improved integration of climate change adaptation into forest sector planning: The project supports the development of a Forest Information and Monitoring System (FIMS), aligned with national requirements by elaborating and operationalizing 10 FIMS modules and developing national emission factors. These will be utilized by 7 government institutions to sustainably manage and monitor Georgia’s forests, including the monitoring of forest carbon stocks and other forest dynamics, which ultimately improves sector planning and forest management.

## B

### Component 2: Market development for energy efficiency (EE) and alternative fuels (AF)

#### 54. Deployment of energy efficient (EE) technologies and alternative fuel (AF) solutions can substantially reduce greenhouse gas emissions in Georgia.

- **Activity 13**: Implementing market-based economic instruments for EE/AF solutions
- **Activity X3**: Creating consumer awareness and providing technical advice on EE/AF products and regulations

#### 55. A range of more energy efficient heat-cleaning technologies is available in Georgia: opportunities vary between relatively simple and affordable locally-produced energy efficient wood stoves or imported analogous product with a higher price and quality to more sophisticated and much pricier boiler-powered central heating systems.

*Please refer to Chapter 5.2.3 of the Feasibility Study for detailed description.*


---
**Page 29**
---

## Energy Efficiency Technologies

The most affordable options are locally manufactured, but non-certified EE stoves which are produced in small workshops in small towns and villages throughout Georgia. Improves indoor air quality by reducing smoke emissions from traditional wood-burning stoves into enclosed chambers. These stoves are made one-by-one by hand without patterns or modern manufacturing techniques. They use less fuel than traditional stoves, but their efficiency is lower (75% of the energy content of the wood used is converted to heat). The price for such EE stoves varies from 600 GEL to 1,500 GEL.

These products are characterized by much higher combustion efficiency and low level of emissions, most or more than 90% of the energy content of the fuel used is converted into heat. The price (between GEL 800 to 1,500), demand for such products is mainly concentrated in more affluent urban areas. See also Figure 13 below for illustration of a difference between locally manufactured and imported EE stoves.

| Made in Akhmeta, Georgia | Efficiency: 75%; Price: GEL 800 |
| --- | --- |
| Made in Bulgaria, EU | Efficiency: 75%; Price: GEL 1,400 |

### Figure 13 EE stove comparison

## Among all alternative heating systems, the most efficient one is the boiler-based central heating systems. There are several companies specializing in design and installation of such heating systems for individual households. The main alternatives to boilers are electric baseboard heaters, gas-fired or biomass technologies (electricity, gas-based and fuelwood-based boilers). The major deterrent to widespread adoption of the EE stoves and other EE technologies is the high initial cost of these technologies as well as the lack of such technology installed. Thermal insulation of rural houses has high theoretical potential to reduce heat loss from buildings and thus energy consumption in heating. This can be achieved by installing thermal insulation within the dwelling with additional insulation by professional workforce can bring up to 60% energy saving. This package is estimated at GEL 1,500 per household. The cost of such installation is justified by a significant annual income of an average rural household. The very basic insulation package for 40 m² of living space could result in savings of approximately 30% on heating costs. EE stoves and boilers are the most expensive option but they also have the highest energy saving potential. Figure 14 illustrates the price difference and saving potential of the various EE alternatives. Among available alternatives, an EE stove is considered as the most optimal solution because it has relatively large fuelwood saving potentials at the lowest price.

## Section 9: Technical Assistance and USB currently available on the Georgian market are not subject to any mandatory standards, are often of low quality/character, and their energy/environmental/health performance characteristics cannot be verified. This leads to substandard, inefficient products being over-represented in the market. There are also quality control issues with imported products. The lack of a national standard for USBs means that many products on the market are not available in Georgia. To address this barrier TASF will support certification of locally produced solid fuel-burning stoves and USBs to the requirements of the European Union Eco-design Directive (2015/863/EU) and the Ecodesign Directive (See description of Activity 2.4 for further details about EU Eco-design Directive and the process to obtain a CE mark). This will be achieved through:

- Raising suppliers' awareness about applicable standards and requirements
- Conducting audits of suppliers' production facilities by accredited national laboratories, including confirmation of the conformity of products and/or production processes with the requirements of the applicable standard
- Only certified products/brands eligible for participation in the GCF-supported financial incentive scheme to be implemented under Activity 2.2.

### Service line 3c: TASF will support development of business plans for EAF-suppliers to support their loan applications to ARDA Bankers for investment in new production capacity and working capital. This service includes the following instruments:

- **Raising awareness**: Credit Project Co-finance interest rates on commercial loans from ARDA Partner Banks for the investment in new SME production assets (up to 11% over 60 months) and working capital (up to 8% over 36 months). The former is an important instrument for enabling start-up investment needs, while the latter is an important instrument for existing producers, e.g. to ensure availability of sufficient liquidity to meet production requirements.
- **Processing Enterprises and Storage Facilities Project**: co-finance up to 40% of the production costs (up to 15% over 60 months) and working capital needs (up to 8% over 36 months). The Project has supported establishment of 145 new SMEs and re-equipped over 900 SMEs and facilitated loan financing for 2,700 SMEs.
- **Scoping up investment opportunities**: Scoping up pipeline and potential portfolio of ARDA to identify opportunities for strengthening environmental development cooperation in Georgia.

### Service line 3d: TASF will support the following activities:

- Scoping up current and pipeline portfolio of ARDA to identify opportunities for strengthening environmental development cooperation in Georgia.
- Scoping of possible new investment directions considering environmental aspects as outlined above, e.g. private sector involvement in waste management, dealing with hazardous waste in agriculture
- Development of a capacity building plan for the Ministry of Agriculture and other state and non-state actors in rural development
- Capacity Development for ARDA—Department for Technical Assistance—to systematically embed environmental considerations into its work.

### Service line 3e: EAF-suppliers marketing. Under this service line, TASF will provide marketing support for EAF-suppliers to help promote their products to potential customers. Specific support activities will include:

- Training of EAF-suppliers on market research and customer identification
- Support with identification of potential corporate and public sector customers through market research and an introduction to ARDA’s existing portfolio of clients

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 38 OF 92

## Sub-activity 2.1.2 Feasibility assessment and pipeline development for new EE-AF solutions

The objective of this activity is to jump-start the market and significantly scale-up demand for EE-AF products in rural Georgia, with a focus on specific product categories and specific financing packages tailored to the need of rural households (in terms of specific energy needs, price, ease of implementation and operation, etc.). The scope will be limited to the following products: improved stoves, biomass briquettes. This may include, for instance, design and installation of energy efficient central heating systems in homes with a focus on low-income families. It is also expected that the project will support the development of (SSTs) systems, biogas, production of pellets and utilization of wide range of agricultural residues (e.g. straw or other by-products). The scope may include the promotion of energy efficient appliances which are commercially available in Georgia.

The BiMFIN financed sub-activity will therefore support identification and promotion of new EE-AF products and technologies that can be scaled up through a combination of market development, pilot projects and policy advocacy. The project will also collaborate closely with the Georgian Partnership Fund (GPF) to identify and promote energy efficient appliances and other EE-AF products that are commercially available in Georgia.

The scope is very nascent or supply and demand side and does not represent a viable large-scale investment case for GCF. However, it is expected that the project will be able to demonstrate the potential of EE-AF products and technologies. The specific type of product and technology that will be identified, the scope and modality of GCF’s investment in the sector will be more precisely defined.

### Activity 2.2 Implementing consumer financing instruments for EE-AF solutions

#### Objective
The objective of this activity is to jump-start the market and significantly scale-up demand for EE-AF products in rural Georgia, with a focus on specific product categories and specific financing packages tailored to the need of rural households (in terms of specific energy needs, price, ease of implementation and operation, etc.). It is also meant to provide meaningful and practical alternative to the population of the regions, which will be affected by the forestry sector reform. The scope will be limited to the following products: improved stoves, biomass briquettes.

#### Sub-activity 2.2.1
This activity will consist of two sub-activities: Sub-activities 2.2.1 and 2.2.2 focusing on the most promising EE-AF products (improved stoves and biomass briquettes) as well as other emerging solutions and products (to be identified and supported in parallel under sub-activity 2.1.2).

#### Sub-activity 2.2.2
Among available alternatives, an EE stove can be considered as the most optimal solution because it has relatively low cost of ownership, high energy efficiency and is widely accepted by consumers. The market offers attractive IRR of 40% (see Figure 14, Box 2 Annex 3 – Financial and Economic Analysis). However, the current situation in Georgia is characterized by a very low level of awareness about conventional inefficient stove which are now priced at GEL 50-70 and have an lifetime of maximum 2 years.

### Improved Stoves

Locally produced efficient stoves are priced between GEL 300 – 800, but they last only for an estimated 5 years and are not certified. The market is dominated by imported inefficient stoves which are sold to rural households at wood stoves on the market must be certified, eco-friendly-compliant; the prices vary from EUR 170-230. These stoves have a lifetime of maximum 2 years (they are made of metal and are not durable as they can be heated) and the design. Levels of efficiency vary somewhat but all have efficiency over 70% (versus 30-40% for inefficient stoves). The market is dominated by imported inefficient stoves which are sold at wood stoves on the market must be certified, eco-friendly-compliant; the prices vary from EUR 170-230. These stoves have a lifetime of maximum 2 years (they are made of metal and are not durable as they can be heated) and the design.

These are already on the market in Georgia – reported average of GEL 1,479 for retailers / imported stoves according to the survey conducted by the project. The average price of locally produced efficient stoves is GEL 1,050; an energy efficient EU-certified stove has been assumed in the project scenario.

It is expected that the activity will stimulate demand for up to 30,000 stoves cumulatively until project end and additionally about 28,000 households will switch to EE. To do so the project will, in partnership with national financial institutions, introduce a new financing instrument (see Box 4 – Financial and Economic Analysis) based on EE stoves and USBL but gradually expanding to other more sophisticated EE-AF technologies (Sub-activity 2.1.2). The financing instrument is expected to be cost-effective for the project (Sub-activity 2.2.1).

#### Result-based financial incentive instrument (financed by GCF: EUR 3.6 million) in the form of price discount on stoves and USBLs, as well as a subsidy for the first year of operation of the stove.

Conservation finance will be provided locally by banks and micro-finance institutions (EUR 10.5 million) to cover the interest rate differential between the cost of EE stove only or EE stove + annual USBL supply package, depending on the consumer choices.

**Page 34**
---

104. **Sub-activity 2.3.1 Design, implementation and marketing of the voucher programme for EE stoves** (for households): The GCF is used to design and implement a results-based financial incentive scheme that will stimulate the demand for EE stoves among households. Initially, the size of the incentive has been set up at 50% of the cost of EE stove purchase. This incentive will be progressively revised and adjusted over the project implementation in response to the overall market development (e.g., through a combination of monitoring and evaluation data and/or feedback from consumers (via marketing and consumer awareness) the population of three target regions. As the SFM will scale-up and EE stoves become more affordable, the incentive will be progressively reduced until it reaches 0% at the end of the project.

105. ARDA will be directly responsible for administration of the voucher programme and sub-contracts a service provider for implementation of the voucher scheme®. Provision of the financial incentive will be result-based including monitoring and evaluation of EE stove adoption and switch to EE stoves upon them presenting the proof of EE stove to households (paid invoice and the voucher®).

106. **Sub-activity 2.3.2 Providing consumer financing for EE stoves and AF for households (Co-financing by Financial Institutions)**: The project will provide co-financing through a combination of:

a) Consumer loans will be provided by banks and micro-finance institutions® in conjunction with financial incentive for EE stoves to enable cash-constrained households to buy an EE stove and procure annual supply of biomass fuel. This loan will be repaid over 12 months at interest rate of 0% (to consumers).

b) Technical assistance will be provided to MFIs and commercial banks to structure and promote such loan products in their portfolio.

c) In parallel, the project will also provide technical assistance to financial institutions to structure loan products for AF. The loan will be repaid over a period of 12 months at interest rate of 0% (to consumers).

A number of Georgia’s financial institutions which have been consulted (Crystal, TBC Bank and ProCredit Bank) are interested in developing such loan products. In addition, the project is also supporting the development of a consumer financing loan product through Crystal to complement GCF-supported financial incentive – see Info box 3.

**Info box 3: Consumer Loan for EE stoves and AF**

This info box provides details on the information related to the Funding Proposal development stages, CFCI. Georgia’s largest MFI is the most advanced in developing a new loan product specifically for EE stoves but participation in the project will be conditional upon the successful completion of the pilot phase. The project will provide technical assistance and currently prototyping a prototype loan product for AF. This loan will be repaid over 12 months at interest rate of 0% (to consumers) and will be used to finance the purchase of EE stove adoption and switch in U.S. Under this project, Crystal and TBC Bank are interested in developing such loan products. The project is also supporting the development of a consumer financing loan product for AF.

107. **Sub-activity 2.3.2 Supporting MFIs and partner banks to structure and promote consumer financing products for advanced EE/AF solutions**: The project, via GZ EE Fund financed by BMZ will provide technical assistance to MFIs and commercial banks in Georgia to structure and promote consumer financing products for emerging technological solutions and new EE/AF products (to be identified and supported under Activity 2.1.2). These loans will be repaid over a period of 12 months at interest rate of 0% (to consumers) and will be used for building insulation works in residential sector. However, little title information is available or exist regarding the potential demand for such loan products among households. The project will be based on market analysis into residential sector potential for retrotts, training and awareness raising activities to promote EE/AF solutions and development of a pilot phase to test the pipeline of projects in residential sector. Loan products for renewable energy system installation by households, such as solar water heaters (SWH) or heat pump, will also be expected.

*For details on the implementation structure of the voucher programme and responsibilities of the service provider please see Feasibility Study Chapter 5.3.2.1 (Sub-Activity 2.3.1).
* For more details please see Feasibility Study Chapter 5.3.2.1 (Sub-Activity 2.3.1).
* The scheme is open to all banks and microfinance institutions in Georgia and it does not give any kind of exclusivity to Crystal.

# Activity 2.3 Creating consumer awareness and provision of technical advisory services for fuelwood users

108. The objective of this activity is to raise awareness about EE-AF solutions, national forest sector reforms and its implications on fuelwood users in the target regions and other locations across rural Georgia.

The focus is on rural households (GCF funding will cover target regions, while SIDA will co-finance the remaining areas). In total, 102,500 households will be reached. Specifically, 82,000 households, including 27,000 women-headed households will be provided with information and material on EE-AF solutions (Sub-activity 2.3.1) and technical advisory services to help them make informed choices about their energy use (Sub-activity 2.3.2). This includes 50,000 HHs in the target regions (160–190 kHs/HH women-headed HHs) across the other regions of Georgia will be supported. In addition, pilot projects will be implemented to raise awareness and provide technical advisory services at community levels. Localized awareness creation and advisory services in the municipalities and villages (Sub-activity 2.3.1) will be implemented by GCF, while the provision of technical advisory services (Sub-activity 2.3.2) is implemented by IEC.

109. Sub-activity 2.3.1 Community awareness-raising and advisory services on EE-AF for fuelwood users in target regions and other locations across rural Georgia will provide information to households about the implications of the forest sector reform, the options for participating in forest management planning and related activities, and the EE-AF solutions that are available to them (Sub-activity 2.3.1). This includes chain-of-fuelwood vNFA (see Activity 1.3), as well as c) the provision of technical advisory services to fuelwood users on EE-AF solutions (Sub-activity 2.3.2). The awareness-raising and advisory services will involve close collaboration with regional and municipal authorities, trustees of the local communities, representatives of the rural women’s organizations, and other relevant stakeholders. A professional service provider will be selected by GCF through an open tender or grant awarding procedure to implement this sub-activity in the target regions (Sub-activity 2.3.1) as well as in other locations across Georgia, while SIDA co-financing will enable replicating and scaling up the same approach to other Georgia’s regions.

In the first stage, the project will reach out to and identify potential local partners and organize a series of meetings to provide information about awareness and communication strategy on EE-AF and the opportunities for participation in forest management planning. The project will also establish a network of local partners where they will be able to share experiences and lessons learned from other projects. In addition, project staff will visit communities to collect baseline data and conduct focus group discussions with households and local SMEs to identify their needs and preferences regarding EE-AF solutions.

In the next stage, once local partners are identified and links are established, the project will work through them to provide information on EE-AF solutions. The project will also establish a network of local partners as a platform to communicate with and receive feedback from local communities throughout project duration. Further, the project will organize awareness-raising events in rural areas (e.g., community meetings, simple (walk-in) energy audits and other type of energy advice to help households and local SMEs identify their needs and preferences regarding EE-AF solutions.

120. Sub-activity 2.3.2 National advocacy and awareness raising: At the national level, the project will run a GCF-funded country-wide awareness-raising and PR campaign to be implemented by the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) in collaboration with other relevant stakeholders. The campaign will use multiple languages with climate change and sustainable energy, including:

- Information on forest sector reforms and their roles, responsibilities, and opportunities related to the forest reform, including local authorities, citizens, community-based organizations, and commercial enterprises.

- Engaging youth and children in SFM campaigns through forestry knowledge publicity, tree species identification, and other activities.

- Organization of forest visit programs for journalists, youth, and students (specifically in the three target regions).

- Writing and publishing forestry-related stories targeting radio, newspapers and TV, as well as using social media to raise awareness about EE-AF solutions.

# Activity 2.4 Enabling policies and regulations

## Sub-activity 2.4.1 Capacity building for introduction and enforcement of energy efficiency and environmental standards and labelling scheme for EE appliances (above) in line with draft Energy Efficiency Law and National Energy Efficiency Action Plan (NEEAP)

### Introduction
The energy efficiency procurement in the public sector, as per relevant provision of the Energy Efficiency Procurement Directive (EEPD), is a key element to achieve the Sustainable Development(SD) is the key government body in charge of developing and promoting Energy Efficiency.

### Sub-activity 2.4.1 Capacity building for introduction and enforcement of energy efficiency and environmental standards and labelling scheme for EE appliances solutions. Due to the missing framework and low level of implementation, there are still significant challenges in the public sector, particularly for domestic produced appliances such as solid fuel space heater. That is why NEEA emphasizes on the development and adoption of legislation and its delegated acts for EE standards and labels specifically for heat stoves.

### Recognizing limited capacity of the Government and the market to simultaneously adopt/adapt at European level and implement national energy efficiency standards and labels specifically for heat stoves

- Development of secondary legislation on Energy Efficient Public Procurement (EEPP)
- Development of the National Guideline on EEPP, including appropriate methodologies, templates, case studies, etc.
- Provision of training and capacity building for public procurement authorities on EEPP application, including tendering procedures and evaluation criteria
- Pilot application of the EEPP in target regions. The support includes: the preparation and facilitation of procurement processes, including the development of technical specifications, tender documents, pre-tender meetings, bid opening ceremonies (as observer) and clarification/negotiation meetings.
