# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 34 OF 92

## Component 3: Livelihood opportunities and local-self-governance in forest management

This component aims to support the transition of forest management from a traditional approach (as described in the letter Annex 15a) towards its formal decision as described in the Funding Proposal but not yet finalized, expected for June 2020.

### 1.7 Transition to sustainable forest management with the ultimate goal to reduce pressure on forests

#### 1.7.1 Under the fuelwood sector reform, the fuelwood supply mechanism and timber harvesting will be solely responsible of the NPA. Up to now, members of local communities depend partly on informal or illegal wood harvesting for their livelihoods.

Under this component, the new forest code and the new National Principles, Criteria and Indicators for Sustainable Forest Management (NPS) will provide a legal framework for the reform of the fuelwood sector. At the same time, the new forest code and the NPS will enable local authorities and private forest management authorities to participate in forest management.

#### 1.7.2 Component 3 taps into these opportunities by:

- Strengthening the capacity of local communities and public authorities for sustainable forest management and conservation (Activity 3.1)
- Introducing and strengthening interest protection mechanisms in order to ensure a transparent and inclusive forest management planning and implementation (Activity 3.2)
- Developing and implementing a strategy for the development of new livelihoods and conservation through vocational education and international university partnerships with cement companies (Activity 3.3)

#### 1.7.3 These activities lead on one hand to a more active participation of local authorities and local population in decision-making processes, and on the other hand, to a more just redistribution.

On the one hand, through increased education and value chain development the local population benefits from diversified livelihoods and business opportunities and thus increases revenue.

#### 1.7.4 This Component utilizes technical assistance to implement the activities. The Component is solely financed by an SDC grant.

##### Activity 3.1: Development and introduction of municipal-level tools, practices, plans and capacities for participatory SFM and conservation

Municipalities will be given back their forest ownership of local communities thus supporting the national forest protection and conservation efforts. In addition, the value-added of SFM remains in the communities for their development.

The new forest code (Article 20) provides a legal framework for municipal-level forest management in the country. The new Forest Code (Article 20 "Competence of a Municipality" and article 21: Forest Management Plan) will provide municipalities with the necessary tools to manage forests at local level. Municipalities, however, this concept is as yet a nascent stage in Georgia, and only two forest areas are set for development under the new Forest Code (the Tbilisi Botanical Garden and the Tbilisi City Hall for the forest area of the city of Tbilisi).

The project will support municipalities to develop municipal-level forest management plans specifically designed for utilization in municipal forest management and conservation efforts. In addition, capacity development activities will be provided to enable local communities to utilize the developed instruments and to participate in management and conservation activities.

##### Activity 3.2: Development, testing and promotion of local mechanisms to better protect interests of all stakeholders

The new forest code and the Criteria and Indicators foresee a much stronger participation of local levels in decision-making processes on forest management plans, in decision-making on grazing areas and utilization of NTFP collection, and in the development of forest management plans. The project will support municipalities in developing forest management plans, in decision-making on grazing areas and utilization of NTFP collection, and in the development of forest management plans.

The project will also provide technical assistance to municipalities for the implementation of these activities.


---
**Page 38**
---

123. Activity 3.3: Development of professional skills on SFM and conservation through vocational education and international partnerships with centres of knowledge

This activity will provide training for a number of individuals, either as staff member of NFA or as part of service contractors, education in vocational training colleges and a respective degree is a formal requirement under the law. The training will be provided by international partners and will include opportunities via the forest sector reform are vast [10]. The Criteria and indicators clearly state that that preference for the development of professional skills on SFM and conservation through vocational education and international partnerships with centres of knowledge is a high priority. This activity will require organisational and business skills related to the production, processing and marketing of forest products. It will also provide training in the use of information technology and communication tools by staff to expand their already existing progress in the forest and value chain sectors. To diversify income opportunities for households and communities, this activity will provide training on entrepreneurship and business start-up. At level university, practical relevant research skills and forestry knowledge is required.

124. Activity 3.4: Introduction of selected value chains (timber, NFTP, eco-tourism)

This activity creates alternative livelihood opportunities for individuals and households in the target regions by increasing their income from other (more formal) sources of income, and thus compensate for loss of (informal) income sources caused by the forest sector reform. This will increase household incomes, which eventually supports potential fuel switch and purchase of legal, high quality timber.

The project facilitates the establishment of the new value chains as alternative livelihood options via technical assistance to households and communities in the target regions:

* Timber value chain: The market development for energy efficient technologies and alternative fuels (see Community-managed commercial value chains for NFTP) is a key opportunity for increasing household incomes through respective value chain development.
* Non-timber forest products (NTPP): Despite the fact that the build-up of NTFP (such as chestnuts, wild fruits, berries and mushrooms) values chains by private and community-based entrepreneurs, is legally possible in Georgia, there are no commercial opportunities for households and communities to benefit from it. While local people often collect them for sale, no harvesting limits are specified beyond which the collection of a given species becomes illegal. The market potential for NTFP is therefore not fully exploited as they remain under-developed, despite the economic potential. Community-run commercial value chains for NFTP will provide households and communities with an opportunity to benefit from this resource.
* Eco-tourism in forests and mountain areas: Tourism potential in Georgia, especially nature-based tourism, is a key opportunity for increasing household incomes through respective value chain development by households and communities to develop and participate in eco-tourism value chains, based on the recreational use of forests.

## for the forest sector

### Sub-activity 1.4.4 Improvements in education and training of the forest sector workforce

The new (draft) forest code requires all foresters and forest workers, including existing staff, to be trained on the new forest code. The Ministry of Agriculture is responsible for providing technical assistance required to address capacity barrier comprehensively and to ensure that the new forest code is fully implemented.

### Sub-activity 1.4.5 Education and training improvements: Increased awareness of climate change adaptation practices

This sub-activity will support the implementation of the education and training needs assessment, which was carried out in 2013. The Ministry of Agriculture will provide technical assistance to ensure that the new forest code is fully implemented.

### Sub-activity 1.5.1 Strengthening of the existing forest management information system (FMIS) architecture and development of FMIS includes

A comprehensive FMIS is required to provide, analyze and utilize data for the various stakeholders in the forest sector, enabling transparent supervision, monitoring, evaluation and reporting on the implementation of the new forest code.

### Sub-activity 2.1.1 Establishment of Assistance and Information Support Facility (AIFS)

TA will address capacity barriers on the side of EE/AF suppliers related to product development and market access for EE/AF products in Georgia. The TA will also support ARISA to integrate environmental considerations into its scope of its activities, including the identification of potential projects that could be developed under the forest sector action plan.

### Sub-activity 2.1.2 Facilitation of market assessment and priority product development for new EE/AF technologies

TA support under this sub-activity will address market failure and the barriers typically faced by small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) in Georgia, which are often unable to develop a new product but face the risk of initial investment in market research and development. The TA will also provide technical assistance to SMEs to enable entrepreneurs to bear down to a lack of strong demand for a product by consumers.

### Sub-activity 2.2.1 Design, implementation and evaluation of the voucher programme for EE stoves for households

TA to ARISA is required to design voucher programme for EE stoves and enable its implementation, monitoring and evaluation.

### Sub-activity 2.2.3 Support to financial institutions and partner banks

Georgia's financial institutions have limited awareness about EE and AF solutions, in particular EE stoves and other energy efficient appliances. The TA will support ARISA EE appliances, but none has had deal with financing EE retrofit measures or installation

## B

### To structure and promote commercial financing products for enhanced EE AF solutions

#### Subsection 2.3.1 GCF, BMZ SIGA
**Level of awareness**, as well as concerns among households about the implications of energy efficiency (EE) measures, are key barriers to market penetration. These can be identified through stakeholder consultations (see Section G.1). It is the key barrier both for EE and AF solutions. The project will address these issues by raising **awareness** in order to address this barrier in particular through on-the-ground work with households and other stakeholders.

### To structure and promote commercial financing products for introduction and implementation of energy efficiency and environmental benefits (EE) AF solutions

#### Subsection 2.4.1 BMZ
Policy changes are required to create lasting effect in the EE sector development framework, as they would include clear open air pollutant emissions and consumers and contribute towards the sector development trajectory towards EE and environmental performance standards in sectors. However, there is not full coverage of the Ecodesign Directive, as well as provisions for EE products and services. The project will address this barrier by providing a market entry point for EE products and services.

#### Subsection 2.4.1 BMZ
Facilitating introduction of EE AF solutions procurement mechanisms

### INVESTMENT SUPPORT THROUGH GCF FUNDING

Investment support is required to address financial barriers and budgetary shortfall in the forestry and household sector. It will work with the following partners: Government of Mozambique, GCF funding is being requested to co-finance alongside BMZ and the Government the following investments:

- Performance-based grant to GEF for implementation of PFs (Sub-activity 2.1.1) EUR 9.6 million
- Performance-based grant to GEF for implementation of EE AF solutions (Sub-activity 2.1.2) EUR 12.8 million
- Construction of BF facilities (Sub-activity 1.3.2) EUR 4 million

#### Subsection 2.2.1 BMZ
- Result-based financial incentive mechanism (voucher program) for EE services for households (Sub-activity 2.2.1) EUR 50 million

In addition, the following investment needs are 100% co-financed by project partners:

Under GEF/GEF Private Investment Window (3.1)
- Procurement of AF and EE AF solutions from investment grants, interest rates subsidies and guarantees to EE-AF suppliers, through the Government via ARDA (Sub-activity 2.1.1) EUR 4.3 million
- Interest rate subsidy for loans provided by forestry companies by the Government via ARDA (Sub-activity 1.1.2)
- EUR 13.5 million

Procurement of EE AF-solves from AF suppliers with loans funding from Chrysler Bank and other (Sub-activity 2.2.2) EUR 10.5 million

GCF grant for Component I (investment support to NFA and DES) will contribute to alleviating budgetary shortfall faced by the Government in implementing PFs, EE AF solutions and BF facilities. The project will also provide technical assistance, to be in the funding pie with significant commitment exceeding its current funding for the forest management system.

## Sub-activity 2.2.2 Providing consumer financing for EE stoves and AF for households

There is currently a lack of an easy-to-use financing product specifically targeted at the purchase of EE stoves and AFs, which are more energy-efficient than conventional stoves and this low activity area is bringing this funding gap via consumer financing. The GCF will provide 100% grant funding to cover the cost of providing consumer financing for the purchase of an EE stove with a microfinance loan at 30% effective interest rate (MFI) or 25% effective interest rate (NBFIs) and AFs with a microfinance loan at 40% effective interest rate. The resulting monthly instalment of GEF 128 is, according to MFIs, not affordable to most of the target population in Georgia. Therefore, the GCF will provide additional grant funding for the establishment built-in account requirement to minimize concessionality in the GCF- funded financing scheme will be put in place in partnership with local financial institutions (banks and MFIs) and other loan terms at all commercial terms covering the remaining balance of the loan.

The GCF will also provide 100% grant funding for the purchase of EE stoves and AFs by households living in rural areas, where the average monthly income is below GEL 80. The resulting monthly instalment of GEF 256 is affordable to most of the target population in Georgia.

The financing scheme will be put in place in partnership with local financial institutions (banks and MFIs) and other loan terms at all commercial terms covering the remaining balance of the loan.
- Banks and MFIs: TBC Bank, Raiffeisen Bank, GEBank, etc.
- Bank etc.: Bank of Georgia

B. Justification for GCF funding request

152. B.1. Funding gap: Forest sector

151. After years of forest degradation, Georgia is actively seeking to implement an ambitious forest sector reform in preparation for a market-based carbon (carbon credit) system; enhance the economic value and increase the socio-economic benefits of its forests. In the past, Georgia has put a lot of efforts and has closely worked with bilateral and multilateral partners to address the challenges related to forest management and protection. However, due to the complexity of the necessary change in both sectors, forestry and rural energy, is high, as is the need for workforce training.

152. Since independence, Georgia’s forestry sector has suffered from declining budgets for forest management and protection, severely undermining its capacity to properly enforce forest laws and regulations. NFA is dependent on external funding (from bilateral donors) to cover its operational costs. In 2016, the level of expenditures was EUR 9.66 million (GEL 28,88 million). Around 80% of NFA’s budget was from their own generated income (EUR 7.74 million or GEL 23.5 million) and around 20% of its budget came from state budget (EUR 1.92 million or GEL 6.3 million), which is deemed important as it allows NFA to be more independent in its decision-making process. This demonstrates a notable change since 2015, where 87% of NFA’s budget was from state funds. While NFA has been able to cover most of the operational costs with their own generated income and the state budget, there is still a need for additional investments in order to meet the needs of the sector. The level of expenditures necessary for investing in the forestry sector, in 2016, was EUR 4.86thha (GEL 14.48thha) per hectare. This means that NFA has been able to cover only about half of its operational costs and requires investments in the range of EUR 250-270 per hectare.

153. The budget is also heavily dependent on administrative costs (60% of NFA’s budget, which is mostly from its dedicated state budget) and other expenses (40% of NFA’s budget). Of this, 53% is from the state budget and 47% from other expenses. In addition to the above, NFA has also been able to complete an additional 30% of NFA’s budget (2016), is from state budget, which is deemed important as it allows NFA to be more independent in its decision-making process.

154. Following the adoption of the National Forest Strategy (NFS) by the government aims to start implementation of SPM in the eight target districts. According to calculations, for the first 15 years*, revenues from carbon credits will cover only about half of NFA’s operational costs and other expenses. The remaining costs, because of the timing required for forest management measures and wood supply system to reach results, are expected to be covered by the state budget. In this context, NFA has been able to complete an additional 30% of NFA’s budget (2016), is from state budget, which is deemed important as it allows NFA to be more independent in its decision-making process.

155. In context, this would be equivalent to half the government's expected budget support to the whole NFA in 2019 (EUR 38 million or GEL 117 million). The total investment required for SPM implementation is EUR 46.5 million (GEL 138 million) across all regions (which include 14 districts). Additional substantial investments are needed to build up the capacities of NFA and other relevant institutions, including private sector contractors, in order to ensure that they have the capabilities on forest management in its NFA and for private sector contractors, improving vocational-and education-based training, strengthening the regulatory framework and ensuring its alignment with the New Forest Code.

*Note: Costs of SPM can offer greater focus on the region and context (accessibility, legal situation, currency, and inflation) where investments are made. In this case, the costs for SPM in the eight target districts would be higher than the national forestry sector which requires investments within the larger reported area that actually covers into forest.

*Note: From year 16 onwards, the NFA will make profit from forest management measures and wood supply system, see next section for details.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 48 OF 92

## The UN Environment-level Criteria and Indicators for ecosystem-based forest management, among other investments.

### As shown above, the need for additional financial and technical assistance to cover the costs of initiating and conducting forest restoration is substantial. A report by the World Bank estimates that US$10 billion in climate change assistance projects are supporting the forestry and rural energy sector. The cooperation potentials are already identified through existing GCF investments in Georgia, including a $25 million (EUR 23 million) technical assistance partnership agreement with Georgia. To address this TCA shortfall, the project in addition to GCF funding has restricted access to the NFA’s cover SFRF investment window.

### In order to address funding shortfall at NFA’s cover SFRF investment needs, the following options can be considered:

- **Option 1:** Increase in public financing. The Government already committed to contribute some substantial additional resources to reduce the funding gap for SFM measures and wood supply in the 8 target provinces (EUR 20 million). This is equivalent to 3% of the total NFA budget (EUR 657 million or GEI 3,391 million). This is the maximum the Ministry of Finance is able to provide to the sector. In addition, the Government has committed to increase its contribution from EUR 20 million in 2018 to EUR 40 million in which this would equate to approx. EUR 3.4 million. In addition, the government makes available additional resources for forest restoration and SFM activities through the NFA budget (EUR 576 million) for forest supervision functions, as well as re-invest NFA’s future revenues in SFM. Georgian Government is now exploring the possibility of increasing its contribution to the NFA budget from EUR 20 million to EUR 40 million. This would be a substantial increase and will require further discussions with lenders about support to rural and agriculture SMEs.
- **Option 2:** The proposed activities for which GCF grant is requested will not directly result in revenue generation, but rather support the enabling environment for SFM planning and implementation. Therefore, it is important that the NFA budget be adjusted to include a dedicated funding stream to prevent illegal logging via DES. None of this investment though is revenue-generating and therefore loan financing would be required to cover these costs. The proposed activities will also contribute towards the NFA wood supply system due to the prolonged period of negative cashflows, towards covering the fund deficit (which is forecasted at 43.7% of GDP in 2019), and towards increasing the forest coverage (grade periods typically do not exceed 5 years). In addition, the SFM represents a complete overhaul of Georgia’s forest sector and will require significant investment to ensure its success. This would require lenders from supporting the project, especially with very long-dated loans that would be required due to cashflow constraints.
- **Option 3:** In addition, the two funding options described above are not advisable in light of Georgia's fiscal and debt situation. The government has committed to fiscal consolidation through an ongoing IMF program. Significant budget cuts have already been made by the Ministry of Finance in 2018 and 2019. The NFA budget deficit is forecasted to continue gradually increasing over the next five years, peaking at 53% of GDP in 2024 before gradually coming down to 5.7% in 2029. The budget deficit has been declining gradually since 2017/18 as envisaged by the IMF. The government is committed to reducing this deficit further and has already made significant cuts, which will continue gradually to 2.5% in the medium term starting from 2023. Maintaining the fiscal deficit within the above mentioned range would require a reduction of EUR 47 million (GEI 218 million) or 69% of the NFA budget for 2019. The budget deficit allow only for increased spending in education and investments in infrastructure that maximizes the economic benefits from the NFA’s forest sector activities, including SFM. This would require new commitments in many other directions and no room for additional liabilities.

### GCF funding is required to address, in the form of TA, extensive capacity building needs of the forest sector stakeholders. The project will also support the development of a national forest management plan (NFM) with GIZ support including forest management planning for 64,292 ha in Akhmeta, Kakheti, but available public funding is insufficient to cover these costs. The NFA budget deficit is forecasted at 43.7% of GDP in the current framework, the value of forest is being depleted through unsustainable extraction of timber and further investment will be required for SFM activities. This project will contribute towards scaling up SFM approach and eventual increase in forest’s economic, social and ecological values.

19. Affordability of EEAP products is the major financial barrier for consumers. Even for the least cost solution, where there is a substantial financial gap and low level of incentive, estimated price of a new EE stove is GEL 1,000 whereas a conventional stove can be bought for GEL 50-70, i.e. two times cheaper. In addition, the high upfront cost of EEAP products makes it difficult to access financing from commercial banks and consumers, with only few units sold currently in Georgia and lack of industry certifications of product quality.

20. The amount of up-front investment required to purchase a new EE stove is well beyond household’s ability to save or borrow. According to the 2016 World Bank report on Georgia’s Financial Inclusion, the maximum borrowing capacity as per Central Bank regulation and banks’ credit requirements also restrict households from accessing loans for EEAP products. The average monthly income of a typical Georgian consumer only finance the purchase of an EE stove with a micro-finance loan at 30% effective interest rate, repeat in GEL 129 is about 50% of the average household monthly income (HMI), it exceeds the borrowing threshold established by the Central Bank.

21. Grant funding is therefore required to address this gap and create a strong incentive for early adopters: those households willing to take the risk and invest in a much more expensive, unknown but promising alternative.

22. The amount of subsidy, i.e., up to 30% of the market price, has been established taking into account requirements to minimize concessionality in the GCF-funded project while enabling households to be eligible and afford EEAP products. This is achieved by limiting the maximum loan amount that can be provided by financial institutions (banks and MFIs) so that other loans at full commercial terms covering the remaining 70% of the purchase price are not available. The loan repayment period will be up to five years, with a grace period of six months from the date of disbursement. The interest rate on the loan is set as a monthly installment for the borrowing household in the range of GEL 80, which MFIs deem is within their lending capacity and risk tolerance.

23. Provision of publicly subsidized loans to meet a minimum level of demand (Section D.5.4 of the FP – determine the best practices). Similar consumer financing schemes in Georgia and elsewhere in the region, such as the EBRD Sustainable Energy Finance Facility (SEFF), provide subsidies for EEAP products, including energy efficient products, such as EE appliances.

24. On the supply side, in view of the barriers explained earlier in Section B.2 EE AP sector does not represent a viable market opportunity to private sector, for consumers. As regards EE-Ap Supplement, lack of effective market demand limits their ability to invest in EE-AP product development and manufacturing capacity expansion. The Government of Georgia has been providing technical assistance (TA) support activities under Component 2 project. In addition, the Government of Georgia through the state-owned company, GFCF, grants financial support for EEAP products to be invested in by private sector. However, constraints among EEAP sectors, such pipelines do not yet exist and there is a need for additional TA support. There is no on-going donor-supported project which would address, even partially, the TA needs in this sector.

B.5.3 Choice of instruments and concessionality

160. Both Components will catalyze significant co-finance from the government, the private sector and project beneficiaries. GCF grants, by filling selected funding gaps while minimizing concessionality, will be crucial to the successful execution of the project.

## Component 1

### 166. The model for SFM implementation and wood supply under the NFA is meant to be financially sustainable as the forest is generating revenues from timber and fuelwood sales that more than offset capital and operations and maintenance (O&M) costs. For the first 15 years, however, revenues will not be sufficient to cover O&M costs. The model assumes that the SFM system will be able to generate enough revenue for O&M with sales volumes to increasing accordingly. Without GCF support, the government would have to inject a total of EUR 20 million into the NFA over its lifetime.

### 167. A EUR 13.2 million GCF grant for Activity 1.1 and 1.3 of the project will contribute to alleviating this shortfall. The Government has committed to step in, out of budget sources, to fill the SFM remaining funding gap or EUR 800,000 per year over a period of 25 years (from 2019-2043). This is equivalent to covering the current funding received by the government to support the current forest management system, pro-rata in the first 15 years and fully in the second half of its lifetime. The EUR 800,000 per year will be expected for the NFA across the entire Georgia – the target districts represent 15% of the total NFA area.

### 168. Due to the prolonged period of negative cash flows, borrowing to cover the funding gap is not advisable, even if the government were able to borrow at a low interest rate (e.g., 3-4% per year over a 20-year period or less). In addition, the SFM activity represents a complete overhaul of Georgia’s forest management system and will require significant investments in infrastructure and capacity building. This is especially true with the very long-dated loans that would be required due to cash flow problems described above.

### Importantly, from year 16 onwards, the SFM will be consistently cashflow-positive, ensuring the long-term sustainability of the forest management system and the financial viability of the NFA. The project’s model is based on this assumption. This long-term financial sustainability is indeed one of the reasons for the government to transition from the current forest management system.

### 170. Without concessionality, the expected financial IRR (of Component 1 SFM) is 2.4% over 20 years, the time period conventionally used to evaluate the effectiveness of SFM projects. This is well below Georgia’s threshold for a financially viable project (5%). The project would be considered financially unviable with an expected negative EUR 8 million. A EUR 13.2 million GCF grant, covering approx. 25% of the SFM’s costs over the first five years, will ensure that the project is financially viable and meets Georgia’s financial viability threshold. The IRR reaches just above the sovereign cost of capital and does not create a financial windfall for the government.

## Component 2

### 171. The objective of GCF grants in Component 2 is to kick start two markets that are currently in their infancy in Georgia and bring them to a meaningful scale: EE stoves and AF production, the replacement of a conventional stove with an energy-efficient one, and the production of wood-based products from sustainably managed forests without GCF support (see section D.8 for details). In practice, several barriers hinder the widespread adoption of EE stoves and AF production in Georgia:

- **EE stoves:** The market is currently dominated by imported stoves with a high retail price point, above 50 Lari (~$17 USD) for a conventional one.
- **AF production:** Currently, there are only few units sold annually in Georgia. This is due to the low quality of wood-based products (e.g., poor quality of sawdust briquettes and stoves or imports (and for the latter, particularly high prices)), and lack of an on-tap financing product specifically designed for the purchase of EE stoves.
- To help consumers bridge this gap in the market, GCF support will be provided to the EE stove producers.

### 172. Through the financial component, the project will stimulate demand for up to 30,000 EE stoves and related services, including a marketing campaign that highlights the benefits of EE AF production businesses and bring the scale of production to the level where economy of scale could drive the product cost down significantly.

### 173. The GCF grant will be used to support the development of a market for wood-based products by providing financial support to such enterprises, to minimize concessionality and market distortions. GCF support will be provided through the following mechanisms: (i) Georgia’s existing Wood-Based Products Support Facility (WPSF), TASP, based at the Agricultural and Rural Development Agency (ARDA); together with BMZ.


---
**Page 54**
---

### Financial exit strategy and sustainability

The implementation of SFM (Activity 1.1) and the land-based activity (Activity 3.1) is in the medium-term cost recovery phase. The project will be financed under Section B.5 and Annex A (Financial and Economic Models), and is therefore financially sustainable. During project implementation, NAF will generate revenues from timber and other forest products, as well as from carbon credits. These revenues are expected to cover 100% of the costs of implementing SFM. NAF has sufficient resources to re-invest in establishing SFM system in other regions. These revenues will also be used for the implementation of the project’s exit strategy (Activity 3.2), as well as operations and maintenance costs for the sustained implementation of forested areas.

The Law of Georgia on Legal Entities of Public Laws provides for financial independence of NAF and ensures that the Charter of NAF has been approved by the Minister of MEPA which entail eligible activities for NAF to generate revenues from timber, other forest products and carbon credits. The eligible activities include forest management activities as defined in the Resolution of the Government of Georgia (GoG) No24. NAF revenues shall be accumulated at the special account opened in the State Treasury and reinvestment to scale.

**Page 55**
---

## Forest supervision by DES is a pure state-budget funded function.

The government of Georgia committed itself to develop and implement a system for forest supervision that will cover all operations and maintenance costs of equipment procured by government funding as well as GCF funding.

### The FIMS and MRV to be established will increase efficiency of data collection, monitoring and analysis and thus the overall cost. Since the operational and maintenance costs are covered by state budget commitments.

## Institutional sustainability is ensured by establishing the institutional and technical setup that sustains and promotes SFM in Georgia:

- **Developing clear standard operating procedures, manuals, guidelines, and other supporting documents for forest management and supervision**
- **Ensuring that the information generated from forest supervision activities is streamlined in the operations of forest management bodies and DES.**
- **Developing a market for EAF products by engaging private sector to practically implement SFM and for forest supervision (DES). This capacity development is not only implemented for and within the public sector but also with the private sector, which will be able to provide services that pave the ground for replication (see also below on knowledge and training platform).**
- **Setting up a market for EAF products by engaging private sector to practically implement SFM and forest supervision (DES)**
- **Ensuring that the market for EAF products is sustainable in Georgia. This ensures long-term availability and continuation of training and knowledge materials and events after project finalization to cover all aspects of EAF production, including post-harvest processing and marketing by private companies in the future. By creating qualified staff and strengthening ownership over sustainability in the forest sector, the market for EAF products will be sustainable in Georgia.**

### For Component 2, the EE-AF market development, the sustainability is ensured via the following approaches

- **The key element of Project's sustainability as far as EE-AF market promotion is concerned is to create lasting demand for EE-AF products. This will be ensured by implementing and enforcing SFM for a long as sustainable forest management is practiced in Georgia. The project will also address the need and demand for AF among rural households with existing EAF.**
- **The project's design involves several innovative elements, such as promotion of activity-based schemes to target specific groups (e.g., women), development of forest sector, and the creation of a market with high leveraging potential. While none of these models alone can address all barriers associated with EE-AF market development, they are complementary in nature and when combined will create a strong foundation for success. They offer an integrated package of scalable solutions aimed at removing barriers to market development and ensuring long-term sustainability of the market for EAF products.**

## Sustainability of market demand: A more formalized supply chain and a higher valuation of fuelwood as a result of new sector management model, along with strengthened consumer awareness will create lasting demand for EE-AF in Georgia. The project will also address the need and demand for AF among rural households with existing EAF. In addition, strict EE and environmental performance standards for EE stoves will substantially reduce the amount of fuelwood required to meet household energy needs. This will result in an increase in demand with new, attractive consumer loan products for EE-AF. The GCF funded voucher program will be used to promote EE-AF and AF among rural households with existing EAF. At this point, the goal of the incentive scheme and awareness activities is achieved: EE stoves are adopted by 350,000 households in Georgia. As a result of these efforts, the demand for EE-AF will increase significantly as EE stove will reduce significantly, while consumer awareness and confidence in the product enhances. A continued strong demand for EE-AF will be ensured.

## Sustainability of EE-AF supply: The need for and form of support required for EE-AF suppliers will largely be dictated by the prevailing market conditions and the strength of the sector. In Georgia, the EE stove sector is expected to grow at a high production rate (i.e., in the order of 10,000 - 50,000 stoves/year) as reached. There is no need any longer to build supplier's capacity for marketing, product quality, etc. All

D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

### D.1 Impact potential

The project contributes to the GCF’s objective and result area:

- Mitigation of emissions from land use, reforestation, reduced deforestation, and through sustainable forest management and conservation of forest carbon stock; and
- GHG emission reduction impacts have been calculated in addition for the energy sector (increased uptake of short-lived climate pollutants) and for the transport sector (reduced fuel consumption). The project’s core impact is reducing only GHG emission reduction for result M.40 have included as the core impact indicator of the project.

GHG emission reductions from SFR Mitigation potential in the project as a result of the introduction of SFR to the forest areas will be achieved through:

- Reducing degradation effects of illegal forest use and avoiding further degradation
- Increasing carbon stock enhancement through SFR

In the past, decades Georgia and the project region has experienced significant loss of carbon within the forests, therefore for the baseline scenario of the project period of 7 years as well as the project lifetime of 20 years a continuation of this trend is expected. The project will introduce sustainable forest management (SFM) in Georgia first requires an overall planning and implementation phase for the entire forest areas, which includes elements such as: developing a national SFM strategy, establishing a national SFM committee, defining guidelines for all forest areas within a forest district with a view to balancing the sometimes divergent objectives of timber production and biodiversity conservation. The project will also develop a detailed plan for the introduction of direct management prescriptions for particular forest sites depending on the identified forest functions. Direct implementation of these plans will be carried out in collaboration with local communities. In addition, there are silvicultural measures within the forests. The first impact of the overall planning and further implementation of SFM at all levels is to reduce degradation effects of illegal forest use and avoiding further degradation. This impact component will affect at the forest areas where they will be just protected or also actively managed.

206. On the project side, GWF/EF will play a critical role in ensuring replication of the project’s significant potential for scaling-up and replication. First, the Facility will enable gradual accumulation of NFA revenues and their reinvestment into the forest sector to ensure long-term sustainability of funding for SFM, completely independent from the state budget. Second, GWF/EF will create an enabling environment for private sector actors (including smallholders) in EE-AF supply chain and ensure the market is capable to respond to growing demand with quality products at competitive prices (e.g., through a public-private partnership window) and ARDA (private sector window) which will also help ensure sustainability of the Facility beyond the project period.

### D.2.2 Potential for knowledge sharing and learning

The project builds on substantial work conducted by GIZ and other donors in the forest sector,178 which has generated a strong knowledge base. Knowledge management in the context of the project aims to strengthen the capacity of key stakeholders (e.g., local communities, private sector actors, government officials) to implement SFM, including through the development of KMTPs. The project will also support the development and implementation of effective communication strategies for knowledge sharing and learning among all relevant stakeholders.

#### 207. Systems for knowledge management and training will be strengthened through the development of the KMTP, which will include a comprehensive set of tools and mechanisms to ensure the sustainability of the project’s effectiveness on project measures, but will also support the long-term sustainability of the project. In addition, the project will develop a comprehensive communication strategy that includes a range of activities aimed at engaging stakeholders at all levels are aware of regulatory changes, as well as the importance of forests to support not only climate change mitigation and adaptation.

The knowledge to be generated through this project, in particular the practical application of SFM in the project regions, is essential to help Georgia ensuring successful implementation of its forestry sector reform. A large share of the project’s outputs will be disseminated via a variety of channels including:

- Knowledge distribution via government and partner’s websites
- Dissemination of best practices through a knowledge sharing platform to strengthen knowledge management, dissemination and training
- SFM-related publications (e.g., books, manuals, soft copies, and other supporting material including e-courses, and videos), and other informative materials on SFM (brochures, posters, etc.)
- Training courses for forest workers and forest managers in the project regions
- Training modules and procedures for SFM and regulatory changes for NFA and DES staff that improve institutional knowledge management and learning within government institutions

Assessment of the project’s impact will be based on the following indicators provided excerpts in the three project regions:

- Ecosystem services, to support ongoing forest information data collection and monitoring
- Documentation of successful experiences and lessons learned from implementation of SFM, and promotion of these through various channels (e.g., websites)
- Vocational education courses to support the education of forest workers and foresters, under the new forest management system

Package of PRD and promotional materials for households on EE-AF products and financing option

Training modules and procedures for SFM and regulatory changes for NFA and DES staff that improve institutional knowledge management and learning within government institutions

### D.2.3 Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

The project’s knowledge management approach aims to link it existing complementary information channels (e.g., the NFP process, local Civil Society Organizations (CSOs), community liaisons and municipalities).179

### D.2 Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies

#### D.2.4 Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies

217. With the approval of the new Forest Code, there are various secondary legal acts that will need to be revised and/or developed in order to ensure a coherent regulatory framework for forest management, conservation and utilization and elaborating secondary legal acts for the timber law. The Project also supports the elaboration of a national strategy on non-timber forest products (NTFP) and the development of rules and regulations for NTFP resources that can be commercially harvested, amounts for each NTFP that can be commercially harvested, fees for commercial harvesting, processes for commercial harvesting (applications, forms, monitoring, timing / zones), among others.

218. In addition, technical assistance will be provided to DES to support the harmonization of standards and systems in accordance with international best practices and the development of a national system for the registration and enforcement under the new forest code and liability law, ensuring coherence with revised related secondary legal acts. The Project also supports the elaboration of rules and regulations on the use of NTFP resources and guidelines for the newly established EBTs to ensure their transparent, sustainable, and efficient operation.

219. As regards EE and AF market development, the project will support MoE/EDG and the Public Procurement Agency (PPA) in developing a national strategy for public procurement of energy-efficient products and services under EE and RE acquis, which have direct relevance and implications for the EE-AF sector and reduction of the fossil carbon footprint. The Project also supports the development of guidelines on the use of international standards and labeling scheme for domestic heating appliances (stoves) in line with draft EE Law and NEAPs and LPPs, as well as the energy efficiency procurement in the public sector, as per relevant provision of the EED and draft EE Law.

#### D.2.5 Contribution to climate-resilient development pathways consistent with relevant national climate change objectives

220. The NDC for Georgia makes a particular reference to the forest sector due to its ability to contribute to climate change mitigation activities. It is the only sector with quantified and clearly spelled out unconditional and conditional commitments in the NDC. The Project will support the implementation of the 2015-2030 national objective for this sector and the ongoing forestry sector reform process as it role in fulfilling national climate change commitments. The Project will also contribute to the achievement of the 2030 national objective, which is a key objective for the forest sector, i.e., to ensure at least 250,000 ha of forests under SFM by 2030. In the ongoing reform process, the Project will support the development and implementation of a new carbon sink strategy with an aim to increase the carbon sink capacity of the forest sector by 10%. In the draft Climate Action Plan (CAP) of Georgia, a new carbon sink strategy is proposed for the period 2015-2030. The Project will support the development and rise in ambition is a direct consequence of this GCF proposal, since expected project results were taken into consideration during the preparation of the CAP and the NDC. The Project will also contribute to the rise in ambition and therefore prioritized for climate adaptation measures by the Government in the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) under the Paris Agreement on Climate Change.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 63 OF 92

## Social and health co-benefits.

Social and health benefits of the project will arise from improvement in living and occupancy conditions for rural households. Due to low efficiency, combustion of fuelwood in conventional stoves is associated with high levels of indoor air pollution (IAP). Exposure to IAP has been linked to a range of respiratory and cardiovascular effects such as acute lower respiratory infections, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, asthma, bronchitis, heart attacks, stroke, and lung cancer. The project will reduce household exposure to IAP by 50% through the installation of energy efficient stoves for 30,370 rural households over a period of at least three years. This has positive effects on life expectancy and quality of life in the target communities.

## Economic co-benefits.

The project will lead to long-term economic benefits for rural households, most of them considered poor or low-income. Installation of EE stoves, for example, will result in reduction by 50% of yearly energy consumption per household (from 30 kg of coal equivalent per year to 15 kg). This will free up time for other activities such as income generating activities and child care. The project will also lead to the development of a value chain for bamboo products, which is expected to create employment opportunities in the three target regions. Bamboo-based products are currently produced by small-scale enterprises that sell their products at wholesale prices. The project will support the establishment of bamboo factories that will employ about 50 people. With anticipated 8-fold market growth for these products a comparable increase in income can be expected.

## Gender sensitive development impacts

Women in rural settings are highly dependent on natural resources, in particular firewood and water. Women spend up to 2 hours per day collecting fuelwood and water from distant sources, which will help secure the resources they are dependent on. Woman-headed households constitute 35% of total households in the three target regions (see Table 1). The project will reduce their time spent collecting fuelwood by 50%. Additionally, the labor burden for women will be decreased as such less fuel wood needs to be brought in the month.

## SOG 15 "Life on Land"

- Reducing GHG emissions due to the adoption of SFM (reduced degradation and enhancement of carbon stocks), and the adoption of more efficient technologies.
- Strengthening forest ecosystems' resilience to climate change including through strengthening forest management, reducing fire risk, and improving forest health monitoring.
- Improved provision of ecosystem services. **SFM** will reverse ongoing degradation trends, which currently lead to the reduced provision of ecosystem services. Most mountainous areas are outside the scope of this project, given that 95% of Georgia's forests are in hilly or mountainous areas. Climate-responsive measures be implemented that consider the high vulnerability of these ecosystems and their contribution to the sustainable management of and ultimately conservation of mountain ecosystems.
- Enhancement of biodiversity through SFM, considering improved planning processes (broad investment opportunities) and implementation of biodiversity investments at scale. Improved monitoring of biodiversity through investments in SFM, SFM planning processes and management activities.
- Protection and restoration of degraded forests through SFM. Targeted silvicultural practices will aim to restore ecosystem health, and diversity (improving resilience), while also reducing the incidence of and impacts from landslides, **support river bank stabilization**, enhance soil carbon stocks, and improve water quality.

## SOG 16

- The importance of the participatory approach for forest management planning and introduction of conflict resolution mechanisms contributes to transparency and accountability of institutions involved in forest management and forest investment.

### D.4 Needs of recipient

#### D.4.1 Vulnerability of the country and/or specific vulnerable groups, including gender aspects (for adaptation only)

22 Climate change and its adverse impacts on ecosystems and economy pose severe threats to Georgia's sustainable development. Vulnerability of forest sector to climate change impacts is underscored in Georgia's NDC. The forest sector is particularly vulnerable due to the high proportion of degraded forests in Western and Eastern Georgia[1]. As a result of these impacts, forest ecosystem services such as the soil protection function are likely to be reduced or lost if no appropriate adaptation measures are implemented. Currently Georgia is, however, lacking comprehensive and evidence-based research on the vulnerability of its forest ecosystems to climate change. This proposal aims at addressing this gap by providing detailed actions to increase the resilience of forest ecosystems. Different forest types, climate conditions and other local conditions determine regional specific risks and needs for forest assessments.

[1] FAO 2014. Forests in Georgia: A National Forest Assessment. Available online at: http://www.fao.org/3/a-i6985e.pdf
[2] Jaffe, S., Smith, J. et al. 2014. Agro-Forestry Climate Adaptation Strategy. Thematic Group.
[3] FAO. 2011. Forests and landdegradation. Bangkok, Thailand.
[4] Georgia NDC. Available online at: http://www.fao.org/3/i9685e/i9685e.pdf

**Page 69**

223. The project will increase the resilience of 270,407 ha of forests through the implementation of sustainable forest management practices. This will ensure that these forests can be resilient to climate change and other stressors.

- Forest vulnerability assessments to inform sustainable forest management based on climate-resilient strategies.
- Recommendations for adaptation actions in the three target regions will be carried out. This will fill a key information gap at the regional level, which is currently lacking. The recommendations will also be integrated into FMPs and annual plans. Recommendations will also be made to strengthen the monitoring of forest resilience and climate change impacts on forests.
- Consideration of climate change risks (current and future) in forest management planning and implementation, including fire risk reduction measures (e.g., prescribed burning, fuelwood harvesting, and maintenance into annual plans). This could include integrated fire management practices, the promotion of resilient tree species, and the establishment of firebreaks. The project will also promote the use of climate-resilient buffer zones along waterways, and improved forest health through regeneration.
- Improved monitoring of forest resilience to climate change impacts (e.g., increased exposure to pests and diseases) and adaptation measures in forest management planning.

D.4.2 Economic and social development level of the country and the affected population

225. Georgia has a human development index (HDI) of 0.78, ranking 179 globally. Major strides have been made in reducing poverty, where the number of people living in poverty declined from 36% to 21% in 2017.

The project takes into account three dimensions: providing an estimate of and assessing the development of a country long-term health and life expectancy, education level, and income. The project will also provide information on the economic situation of Georgia, including its GDP per capita, poverty rate, and unemployment rate.


---
**Page 70**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 PAGE 67 OF 92

The number of people living in poverty and extreme poverty in Georgia is higher than in other countries in Europe and Central Asia. Also nearly half of the poor population is considered as "vulnerable to falling into poverty" (World Bank, 2014). In 2016, with inequality levels slightly lower than Turkey and Russia, and higher than Armenia and Azerbaijan.

### Vulnerability in target regions

Project target regions are among the most socially vulnerable in Georgia. The region of Kakheti is ranked number three after Tbilisi and Imereti in terms of total number of households that receive social assistance (World Bank, 2014).

Poverty and low-income level are an important social issue and also a major barrier to wider uptake of energy efficient technologies among rural households. Through the GFRP, the project will work and support Georgia’s efforts to improve access to affordable and reliable electricity for all Georgians by promoting efficient heating appliances. Moreover, The GFRP-supported component 3 directly addresses vulnerable groups in terms of social protection, poverty reduction, and access to energy services, and to develop mechanisms at local level to better protect the interests of adversely affected stakeholders.

### D.4 Absence of alternative sources of financing (e.g. fiscal or balance of payments gap that prevents government from implementing projects)

The government’s financial operations are constrained by fiscal consolidation, to which the government has committed under an ongoing IMF program. Significant budget reforms were introduced in 2017 and no increase in public spending is expected for the next three years. The fiscal deficit is projected to be reduced gradually since 2017/18 as envisaged by the IMF. The deficit is forecasted at 2.6% of GDP in 2019, has to be below 3% of GDP in 2020 and 2021.

Mimicking the fiscal deficit within the above-mentioned limits is extremely important since this is the debt sustainability threshold for Georgia. The government’s medium-term budget framework (MTBF) projects that the reduced gradually and maintained within 35–40% of GDP in the medium term. Within this fiscal consolidation framework, the government has committed to reduce public spending on social services and investments in infrastructure that maximizes the usage of Georgia's transit and tourism potential. At the same time, there are constraints on including new commitments in many other directions and no room for additional liabilities.

### D.4 Need for strengthening institutions and implementation capacity

Key government institutions in the forest sector for the implementation of this project are the Ministry of Environment Protection and Agriculture (MEPA), National Forestry Agency (NFA), the Department of Supervision (DES) and the Environmental Information and Education Center (EIEC). The forest sector reform clarifies the roles and responsibilities of these institutions, which will improve their capacity and effectiveness in the sector. In the course of the project design, capacity assessment has been undertaken to

## D.5 Country ownership

### D.5.1 National climate strategy

#### 231. The government of Georgia commits to reduce its GHG emissions by 15% below the Business as usual scenario (BAU) for the year 2030 in its NDC submission to the UNFCCC.

The committed mitigation target is a 34% reduction in emissions intensity per unit GDP compared with BAU. This translates into an absolute emission reduction of 68 MtCO2e, which is equivalent to 43% of emission intensity per unit of GDP from 2010-2030.

Georgia’s nDC singled out forests as a key sector for action and committed to reducing emissions in the forest sector by 50% below BAU. This represents an absolute reduction commitment, which is equivalent to 43% of emission intensity per unit of GDP from 2010-2030.

In the ongoing update of the NDC the government has further strengthened its ambition for the forest sector and committed to reducing emissions in the forest sector by 50% below BAU. This represents an absolute reduction commitment, which is equivalent to 43% of emission intensity per unit of GDP from 2010-2030 (conditionally). In the ongoing update of the NDC the government has further strengthened its ambition for the forest sector and committed to reducing emissions in the forest sector by 50% below BAU. # D.5 Capacity of Accredited Entities and Executing Entities to deliver

## D.5.4 Capacity of Accredited Entities and Executing Entities to deliver

234. GIZ is one of the largest international providers of capacity development and technical assistance on climate change worldwide. GIZ is currently carrying out over 300 climate-related projects worldwide, with combined funding of approximately €1 billion. The majority of these projects are implemented in developing countries, supplemented by activities on climate financing. A significant part of GIZ’s work is implemented in least developed countries (LDCs) and small island states (SIDS). GIZ has a strong track record in the areas of forest management and community-based forest management (FSC/MCPFE), natural resource management (NRM), forest biodiversity, and climate change. The total budget for GIZ’s projects worldwide is €1 billion. The current GIZ forestry portfolio amounts to EUR 47 million, allocated across 63 forestry projects worldwide.

235. GIZ acts as the executing entity in this project. GIZ has a strong track record of implementing National Forest Agency (NFA), Department of Environmental Supervision (DES) (representing the State of Georgia), and Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MENR) projects, including the current project. All four national EEs are established as legal entities of public law under the Ministry of Environmental Protection and Agriculture. The capacity of each EE to deliver this project has been assessed and confirmed through a due diligence process. See also section A.4.2 for details of the Executing Entities’ experience and track record.

## D.5.5 Role of National Designated Authority (NDA)

The project has been developed with active engagement from and under the strong leadership of the NDA (Ministry of Environment Protection and Agriculture). The Project Proposal was submitted to the GCF Secretariat in 2017 and the Concept Note was prepared under close consultation of key stakeholders in the sector. The draft version of the Project Document was reviewed by the NDA and other relevant stakeholders prior to submission to the GCF Secretariat in July 2018 in form of an endorsement letter. Since then, the project has formed part of the national climate change strategy and action plan (NCCSAP) and has been subject to multi-stakeholder workshops to provide feedback on the project. A letter of no objection has been provided by the NDA, which confirms that the project is consistent with Georgia’s country priorities, strategies and plans, and that it is in accordance with relevant laws and regulations.

## D.5.6 Stakeholder engagement

The project preparation team of GIZ met with a variety of stakeholders from government, private sector and civil society organizations to ensure alignment on project design, including 25 planning workshops, a separate consultation workshop with NGOs and field trip and stakeholder consultations in the target region. For details on the stakeholder engagement during project development see section A.4.3.

## D.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

### D.6.1 Cost-efficiency

The project will result in 95.1 million tCO2eq of emission reductions over the project lifetime of 20 years. This equates to a mitigation cost to the GCF of EUR 29tCO2eq (see section E.2.2) and represents considerable value for money, given that the average cost per tonne of CO2eq in the carbon market is currently considered as well (see section B.3). Relevant international benchmarks for abatement costs in forestry sector are also available (see section B.4).

McKinsey 2008: A Pathway to an energy and carbon efficient Russia

McKinsey 2008: An Australian Cost Curve for Greenhouse Gas Reduction


---
**Page 73**
---

The document discusses economic return metrics for forest projects under the Green Climate Fund (GCF), specifically focusing on the Economic Rate of Return and Financial Rate of Return.

### D.6.2 Economic Rate of Return

- **Definition**: The economic internal rate of return (ERR) reflects two categories of incremental benefits: (i) value of carbon sequestration from forest activities; and (ii) economic value added.
  - Incremental benefits include:
    - Incremental employment at the NFA and its contractors
    - Incremental household income from sale of commercial forest products, including fuelwood
    - Incremental employment at LULUCF projects
- **Example**: A project in Georgia with a ERR of 10% using a discount rate conventionally used in similar economic analyses.
- **Comparison**: The ERR is consistent with the European Commission's recommended CO2 price assumption of EUR 30 per tonne of CO2 equivalent (tCO2e) for projects starting after mid-June 2019.

### D.6.3 Financial Rate of Return

- **Definition**: In Component I, only Activity 1.1 (SFM implementation) and 1.3 (Fuelwood supply generation) are considered. The financial rate of return is the internal rate of return (IRR).
- **Activity 1.1**: The expected ERR without GCF support is 2.4% over 20 years, which is below Georgia's fiscal cost of capital.
- **Activity 1.3**: The ERR for fuelwood supply generation is 5.8%, exceeding the fiscal cost of capital by 0.8 percentage points.

### D.6.4 Best available technologies and practices

- **Best practices**:
  - Based on revised guidelines of key forest management regulations.
  - Consideration of new forest codes, national priorities, criteria and indicators for SFM, and management-level criteria and indicators for ecosystem-based forest management.

#### D

**244. Best practices for EE-AF market development:**
The project is using best international experience and practices in designing EE-AF discount programme for households, in particular German experience with jump-starting the market through a Renewable Energy Programme (MAP) [157] bonuses for households have been provided to stimulate wider uptake of EE-AF. The European Commission has also supported the development of an EU-wide market incentive scheme for renewable energy technologies and services. This has led to substantial growth of EE-AF market in household segment. It is still in operation and is being progressively adjusted to reflect new developments in the field, including the introduction of a range of investment support schemes for EE-AF in EU.

**245. As far as policy and regulatory environment is concerned:**
The project builds on and seeks to transpose applicable international experience and practices into national legislation and operational best practices in the field:
- Energy Efficiency Directive (EED) 2012/27/EU
- Energy Performance of Buildings Directive (EPBD) 2010/31/EU
- Energy Labelling Directive 2010/30/EU
- Ecodesign Directive 2009/125/EU

## Activity 2.3 Implementing consumer financing mechanisms for EE-AF solutions

This Activity jump-starts the market and scaling-up demand for EE-AF products. It provides financial incentives to households, organizations, local banks and MFIs, by providing them with information (awareness and financial incentives) focusing mainly on expanding other EE-AF technologies. By providing consumer financing mechanisms, it enables the purchase of an EE stove and/or other EE-AF appliances for the annual supply of AF households.

### 2.3.1 Creating consumer awareness and promoting EE-AF advisory services for fuelwood users

This Activity will reach out to fuelwood users and Georgia's population at large with information on the environmental, health and economic implications, as well as alternatives to fuelwood use. It will provide them with informational and technical advisory support in relation to EE-AF stoves and other technologies across rural Georgia, as well as urban areas. At least 10% of the target region's households will be supported in the target regions and at least 1 million people will be reached through awareness raising campaigns. The share of people supporting the main direction of the campaign is expected to reach at least 50%.

### Activity 2.4 Enabling policies and regulations

This Activity provides technical assistance to public authorities to accelerate transition and implementation of relevant policies and regulatory instruments envisaged in the EE Action Plan, including:

- Energy efficiency and environmental standards and labeling for EE-AF solutions
- Facilitating introduction of EE procurement practices in public sector

E7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements

246. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (in addition to E&MIP and GAP monitoring) will comply with the relevant GCF policies, as stipulated in the ABA, FAF and PAF.

247. The project will apply a customized results-based Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) system. The system will be based on:

* GIZ Standard Operating Procedures ("GIZ's evaluation policy - principles, guidelines and requirements")
* The project implementation schedule
* Results-based monitoring and performance reporting
* Project partners' Standard Operating Procedures
* Project partners' internal M&E systems and/or other stakeholders in Georgia

248. The M&E system will track project inputs, actions, activities, outputs, and impacts as well as associated financial flows across all Components at project districts and at national level in Georgia. The overall responsibility and oversight for M&E and reporting lies with the GCF AE unit of GIZ head office. The national Project Management Unit (PMU) in Georgia will implement the M&E system and work closely with EEP, Government project partners and development partners.

249. Recruitment of M&E staff: Immediately when the project commences, GIZ's GCF AE unit at head office in Germany will make available one expert to oversee, coordinate and support the implementation of the M&E system for the project. The expert will be responsible for providing guidance on the implementation of the project's M&E system. As soon as GCP proceeds become available in Georgia, the PMU will recruit full-time M&E specialist. The PMU will additionally hire an international consultant to company to design and to support the management of the M&E system and provide on-the-job training for PMU and other stakeholders of the system when requested.

250. Interim evaluation: GCF AE unit will initiate an Interim Evaluation at year four of the project (or at any time that GIZ, the NDA and/or the Project Steering Committee considers it necessary). The Interim Evaluation will include a review of the progress made in achieving the project's results and key project stakeholders including target groups and beneficiaries, project partners and contributing development partners. The Interim Evaluation will include


---
**Page 86**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 83 OF 92

E

* A review of the institutional, administrative, organizational, environmental, social, economic, technical and financial aspects of the project based on the assumptions and risks included in the design (among others as specified in the Funding Proposal and Feasibility Study) and M&E system.
* An assessment of the need to change or waive conditions relevant or not be changed or waived due to altered conditions.
* A review of the viability of remaining planned impacts.
* An assessment of the need to restructure or reformulate the project and the effects of such restructuring on the project's objective and long-term goals.

251. GIZ’s AE unit will make available an MTR report to the GCF Secretariat and project stakeholders.

Final evaluation: In due time before the completion of the project, GIZ’s AE unit will initiate a project completion mission, in which the implementation of the project based on the approved design (including assumptions and risks) is assessed. The assessment will be conducted by an independent third party and may involve project stakeholders including target groups and beneficiaries, project partners and contributing development partners. At the time of the project’s physical completion or commissioning, and before the expiry of the guarantee period, GIZ’s AE unit will make available a final report to the GCF Secretariat and project stakeholders.

253. Data collection and frequency: The PIU will coordinate data collection for implemented activities (indicators, implementation challenges and financial status) through responsible executing departments / divisions at the national and regional level on a regular base. The PIU will supervise and guide the monitoring and evaluation.

# Risk Assessment and Management

## F. Risk Factors and Mitigation Measures

### Selected Risk Factor 1
**Category:** Technical and operational  
**Probability:** Medium  
**Impact:** Medium  

#### Description:
254. Forest management inventories and FMIs developed may differ from the forest model, resulting in a different prioritization of FMIs for different target areas (e.g., the amount of timber that can be sustainably harvested).

#### Mitigation Measures:
- The Feasibility Study emphasizes that while the forest model was developed using the best available information (existing studies and extensive consultation with the Georgian government and other stakeholders), it is not a perfect representation. FMIs should be further informed by up-to-date forest inventories, particularly in cases where data and information are lacking.
- Forest Management Inventories conducted to inform FMIs will provide more accurate information and prioritize necessary FMI activities.

#### Description:
254. While the proposed SFM practices are not expected to drastically change since SFM involves a broad approach that is already being implemented by the Georgian government, some adjustments may be required in practices and targets may be required (e.g., areas where certain practices are implemented, annual allowable cut levels). The Feasibility Study provides an overview of the main assumptions for FMIs. Changes in SFM activities may also impact the revenues from SFM; however, business plans will be developed based on the forest model and confirmed SFM activities.

#### Description:
When necessary to change (adjust) targets, close communication will be ensured with GCF and other project stakeholders regarding changes to SFM best practices (and benchmarks), and a clear justification for any adjustments will be provided. 

### Selected Risk Factor 2
**Category:** Technical and operational  
**Probability:** Low  
**Impact:** Medium  

#### Description:
255. Over-commercialization of forest resources (timber and non-timber forest resources) to increase revenues at the expense of long-term sustainability may lead to a shift from SFM to unsustainable forest management practices focusing on the over-exploitation of timber and fuelwood.

#### Description:
- Forest Management Inventories to be conducted as part of the FMI development process, based on best practices. FMIs will be directly linked to MFIAs and revised by MoFPA. Trainings will be provided to MoFPA staff in conducting forest management inventories.
- Training sessions for NFA to conduct forest management inventories, developing FMIs based on inventory data, and preparing FMI reports will also be provided.

### Footnote:
The main assumptions for the forest model are provided in the Feasibility Study. Chapter 3.2.3.4.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V.030 | PAGE 85 OF 92

## Selected Risk Factor 1

### Category: Governance
**Probability:** Low  
**Impact:** High  

Description:
256. While efforts to reduce corruption in the forest sector have been successful, there is still a risk that corruption could persist.

Mitigation Measure(s):
- The country's anti-corruption strategy has been strengthened within the sector under the National Forest Law and ongoing monitoring areas are now regularly monitored.
- A regular range of patent districts and logging areas are now subject to regular monitoring by the timber concession system, which is being phased out and more transparent systems present.

### Category: Technical and operational
**Probability:** High  
**Impact:** Low  

Description:
257. Staff turnover may result in loss of knowledge, information, and capacities.
- Development of SOPs and secondary legal acts, standardized training modules for onboarding of new staff members that ensure the implementation of standard practices by new staff members.
- Standardized basic training, and improved knowledge management.

Post of Forest Management: The capacity and capabilities will be built within existing structures to ensure that the same training can be repeated, scaled up to other district regions in the country. Training modules will be integrated in the online platform (e.g., e-courses, videos from in-person trainings, course materials, etc.)
