# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 86 OF 92

## Selected Risk Factor 5

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

### Description

258. Limited opportunities for local people to benefit from sustainable forest management under the new system (e.g., lack of access to markets, limited participation in decision-making processes, lack of fuelwood). Strengthened forest supervision and law enforcement combined with the pricing of fuelwood may negatively impact the livelihoods of vulnerable dependent households.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

- Past use of the forest was often conducted in illegal/unlawful ways and based on unsustainable utilization practices. The project will support the development of a new Forest Management Code and national criteria and indicators for SFM, especially later under the social principle, recognizing the rights of indigenous peoples and local communities to participate in decision-making and implementation process. Community-outreach and continued engagement is a cross-cutting measure.
- Local communities will be provided support and incentives to switch to more efficient energy applications (e.g., improved cookstoves, solar power) and alternative livelihoods (e.g., beekeeping, fuel cutting).
- Context 3 provides alternative livelihood opportunities for rural communities through forest and energy related value chain creation.

### Description

Given the importance of ensuring stakeholder consultation and engagement in the forest management planning process, the project aims to set a positive example of how such engagement can be achieved. The project will support the development of a new Forest Management Code (delivered one at each of the beginning, middle and final validation), and additional technical support for the implementation of the code. This will include the conduct of public and full consultations. Georgia promotes SFM, and thus this participation process also is an important pathway to promote SFM in Georgia. The project will provide financial support for these activities (e.g., travel, accommodation, recreation etc.). Targeted support will be provided to NPA and local communities to improve their awareness on the new Forest Management Code.

### Description

Awareness raising will be conducted at various levels and engaging diverse actors within Activity 2-3. A series of workshops will be organized for key stakeholders (e.g., government officials, forest sector reformers and alternative energy efficiency NGOs, local municipalities and communities) to discuss the new Forest Management Code and its implementation.

### Description

The new structure of the forest management will result in additional jobs for local people involved with the harvesting of timber, non-timber forest products, and other activities such as seasonal staff. To the greatest extent possible the project will support the hiring of local people through a dedicated employment program (e.g., community-based enterprises) to provide sustainable operations on the ground. This will help transform jobs from the informal sector, providing more significant income.

### Description

Currently the NPA is not able to provide sufficient information about households currently receiving payments for their forest products. This will be challenging for these households to pay CEF $1/m2.

## Description

**259. The approval of policies and regulations is linked to political processes within the country, over which the project has no control. Thus, there are risk that policies, secondary legal acts, forest management plans, etc., may be changed or delayed in their implementation, leading to delays in project start-up. Delays in approval of the forest code, FMPs, and key regulations may also impact the project.**

### Specific measures to address this risk are limited given that this process (for Laws and regulations) is embedded within Parliament and is thus out of the project's control.

#### Delay in the elaboration, adjustment and/or approval of some FMPs, work on others can be continued with the support of donors such as GIZ. The team is able to start relatively quickly given that the current versions of NFIs and FMs are available for reference.

#### Outside of the project, GIZ has already committed to support the Government of Georgia with the revision of the current versions of 179, 241 and 242 prior to project start, to ensure their timely revision.

### GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

#### G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment

204. The Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA) and Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) have been prepared in support of the project and are presented in Annex 6. The project has been designed to comply with the requirements set out in the National Forest Code (NFC), the National Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan (NBSAP), the National Forest Programme (NFP), the National Environmental and Social Safeguards, an Assessment of the environmental and social impacts of the Project was undertaken as part of the ESIA. The project has been designed to avoid or mitigate adverse effects on biodiversity, water resources, air quality, land use, human health, and cultural heritage; to prevent significant adverse impacts from occurring; and to manage any adverse consequences that may occur.

205. The project has the potential to cause low to medium environmental and social impacts. In total 38 impacts were identified as potentially significant (17 were rated as high risk, 9 were rated as medium, and the rest are negligible or could not be rated since activities included the implementation of secondary laws not yet developed). A number of these risks have been mitigated through the ESMP.

206. The impacts include low to medium risks due to minor civil works during the construction phase and logging and maintenance of roads during the operations phase and include impacts on wildlife, risk of sedimentation and erosion, and potential for increased fire risk in the vicinity of the project area. Other risks identified as potentially significant are related to the loss of biodiversity and habitat degradation associated with forest harvesting activities that will be undertaken both during construction and operations.

207. Social impacts are mostly due to the application of the new Forest Code through the development of the ESMP, which includes measures such as the ban on cutting trees for fuelwood and timber and impose restrictions on livestock grazing and gathering of Non-Timber Forest Products (NTFP). The main risk for communities concerns the restriction on NTFP collection. This will have a significant impact on the livelihoods of communities that rely heavily on these resources, particularly during the cold winter months.

208. The project does not require any involuntary land acquisition and/or resettlement. It will require land for the construction of 14 Business Service Units (BSUs). These BSUs will be constructed in land belonging to the state and private sector. No compensation or relocation is required as these units are planned to be built on land that will be used under voluntary agreements. Where a voluntary agreement cannot be established, the land will not be used.

209. Prior to undertaking any of the project's interventions, additional stakeholder engagement will be conducted to ensure that the local population is fully consulted to ensure the project will not impact them and/or their livelihoods; collect information on the social and environmental impacts of the project; and identify opportunities for ESMP Plans will be one of the key activities of stakeholder engagement and strongly supported in Activity 3.1 and 3.2. Assessment of the potential social and environmental impacts is an integral part of the ESIA and ESMP, which will be undertaken through voluntary agreements.

210. Capacity building of MAFA, NFA and DES has been proposed as a tool to manage the project's impacts. This includes training on conflict management; mediation and dispute resolution; II) communication and engagement with communities; III) occupational health and safety (a cross-cutting theme that will be integrated throughout the project); IV) biodiversity conservation and sustainable forest management, including the creation of natural ecosystems and areas for strengthened biodiversity awareness. The objective is to build institutions/capacities for dialogue and conflict resolution, as well as capacity in communication capacities within the three districts to build inclusive sustainable development.

211. Other mitigation measures that have been proposed include project options have been proposed in ESMP, which will reduce the potential impacts of the project to an acceptable level (Annex 6b).

212. Annex 7a "Stakeholder Consultation and Engagement Plan and Grievance Mechanism Report" summarizes the process used for stakeholder engagement during the preparation of the ESIA and ESMP. It includes all feedbacks and discuses recommendations and concerns, the environmental and social impacts, the preliminary assessment of risks and mitigation measures to be implemented in order to avoid or minimize adverse impacts. All in all, 364 people have been consulted in 25 working meetings and 2 workshops including:

- Regional, local and community consultations in the three selected project regions and 9 target districts

# Stakeholder Engagement

## General assessment of gender equality

### The identification and analysis of the Gender Strategy (GS) and the Gender Action Plan (GAP)

#### Identification of the GS and GAP

The process to develop these documents has been guided by the GCF Gender Policy, which provides a framework for the development of the GS and GAP documentation, including regulatory documentation and the standards that the project will need to abide by. The process involved: a) Analysis of the gender equality situation in Georgia, including the country context and the three target regions and the preparation of the stakeholder engagement document; b) Analysis of primary data on the type of firewood they are made of, give much warmth! Poor households are also vulnerable as regards their ability to access alternative fuel sources. The project will make a particular emphasis in this particular priority for women-headed households, which comprise 33% of the total rural areas.

#### Based on conducted assessment

The following key recommendations were made to address the identified opportunities and challenges related to gender equality from the project:

- Strengthening gender competencies in partner structures
- Establishing a gender focal point within the project
- Including women as key actors and change agents in planning and decision-making processes; e.g. by promoting women cooperatives in the supply chain of raw materials for alternative fuel production.
- Improving access to information, training, and technical advisory services for EEAF solutions and financing schemes that specifically focus on vulnerable households (women-headed households) and their ability to access alternative fuel sources
- Ensure gender mainstreaming in information campaigns, organized and led by women's organization, to increase the awareness of the project among the community on the key role women play for the successful implementation of EE-AF solutions.
- Support the collection of gender-disaggregated data

### As a result of stakeholder consultation and conducted analysis

The following key gender-related results of the consultations were identified:

- Minimum 30% of participants in the FMP and business plan development consultative consultations are women
- An equal number of men and women from each forest region are invited to stakeholder consultations on forest related topics
- Women's organizations are included in the target regions state that they have easy access to fuelwood through the new system
- 50% or more of trainings, training materials at the knowledge management and information platform, supported by the project are gender sensitive
- The FMS has integrated gender-disaggregated data collection options in its concept
- At least 25% of beneficiaries of the voucher programme are women-headed households

* **25% of the households visited by the technical advisors of the local information centers are female-headed.**
In order to ensure that the GAF is implemented and expected gender-related results are achieved, regularly monitored and evaluated, gender mainstreaming and monitoring training will be conducted for all GAF and Project implementation staff and representatives of PMA, DES, ECA, ANDA, GIZ and responsibilities assigned accordingly.

### G.3. Financial management and reporting

The financial management of the project will follow GIZ’s internal rules and regulations.
GIZ has bank accounts with Deutsche Bundesbank und Commerzbank. GIZ will not open a specific bank account for each project. Instead, it will use its existing bank accounts to manage all projects. In addition, GIZ will set up other funds by setting up separate cost units exclusively for the funds disbursed by the GCF for each funded activity. The funds will be managed in accordance with the rules and regulations of the German government according to generally accepted accounting principles and procedures accepted by the German government.
As a general rule, all project-related financial transactions must be recorded in the books of account of the executing entity. In case of procurement, GIZ will follow its own procurement guidelines. GIZ is required to comply with the German Act on Public Procurement (GWP), the German Regulation on the Award of Public Contracts (GWV) and, if applicable, the Contracting Rules for the Award of Public Service Contracts (VOB und UVK) when providing services, construction work, and other goods.
When awarding contracts for supplies and services (including consultancy services) to be financed in full or in part from project funds, GIZ will follow its own procurement guidelines. An exception is made if the German Act on Public Procurement and/or any applicable rules of law apply.

The Procedure Guidelines shall not contravene the applicable national procurement law and/or regulations for public procurement, which apply in the executing Entities country.
In principle, the regulations of the executing entities will be followed with regard to the awarding of contracts. However, GIZ may deviate from these minimum standards. While implementing a project with public funds the executing entities should take reasonable account of the interests of all stakeholders as well as ecological and social aspects.

### G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal

No confidential information.
The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.
If the accredited entity discloses a funding proposal, including its annexes, to third parties other than the GCF, it must provide the GCF with a full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with an explanation of why such disclosure was made and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accredited entity’s disclosure policy.
The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.

**Footnote:**
The average number of women-headed households in Germany is 20%. However, the share in urban areas is higher than in rural areas. The awareness activities target rural areas; therefore a conservative approach is taken here.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP132

## Proposal name: Enabling Implementation of Forest Sector Reform in Georgia to Reduce GHG Emissions from Forest Degradation

### Accredited entity: Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit GmbH (GIZ)

### Country/ies: Georgia

### Project/programme size: Medium

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| The project aims to achieve a large volume of emission reductions (5.3 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent over 20 years) by implementing sustainable forest management practices, improving the efficiency and improvement of forest and rural energy value chains, leading to the provision of heating fuels for communities. | The project requires the coordination of a number of government agencies, local authorities, and partnerships with the private sector, which makes implementation more complex than usual. |
| The project aims to set up an innovative financial and technical support facility that integrates the forestry products supply chain and energy value chains in rural communities, tailored to the local barriers to the use of wood and the potential usage of forests by communities. | Coordination with GCF is required with a grant from the Swiss Agency for Development and Cooperation, still requires further due diligence on the project’s capacity for change perspective, it helps strengthen the project’s sustainability through improving community involvement and transparency in the implementation of forest sector reforms, while supporting formal livelihoods linked to forest value chains, including providing skills and vocational training. While not fully developed and requiring further due diligence, there was merit in considering inclusion by the proposal due to its strong linkage with the rest of the portfolio. |


---
**Page 103**
---

The project aims to leverage financing from the banking sector and reinvest revenue from the National Forestry Agency with a total investment of over EUR 140 million over the next five years.

5. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the respective term sheet and addendum XVIII, titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations".

## Summary of the Secretary's assessment

### Project background and climate rationale

- Georgia is an Eastern European country with extensive forests that play a prominent role in the absorption of carbon dioxide. However, the absorption capacity in the land use, land-use change, forestry (LULUCF) sector has been declining since 1990 due to deforestation, principally, of the demand for fuelwood from the rural population. Up to 90 per cent of rural households still rely on firewood as their primary source of energy and this is significantly heating during Georgia's cold winters. This leads to forest degradation and loss of carbon absorption capacity, which is projected to decrease between 1998 and 2030.
- The project aims at increasing the contribution of the LULUCF sector to Georgia’s national contribution (NDC), aiming to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 6 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent over a period of five years. This will be achieved through the restoration of 15,000 hectares of state-managed forest lands.

- The proposed by the German Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) aims to complement Georgia’s NDC and the Forestry Sector Reform initiated in 2013, in 270,807 hectares of forest in three target regions: Guria, Kakheti and Mtskheta-Mtianeti. The long-term goal is to integrate these state-managed forests into the system.

- The implementation of successful SFM is a complex process requiring the coordination and support of public sector institutions, the private sector, and the rural population as historical users of forests for the fulfillment of their energy needs. The accredited entity (AE), GIZ has identified several challenges including outdated forest management practices, inadequate inventories and databases, and inadequate monitoring and financing for the forestry sector.

- Furthermore, GIZ has also documented that there is strong potential in energy savings in the rehabilitation of existing state-managed forests through the use of modern, low upfront cost, for energy-efficient ones, which last longer and consume approximately half the fuel. But this potential remains untapped due to a lack of information on the characteristics of the stoves to make informed choices about their purchase or replacement. This is further compounded by the ability to raise capital, implement quality standards or access certification. Prospective producers are also not aware of the market for energy-efficient and alternative fuels.

- A regulatory framework is also needed to provide an enabling environment for the development of the market.

- The proposed by GIZ presents two pronged approach to tackle the issue: component 1 will support the establishment of a nation-wide SFM system, while component 2 will address the main challenge in the rural population through the development of a market for energy-efficient and alternative fuels. Component 3 will contribute to the sustainability of the project by addressing potential adverse

### Component-by-component analysis

#### Component 1: Sustainable forest management (total cost: EUR 138,995,825; GCF cost: EUR 22,045,092 or ~16 per cent)
- Activity 1.1 will cover the bulk of funding and will contribute directly to the implementation of the forestry Sector Reform through the development and implementation of eight forestry management plans (FMPs) and the establishment of an enabling environment addressing institutional coordination and capacity building.
- Activity 1.2 will support the development of a national forest policy, including the preparation of specialized equipment capable of reaching remote areas to collect evidence of illegal activities. This is critically important in rural settings where the majority of firewood and forest products have been harvested for centuries.
- Activity 1.3 will support the creation of the infrastructure that will enable NFA to sustainably exploit the forests in accordance with the FMPs, generating revenues to finance the maintenance of the FPA network. This activity includes the development and operation of fourteen business service yards, including construction, purchase of equipment, and staffing.
- Activity 1.4 will provide guidelines, operating procedures, and training to optimize the use of equipment at the service yards.


---
**Page 105**
---

processing of timber as the key revenue-generating mechanism for NFAs and EU establish a mechanism to provide quality fuelwood to the population to address their energy needs.

**Component 2: Market development for energy efficiency and alternative fuels (total cost: EUR 28,337.542; GCF cost: EUR 8,660.69% or 30 per cent)**

1. The project will support the Government of Georgia in its shift to rural energy access in Georgia by moving from inefficient stoves and low-quality fuelwood, obtained from highly informal markets, to a market with efficient stoves, reliable supply of quality fuelwood and the provision of technical assistance.

2. The project will work on both the supply and demand side for energy efficiency and alternative fuels (EEAF) products in Georgia. This will be achieved through the creation of the Technical Assistance and Investment Support Facility (TAISF) within the Agricultural and Rural Development Agency (ARDA). The Technical Assistance and Investment Support Facility (TAISF) will:

(a) The improvement of technical, managerial and financial capacities of small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) in the energy efficiency and alternative fuels supply chain through a training package and direct advisory services.

(b) The certification of products in line with international standards, addressing critical information needs for consumers and allowing the market to shift away from sub-standard products:

(c) Facilitation of access for energy efficiency and alternative fuels suppliers to financing through the creation of an investment support scheme via ARDA partner banks and to access ARDA investment support instruments.

(d) Marketing support, including the development of a marketing strategy and product branding, identification of potential institutional customers and the publication of a catalogue.

(e) Investment support in the energy efficiency and alternative fuels supply chain, with publicly-funded financial instruments to enable energy efficiency and alternative fuels suppliers to access financing. The investment support scheme for green energy products/equipment suppliers would also be supported by technical assistance from the Swedish International Development Agency (SIDA).

3. Also on the supply side, the project identifies the scope for innovation in the development of new technologies and business models that could meet rural households for which a market does not currently exist, such as central heating installation, solar water heating systems or biogas plants.

4. The promotion of energy efficiency and alternative fuel solutions would be conducted through a sub-activity financed by the German Federal Ministry for Economic Cooperation and Development to identify such opportunities.

5. On the demand side, the project includes activities to implement consumer financing instruments that will enable rural households to access EEAF products and technical services solutions, thus addressing the key financial and informational barriers identified.

6. Access to finance is critical to generate demand for energy efficiency and alternative fuel solutions in Georgia. The majority of rural households are small farmers who represent a very substantial portion of the income of rural households and typically do not qualify for access to loan-based financing. The project will support consumers adopting EEAF products and alternative fuels with two interlinked financial instruments (see below), which would


---
**Page 106**
---

# together stimulate demand for up to 30,000 users cumulatively until project end with about 28,000 households switching to alternative fuels:

(a) A results-based financial incentive instrument financed by GCF (EUR 3.6 million) in the form of a price discount (via a voucher scheme) on the purchase of an energy-efficient stove or water heater for up to 70 per cent of the market price, which will be managed by ARDA; and

(b) A consumer loan instrument, financed by local banks and micro-finance institutions (EUR 1.5 million), providing loans at interest rates ranging from 3-6% to 70 per cent of the costs of the energy-efficient stove and alternative fuels (e.g., upgraded solid fuel stoves). The loan will be managed by ARDA in collaboration with local financial institutions and partners both to structure and promote consumer financing products.

### Due to the complex nature of the forestry reform, the international gaps hindering consistent implementation of forest management practices, and the importance of generating behavioural change in the management of forests by communities, the project focuses on three components: (a) awareness raising and dissemination of information about locally available energy efficiency and alternative fuel solutions; (b) outreach and awareness-raising activities to promote the use of energy-efficient stoves among households participating in forest management planning and implementation, the impact on fuelwood availability, price and availability of energy efficiency and alternative fuel alternatives, and the new opportunities for income generation through the development of value chains and solutions; and (c) supporting local communities in developing their capacity to identify and promote optimal energy efficiency and alternative fuel solutions.

### Supporting the intervention above, this component will also develop the regulatory environment with the introduction and enforcement of energy-efficiency and environmental standards for stoves and water heaters, the promotion of locally developed solutions, and the introduction of energy-efficient production practices in the public sector.

#### Component 3: Livelihood opportunities and local self-governance in forest management (total cost: EUR 4.5 million)

This component, which is not fully defined and is dependent on the confirmation from the implementing agency, will be developed based on the proposal by enabling communities to better adapt to the changes introduced by the Forestry Sector Reform (for example, the loss of livelihoods related to the informal exploitation of forest resources). The proposed activities under this component are aligned with the Forestry Sector Reforms focus on economic benefit-sharing by communities. The component would help communities in developing their capacity for sustainable forest management and conservation, introducing participation and interest protection mechanisms in order to ensure that they can continue to derive benefits from forests while also supporting developing professional skills and knowledge for forest management and conservation, and supporting the development of alternative income sources to formal livelihood options available in various forest-related value chains.

While the component is not fully defined and requires further due diligence, and despite not being included in the project proposal, it is proposed that GCF will develop a separate presentation after approval and prior to the effectiveness of the funded activity agreement (FAA).

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

- The project has significant impact potential by contributing to the reduction of 16.1 million tCO2e over the project lifetime through its focus on implementing SFM and promoting energy-efficient stoves and alternatives here.
- The project addresses the key driver of deforestation in the country, which is the conversion of forest land into agricultural land.
- The project targets forest restoration and the implementation of SFM on 270,807 hectares across three components. This will be achieved by building capacity for sustainable forest management built up by the project until the end of its implementation, playing a crucial role in scaling up SFM to other regions.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

- The project is expected to have an innovative combination of supporting the implementation of SFM in accordance with the newly adopted forest code and co-financing through GEF.
- The opportunity for the forest sector, forest-dependent communities and innovative solutions in the energy-efficiency and alternative fuels sectors to establish a solid platform for the future and for upscaling the project.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

- GREF is a long-term solution that will be able to change and adapt itself overtime to meet the needs of the communities, forest-dependent communities and innovative solutions.
- Furthermore, GREF is unique due to its very adaptive approach, which can easily be replicated in other regions. The project also has a strong focus on local communities who are dedicated to meet local demands and challenges. GREF is also a key part of the exit strategy of the project, as it will continue to build up the skills needed for the implementation of SFM investments.

#### 3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

- The project has high sustainable development potential due to its main focus and its combination of forest interventions, which promote sustainable investment with communities at the centre.
- The environmental co-benefits are achieved through healthier and better

## Needs of the recipient

### Scale: Medium

25. The forest-energy-nexus in Georgia, with its current challenges of unsustainable harvesting and use of fuelwood, poses a significant problem for Georgia to solve. The project targets the rural areas of Georgia that are still heavily dependent on fuelwood as their primary source of energy.

26. The communities in the target region are considered some of the poorest and most vulnerable in Georgia’s countryside; not to mention “energy poor” with no access to other sources of energy than fuelwood.

### Scale: High

35. Country ownership is considered to be high due to key national targets and priorities set on the forest and energy sector. The project is fully aligned with the NDC objective for the forestry sector, which aims at increasing the share of renewable energy in total final energy consumption by 2030.

### Scale: High

36. Efficiency and effectiveness

37. The rural areas of Georgia still rely nearly 100% per capita on fuelwood for meeting the daily energy demands for heating and cooking. There are no alternatives at present that are cheaper or more efficient than fuelwood, which is likely to remain a primary source into the future.

38. There is a strong need for public investments to help Georgia identify models such as those presented in this project as a means of reducing the reliance on fuelwood.

39. The project presents a high level of efficiency and effectiveness. The cost per ton of GHG reductions is very low, at EUR 20.48 of GCF funding per tCO2eq reduced, benefiting from major impacts in terms of avoided deforestation and land degradation. The project has a high co-financing ratio of 4:1.6, and offers a unique opportunity to leverage significant public and private sector co-funding.

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards


---
**Page 109**
---

The project has classified the environmental and social risk category of the project as medium (category B), with risks and/or impacts that are potentially rare or limited to local occurrences, have largely reversible consequences, and are easy to manage. The Secretariat continues to monitor the project implementation closely in order to ensure that it is within the environmental and social risk accreditation level of the AE. It has prepared a detailed environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA), as well as an action plan and social management plan, which have identified the potential positive and adverse impacts of the project as well as provided for mitigation measures on the negative impacts. While none of the activities are expected to result in involuntary land acquisition or displacement under national regulations, procedures for identifying and screening environmental and social issues will be undertaken during project implementation.

The project is not expected to require any involuntary land acquisitions or negative resettlement impacts, including voluntary resettlement or a negative change to existing tenure rights. It will, however, require land for the construction of the hydropower plant and associated infrastructure, as well as for the establishment of abandoned brownfield sites through voluntary agreements. The introduction of selected forest-related value chains is expected to provide new employment opportunities that are thoroughly assessed, and management measures have to be put in place. The promotion of forest-related value chains will also require the development of appropriate social and environmental measures to ensure sound labor and working conditions. As the project also provides a subsidy to suppliers of certified energy-efficient stoves, the ESIA helps make sure that the suppliers adhere to relevant national regulations and safeguard requirements.

The project is expected to have positive impacts on local communities as sources of income having formal livelihood options in various forest-related value chains could create economic displacement and thus requires support. The project's intervention to strengthen capacities of community-based organizations (CBOs) for sustainable forest management, including best practices, also serves to mitigate potential adverse social aspects of the activities. The project will however require land acquisition and resettlement of indigenous peoples, including ethnic groups, as well as any activities in watersheds or land that is likely to contribute to biodiversity loss.

The project identified the presence of rural populations/communities dependent on forests as well as the presence of transient populations (cattle herders). It also highlighted the potential for negative social impacts from the introduction of new forest-related value chains with reduced opportunity to collect firewood, decreasing pastures for grazing and the introduction of new livelihood activities. The ESIA will include a detailed resettlement plan, including the identification of locations where grazing and gathering of NTFP will be allowed in order to avoid conflicts between permanent and transient forest-dependent populations (e.g., cattle herders) are included in the consultation process. The project will exclude activities that result in significant damage or loss to cultural property, including sites with archaeological significance, as well as those that have a negative impact on natural values.

The ESIA also includes institutional arrangements for project implementation with a focus on who will carry out specific functions relating to environmental and social safeguards in project implementation. This includes defining the roles and responsibilities of project staff and associated institutions, as well as providing guidance on how to conduct an environmental review, preparation and implementation of safeguard instruments, and monitoring and evaluation procedures. The ESIA also provides for capacity building activities aimed at enhancing capacity to implement environmental and social governance standards. The project aims to provide

# Gender policy

The AF has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan, and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy.

### Legal framework for women's empowerment in Georgia

In addition to the Constitution, which guarantees equal rights on the existence of an enabling environment to address gender issues and women’s empowerment in the Constitution of Georgia and the country’s adoption of a number of laws and regulations. Georgia is also a signatory to several international conventions that promote gender equality, including the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women. It has adopted institutional mechanisms for the protection of women's rights and the elimination of violence against women. However, although the legal mechanisms are provided for, there are challenges to ensuring their effective implementation.

### Gender perceptions in Georgia

Gender perception points to low gender parity. The Law on Gender Equality (Article 10) ensures equal opportunities between men and women in all spheres of life. Women have access to customary law and favour men in ownership rights of parental house and land plot. Georgia has adopted the National Action Plan for the Elimination of Violence against Women and the Victims of Domestic Violence, however, domestic violence is high in the localities where the project to be implemented, with women and men showing high degrees of tolerance and perceptions that violence is justified under certain circumstances. The prevalence of early marriage also contributes towards increased exposure to domestic violence.

### Gender equality in decision-making

The gender equality review as well as consultations with communities and non-governmental organization representations has indicated that there are inherent gender inequalities to the detriment of women and women-led households, which limits their participation in family decisions making positions. Despite the existence of a positive attitude of the population towards female education, women still face barriers when it comes to accessing formal education opportunities as well, where the forestry sector is dominated by male employees, with men in the majority having access to better wages than women. Women also have limited access to credit and loans for business purposes. In addition, women are less likely to be involved in important decisions being made. In addition to women’s participation in decision-making being limited outside the home, the same is reflected within the home. Women exercise less power in family decision making positions as well as in household management and ownership of dwellings.

# 4.3 Risks

## 4.3.1 Overall proposal assessment (medium risk)

### 46. GCF is requested to provide a grant of EUR 32.7 million to support the establishment of a nationwide SFM system and the promotion of energy-efficient heating stoves and alternative fuels. The project aims to reduce deforestation, improve forest management practices, and unassailable fuelwood consumption. The project aims to achieve co-financing from multiple sources including the Global Environment Facility (GEF), the European Union (EU) Climate Action and Development, SIDA and the Swiss Agency for Development and Cooperation (SDC) [to be confirmed]. The project will also include a component on debt relief, with the repayment of arrears and outstanding debt from Crystal of EUR 10 million; and grant, in-kind and guarantees from the Government of Georgia of EUR 110 million.


---
**Page 112**
---

### 4.2.2 Accredited entity/executing entity capable to execute the current programme (medium risk)

* OIG of AN and one of the co-executing entities managing GCF resources amounting to EUR 11.7 million.
* The project has been working in Georgia since 1992, and its country office manages regional programmes covering Armenia, Azerbaijan and Georgia.
* There are three other EEs for this project: namely AMDA, the Department of Environment Supervision and the EEB. These entities have a strong relationship with AN and GCF. Based on the assessment, the project has been designed to address identified gaps such as the number of staff in AN and its capacity to manage the programme.

### 4.2.3 Program specific execution risks (high risk)

* **Revenue shortfall risks:** The implementation of the FMP will lead to the production of timber and harvest farming. It is expected that the sales proceeds of these outputs will finance the project activities. However, there are several factors which could contribute to lower volume and/or prices.
* The AE has indicated that it is believed to be in line with the projection, and impacts on revenues would therefore be minor. However, with regard to market conditions, if the production of timber and harvest farming were to decline, revenue would lead to lower revenues. Therefore, it may prolong the maintenance of equipment or postpone the start-up of the project.
* **AE oversight for revenue:** The estimated revenue of EUR 84.7 million has been included as co-financing from the NFA, and it will be re-invested into the upscaling and operations and maintenance costs of the project. This is in line with the requirements set out by GCF under the new forestry sector legislation of the Government of Georgia, and NFA has the financial independence to make such decisions as per its mandate.
* While the AE did not provide clarifications on its oversight mechanism for the refow of forest revenue, it is expected that the AE will be responsible for ensuring that there is no leakage during implementation and that the term sheet includes a clause for the plough back of revenues.

* **Complex implementation arrangements and risk of underperformance:** Complex implementation arrangements and co-financing directly linked to project performance would reduce the likelihood of achieving the desired results. The current financing mechanism: Parts of component 1 will be implemented via a performance-based grant mechanism, with the AE required to provide a minimum 50 per cent equity contribution for equipment and forest/road construction, and another 50 per cent will be provided by the government (NFA). There will be performance assessments in years 1, 3, and 6 to consider the microeconomic impact of the project. The AE has indicated that there is no requirement to provide a minimum 10 per cent equity contribution from potential energy-efficient equipment or forest/road construction projects under this component until commissioning, as it cannot be confirmed because it is not yet known how many potential suppliers will apply for the tender. This could lead to delays in project implementation and the second disbursement of GCF financing.
* The close monitoring of the AE is required for each activity to ensure that the co-financing matures on time and achieve the planned performance.

10. In government approval. The approval of policies and regulations is linked to political processes within the country and it is beyond project control. Delay in approval of the forest code, FMPs and key regulations may adversely impact the project and slow down project implementation.

52. Financial and economic analyses. The AE has provided financial and economic analysis for the project. In the absence of a government concessionary loan or grant, consequently, the expected financial internal rate of return (FIRR) of component 1 (SFM) is 34 per cent on average over its lifetime. If the project were to be implemented under a government concessionality, then the expected financial internal rate of return (FIRR) of component 1 (SFM) is 24 per cent on average over its lifetime. The AE has also provided an economic analysis for the project. Assuming that 50 per cent of the capital expenditure of SFM over the first seven years would increase the FIRR to 9.7 per cent, for example, a 2,600 ha forest estate at 41 per cent with windfall potential. However, it is acknowledged due to the high cost of energy-efficient technologies and the lack of competitive markets, GCF grants will be used to incentivize the purchase of energy-efficient stoves in the form of a voucher scheme. The economic analysis results in an economic internal rate of return of 34 per cent assuming the average market price of EUR 24.

### Results monitoring and reporting

#### Logical framework

The logical framework has been designed with relevant details, including reporting on core indicators for mitigation and impact, and outcome and output indicators for the result areas and outputs of the project. The framework also includes a performance measurement framework. Midterm and final targets are specified.

There are four clear result areas and they in turn give appropriate monitoring indicators. These include (a) reduction in emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (REDD+), (b) switch in the fuelwood supply from illegal outtakes to a sustainable level of outtakes by NFA for project progress and prepare monitoring reports, among other items.

Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (in addition to the environmental and social safeguards) will be established as part of the project. The GCF will monitor adherence with the ESF and gender action plan objectives; and v) supporting the coordination of project activities with relevant stakeholders at national level, including government agencies and civil society (see section B.4., para. 145, of the funding proposal). A Project Implementation Unit, with representatives from all partners, will be established to ensure that the spirit of the letter will include to discuss, monitor and promote the best possible synchronization of implementation activities in order to ensure that the project is on track, and monitor project progress and prepare monitoring reports, among other items.

Details on the budget for component 3 are still pending and will be available once SDC funding has been confirmed. ### GCF-level outcomes and impacts are specified in line with the GCF’s pre-set result area guidelines. The following table provides a summary of the core indicators. For illustrative purposes, a change process is explained with anticipated, gradual levels of progress towards the final year of project implementation:

- **MoD**: Increased number of small, medium and large low-emission power suppliers. This outcome is indicated by “MoD”. Number of households and individuals (males and females) that will have access to electricity from renewable energy sources by the seventh year, the final year of project implementation, will be reached.
- **MoF**: Improved management of land or forest areas contributing to emission reductions. This outcome is indicated by “MoF”. Number of hectares of forests under sustainable and effective management that contributes to CO2 emission reduction. It is forecasted that 270,816 ha of forests will have been managed in accordance with the project agreement and approved by MOEPA by year seven, the final year of project implementation.
- **MoA**: Anticipated impacts are:
    - **MoA1**: Reduced emissions from land-use, deforestation, reduced deforestation, SFM, and conservation of enhancement of forest carbon stocks. This impact is indicated by “MoA1”. Tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2eq) reduced or avoided (including increased sequestration) per year. The total cumulative emission reduction by the end of year seven will be 5.3 million tCO2eq.
    - **MoA2**: Core indicators are estimated, computed on the basis of forest impacts.

Core indicators:

- **E.2.1**: Expected tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (tCO2eq) to be reduced or avoided. An average reduction in emissions per year will be 0.7 million tCO2eq and will be abated over the 20 years of the expected lifetime of the project.
- **E.2.2**: Estimated cost per tCO2eq, defined as total investment cost/experted lifetime emission reduction. The estimated cost per tCO2eq removed is EUR 234 and total cost per tCO2eq reduced is EUR 11,086.
- **E.2.3**: Expected volume of finance to be leveraged by the proposed project/programme as a result of the implementation of the project/programme from other sources. Expected total leverage ratio is 5:6.
- **E.2.4**: Expected number of direct and indirect beneficiaries, disaggregated by sex. It is estimated that by the end of year seven, a total of 983,137 beneficiaries, of which 51,135 are women, will have received the project-funded financial incentive for an energy-efficient stove or alternative fuel. The number of direct and indirect beneficiaries receiving information and advisory support through the project: 520,000 of them female; and male indirect beneficiaries will account for 18 per cent of the total population, and female indirect beneficiaries will account for 14 per cent of the total population.
- **E.2.5**: Number of beneficiaries relative to total population, disaggregated by sex. It is estimated that direct and indirect beneficiaries receiving information and advisory support through the project: male direct beneficiaries will account for 30 per cent of the total population; female direct beneficiaries will account for 18 per cent of the total population; male indirect beneficiaries will account for 13 per cent of the total population, and female indirect beneficiaries will account for 14 per cent of the total population.

Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP132

Proposal name: Enabling Implementation of Forest Sector Reform in Georgia to Reduce GHG Emissions from Forest Degradation

Accredited entity: Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit GmbH (GIZ)

Country/(ies): Georgia

Project/programme size: Medium


## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Impact potential Scale: High

Based on the submitted funding proposal for the proposed project and answers thereto, which were provided by the Ministry of Environment Protection and Natural Resources (MEPNR) of Georgia, it is concluded that Georgia is committed to contributing towards international efforts to address climate change.

The country has recognized the important role its forest resources can play in delivering mitigation benefits. The business as usual scenario shows that national GHG emissions will continue to increase over time. In order to meet their nationally determined contribution (NDC), Georgia recognizes its forest sector as a primary source of GHG emission reductions and has committed itself to deliver mitigation benefits from the forest sector.

The forest sector can deliver an estimated 6 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (MtCO2eq) of GHG emissions by 2030. This is based on the assumption that about 15% of Georgia’s total land area, or about 250,000 hectares (ha) of State-managed forest land over the period 2020-2030. Given the potential of the forest sector of Georgia to deliver mitigation benefits, the project idea on which this funding proposal is based is considered highly relevant.

The funding proposal clearly explains that there is evidence that the land use and land-use change in Georgia’s forests are driven by rural households’ need for firewood primarily due to unsustainable harvesting by rural households as a result of the need to meet their ever-increasing energy demand. It is reported that about 70 per cent of rural households in Georgia depend on firewood for cooking, heating and lighting. Their unsuitable sourcing of firewood from the forests of Georgia has been strongly attributed to the degree of poverty among these rural households.

The funding proposal further identifies that the poor management practices identified as a further cause of forest degradation, the funding proposal emphasizes that the project idea will address this issue by promoting sustainable forest management in Georgia’s forests. Sustainable forest management, which will be at the core of the proposed project framework to deliver mitigation benefits, involves the management of forests so that their benefits are sustained over time and that the needs of present generations for food security, are optimized to meet the needs of society in a way that conserves and maintains forest ecosystems.

The funding proposal further explains that Georgia has experienced significant degradation of forests in Georgia and the need to reverse that trend. The Government of Georgia established the Technical Forest Service Agency (TFSRA) in 2013 with the mandate of TFSRA to place all of forest land under an effective sustainable forest management programme.

The Government supports this proposed project submission to GCF as it will enable it to have a


---
**Page 118**
---

### Sustainable Forest Management Framework

#### Introduction
4. Aside from the sustainable forest management framework, which will help to reverse the observed degradation of forests in the country, and the role that unsustainable harvesting of wood from these forests plays in this degradation, the project document also recognizes the fact that efficient use of fuelwood can contribute to the restoration of degraded forests by increasing the availability of high-quality fuelwood and reducing forest biomass. The project concept also takes into consideration the fact that, although wood harvesting is a major source of income for many people living near these forests, it is not the only livelihood activity available in those areas.

#### Project Components
The above-mentioned considerations formed the focus of the concept of the proposed project, which was reviewed by the independent TAP. According to the information provided in the funding proposal and annexes thereto, the project has been developed around three major components that are designed to address these issues and contribute towards achieving the project's objectives.

The three components can be summarized as follows:

- **Component 1: Introducing a nationwide sustainable forest management system that will cover all forest districts.**
    - Establishing the nationwide main building blocks for sustainable forest management in Georgia with the following characteristics:
        - The institutional, legal and regulatory environment needed for the successful launch and operationalization of sustainable forest management across the entire country.
        - The necessary knowledge, data and information system; and
        - Appropriate human and institutional capacities.

- **Component 2: Implementing sustainable forest management facilities in the following target regions of Georgia:**
    - Kakheti;
    - Mtsheta-Mtianeti; and
    - Guria;

- **Component 3: Putting in place a robust business model for sustainable forest management.**


---
**Page 119**
---

# Component 2: Promoting market development for energy efficiency and alternative fuels

This component will be delivered through activities that will address the main driver of Georgia's forest degradation, namely unsustainable fuelwood consumption by the rural population. The key pathway to tackling this driver is by accelerating the growth of energy efficiency (EE) and alternative fuels (AF) markets in Georgia. This market development will provide a steady supply of energy to rural consumers who, in the absence of the project, will continue to use fuelwood to meet their energy demands.

The activities that will be implemented as part of this component are:

(a) Accelerating the EE and AF supply chain.
(b) Catalyzing the demand for EE and AF activities through consumer awareness-raising and promotion of financial incentives.
(c) Creating a conducive policy environment and investment framework for the EE sector in partnership with EE-AF suppliers, the financial sector and the government framework for the EE market.
(d) Addressing demand-side, supply-side and policy barriers to the EE/AF market. The barriers that will be mitigated by this project activity as part of the component 2 activities are:

(i) Underdeveloped markets for EE and AF technologies;
(ii) Affordability of EE and AF access to finance, especially for poor households;
(iii) Low consumer awareness about technology and fuel alternatives and a relatively low demand for EE/AF products.

(iv) Absence of an enabling policy and regulatory framework

### Component 3: Promoting livelihood opportunities and local self-governance in forest management

It is the view of the independent TAP, after reviewing the documents submitted for this project, that the activities proposed under component 1 are not sufficient to ensure that sustainable forest management measures will be implemented and who will be the key recipient of these benefits. To address this issue, it was recommended that the activities of component 2 may be out of sync with these developments. To carry on the activities in a manner that is consistent with the new developments and to cater for the new sustainable forest management, component 3 was designed and proposed. The focus of the component 3 activities will cover the people, institutions and policies that will be directly impacted by the project.

Key issues that can become clear barriers to success of the component 1 and 2 activities, which the activities of component 3 will attempt to mitigate, include:

(a) Absence of a conducive policy environment for local authorities to participate in sustainable forest management and conservation;
(b) Insufficient participation and representation of local stakeholders in forest-management-related decision-making.
(c) Lack of qualified personnel and insufficient education opportunities in the forest sector.

12. The potential mitigation benefits and co-benefits that will be delivered by this project include the following:

The project will involve the implementation of sustainable forest management in Georgia. Direct implementation of these management methods will range from better supervision of the forest restoration process and protection of the restored forest areas to the introduction of new techniques for the reforestation, afforestation, and reforestation and implementation of sustainable forest management on 270,807 ha across the three levels of forest management. The project will also have indirect action/reverse impacts at the following two levels:

At the first level, the overall planning and better implementation of sustainable forest management are likely to lead to the reduction of the degradation of forests compared to the baseline scenario. This impact level will affect all forest areas, which is estimated to be 105,247 ha (39% of total forest area).

See section 5.2.3 of the feasibility study.

**Page 122**

The second level consists of those forests where direct silvicultural measures will be implemented, which will include the promotion of natural regeneration and enrichment planting that will actively increase carbon stocks over time.

### (a) The Em-Ante Carbon-balance Tool (EmAT) of Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) with tier 2 data quality assurance was used in the determination of the reduction in forest degradation when the sustainable forest management measures were implemented on 270,867 ha of forest in Georgia. It has been estimated that the reduction in GHG emissions from forest degradation will be 13.9 MCO2eq over the project duration and a GHG emission reduction of 54.3 MCO2eq during the 20-year lifetime of the project.

Standweise data were used to determine species and density depth models, which in turn were used for the calculation of forest degradation. The sustainable forest management measures are implemented on the target areas. Forest lands where natural regeneration is expected will be excluded from the area covered by the project within areas clearly identified as part of the multifunctional zoning, and will therefore include all protective function areas or areas eligible due to any other reason (e.g., those that are not eligible for forest management). The sustainable forest management measures will be gradually delivered as a result of the sustainable forest management measures coming into effect. It is estimated that the sustainable forest management measures will cover over a forest area of not more than 157,359 ha.

Enhancements in GHG emissions reductions are expected to be achieved through a GHG emission reduction of 3.194 MCO2eq during the seven years of the project duration and 9.125 MCO2eq over the project’s 20-year lifetime period.

The proposed project will result in GHG emission reductions through market development of EE/alternative fuels, especially where these solutions are adopted by rural households. The introduction of high-efficiency stoves and cookers is expected to have a substantial reduction in fuelwood consumption and, consequently, GHG emissions reduction. The estimated GHG emission reduction from the Kyoto Protocol’s Clean Development Mechanism (CDM) small-scale methodologies AGS-LE: Switch from non-renewable biomass for thermal applications by the user (version 9/01) and AGS-LE: Energy efficiency measures in the household sector (version 2/08) and the CDM methodology LOM-10/08 were used to estimate the GHG emission reductions that will result from the introduction of EE/alternative fuels. The estimated GHG emissions reduction is expected to be 35 MCO2eq over the project’s lifetime period.

The sustainable forest management methodologies used in all these calculations as sound tools to deliver reliable estimates of GHG emission reductions. The total mitigation potential of the sustainable forest management measures that have been developed for this project and when implemented on the 270,867 ha of Georgian forest will be about 1.83 MCO2eq by the end of the project (seven years), with an additional reduction in GHG emissions over the project’s 20-year lifetime period. This GHG emission reduction is expected to be achieved through the implementation of this project to avoid double counting.

### (c) The sustainable forest management methodologies used in all these calculations as sound tools to deliver reliable estimates of GHG emission reductions. This GHG emission reduction is expected to be achieved through the implementation of this project to avoid double counting, and

In addition to the project mitigation benefits reported above, the project will deliver direct benefits to about 784,337 beneficiaries who will be the project-funded financial service providers and their clients. These include smallholder farmers and households in the target regions. ### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

#### 1.2.3 Comprehensiveness

In the absence of this project intervention, the forests of Georgia will continue to progressively degrade as the unsustainable sourcing of forestwood from forests continues. Rural households and communities in the target regions will continue to rely on firewood for their energy demand; they will continue to utilize the fuelwood in exceptionally low-efficiency cookstoves. The project intervention will deliver a high climate impact potential by enabling the implementation of forest management measures on the target forest areas, which will, over a period of time, lead to a reversal of the degradation of the target forest areas and catalyze the enhancement of carbon stocks.

Activities in components 1 and 2 of the proposed project will involve key participation from rural households and communities. The project will also engage other stakeholders in Georgia that has the mandate to manage the forestry sector in the country. Without component 3, other forestry sector stakeholders (e.g., rural populations, municipal institutions whose mandate covers forest management, and private sector actors who are involved with forest-based technologies), may not be as involved as they should in the operationalization of components 1 and 2. The project will also engage these stakeholders to ensure that their voices are heard by those stakeholders who would otherwise have been marginalized, thus creating barriers to the success of the project. Component 3 is designed to address this issue. It leverages the experiences gained during components 1 and 2 to help in diversifying livelihood strategies for the poorest households and communities in Georgia. The integration of poverty reduction and project integration will deliver comprehensive mitigation of barriers, which will further contribute to the success of the project and deliver a paradigm shift.

In addition to the above, unless the forests of Georgia continue to degrade, adequate budgeting together with international grants may be inadequate to deliver a reversal of the degradation of these forests. The proposed project intervention, through the grant from GCF by crowding in several other fund sources, enables the sustainable forest management concept to move from an idea closely developed with bilateral and multilateral donor funding into reality. This will not only enable Georgia to meet its national goal of engaging an agricultural advisory service provider but also contribute to the international goal of mitigating climate change through the delivery of real and measurable emission reductions.

The independent TAP believes that the structure of the forest management approach described in the funding proposal and annexes thereto, especially the basic sustainable forest management principles and integration of the project components, will deliver a workable


---
**Page 124**
---

governance framework for the forest sector that will be better than in the status quo ante situation and more structured and will deliver multimodal benefits, including: (i) a reduction of forest degradation, estimated to amount to about 0.81 biomass/ha/year; (ii) enhanced carbon sequestration, estimated at 2.54 tonnes CO2e/ha/year; (iii) elimination of illegal logging, which is estimated to currently comprise 15 per cent of residential fuelwood consumption in Georgia and 30 per cent of industrial fuelwood consumption; and (iv) the introduction of new forest management technologies, which is described in the funding proposal as currently at a nascent stage. These benefits are among the key outputs on the proposed project, which supports the conclusion that the proposed project, if successfully implemented, will deliver a high paradigm shift.

### 3.2.2 Innovation

Innovation is partially delivered when changes are made to the way things are done according to the status quo ante, with value creation and the ultimate creation of a better way of doing things. The funding proposal and annexes thereto present an innovative situation prevailing in the forest sector in Georgia before this proposed intervention was conceptualized. This innovation is based on the following: (i) the identification of a situation that is presented convincingly in the funding proposal and annexes thereto, which is primarily characterized by forest degradation; (ii) the identification of the key outputs of the proposed project, however, likely to (i) decelerate the process of forest degradation in Georgia; (ii) eliminate illegal logging from the forests; (iii) introduce and gradually establish EUC alternatives for fuelwood supply; (iv) create a market for wood products in an export market; and (v) introduce new livelihood opportunities to people living in rural areas, especially women. The funding proposal and annexes thereto present an innovative situation that, if delivered by this project, will deliver innovation. Thus, the proposed project structure is expected to be innovative as it addresses the key challenges of forest degradation prevailing in Georgia, and the way in which rural energy is supplied and utilized at the end-user points in rural areas. The funding proposal and annexes thereto also present an innovative situation for the project concept in other forests and villages in Georgia which are not included in the project target areas, thus delivering improved and expanded quality of the project through learning-by-doing and replication as replicated in other countries, further expanding the paradigm shift capacity of the project.

### 3.2.3 Creation of an enabling environment/sustainability of outcomes

The ability to sustain the sound outcomes of this project will only be engendered if the project is successfully implemented. The funding proposal and annexes thereto supporting the funding proposal and annexes thereto, the independent TAP specifically studied the documentation to identify the key outputs that are expected to be delivered by the proposed project. These built into the project delivery are likely to create such an enabling environment if the project is successfully implemented.

### 3.2.4 A complementary framework for sustainable forest management in Georgia will be created and strengthened

Prior to the start of this project, BMZ funding of the International Forest Policy Project (IFPP) was provided to Georgia. The IFPP has worked on overhauling the existing regulations to align with the new Forest Policy of Georgia. However, the need for further reforms has been built into this project to ensure that all forest management activities in Georgia are aligned with the principles of sustainable forest management to be newly implemented in the country. A functional legal framework will be created and strengthened by the proposed project, which will ensure the sustainability of outcomes delivered by the proposed project. The independent TAP specifically studied the documentation to identify the key outputs for sustainable planning and implementation of this project and its replication in other forests in Georgia will be created by the proposed project;


---
**Page 125**
---

Enhanced capacities of sustainable forest management local stakeholders will be built through:

- The National Agency for Forest Management in Georgia (NAG), which will receive training on techniques and management of public service vehicles.
- Training of staff from NAG to develop a Public Private Partnership (PPP) model, ensuring harvesting levels are based on ecosystem-based forest management principles.

Enhanced sector steering and coordination: through activity 1.4, the project structure will be improved by implementing a Sectoral Coordination and Planning (SCP) process to facilitate improved cross-sectoral cooperation and coordination as well as information exchange between relevant public and private entities with relevance to the forestry sector of Georgia. This will enhance the cross-sectoral flow of information on sustainable forest management practices.

The independent TAP observed that many subactivities under activities 1.1, 1.2, 1.3, 1.4 and 2.4 have been designed to create a conducive policy, regulatory and operational environment for the successful implementation and replication of the proposed project in Georgia. According to the information reviewed by the independent TAP, this will be achieved through:

- The gradual phase-out of inefficient stoves from the market by introducing and facilitating the adoption of mandatory EE and environmental performance standards.
- The creation of an initial strong demand for EE/alternative fuel products through local development and application of energy-efficient public procurement policies.
- The provision of technical assistance to relevant public authorities to accelerate the implementation of EE measures in rural areas, including the use of renewable energy instruments envisaged in the EE and AF, with direct relevance and implications for the EE sector such as the reduction of biomass consumption in rural areas.
- Two projects will be implemented by GIZ (German Agency for International Cooperation), as the responsible executing entity, with support from BMZ for the:
  - Implementation of the EU energy performance standards and labelling scheme for EE heating appliances (Intervis);

Introduction of EU procurement in the public sector; and

The independent TAP concludes that all these activities will contribute to establishing an enabling environment for the success of the proposed project and its replication in other forest areas that are not part of the target areas of this proposed intervention in Georgia.

### 1.2.4 Potential for scaling up and replication

#### a) Potential for scaling up and replication within Georgia

The project proposal has been planned and designed to be implemented in eight target districts (covering about 30% of the territory provided in the funding proposal; the target districts cover 14 per cent of the forests under the management of NFA). This means that there are ample opportunities for scaling up and replication within Georgia, including other areas along with the project area. If the forest management concept is implemented on all forests managed by NFA, there will be about 1.5 million ha of forest remaining where the project, after the success of the "proof of concept" phase described in this document, can be scaled up and replicated. This would include areas managed by other forest management bodies (e.g., the Adjara Forestry Agency, the Agency of Protected Areas, etc.). The project will also contribute to the implementation of the new Forest Code. Therefore, the potential for scaling up and replication of the project concept is very high as it can be implemented in all forests under NFA management, provided that the enabling environment at the national level, when implemented, will require about seven years to build the capacity of relevant forest sector stakeholders (e.g., the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development, etc.) and the enabling environment at the local level. The project will also provide time to build the capacity of relevant forest sector stakeholders in Georgia (NFA, DSEs, etc.) into learning-by-doing and facilitate the scaling up and replication of the project idea to other parts of Georgia.

#### b) Potential for knowledge-sharing and learning

The project design and implementation plan includes a strategy for knowledge-sharing and learning that will act as a basis the related work carried out by the accredited entity (AE) (GIZ). The AE will be responsible for disseminating information and knowledge relevant stakeholders in Georgia. This will be achieved through various activities, including but not limited to implementing this project. The previously developed knowledge base, coupled with the fact that GIZ and other donors were involved in the design of the project concept in conjunction with many of the relevant stakeholders (e.g., the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development [Georgia], has provided a strong base for knowledge dissemination and learning. The dissemination of project concepts will be achieved through various activities including but not limited to implementing this project, including workshops, seminars, training sessions, etc. The AE will also disseminate information to relevant stakeholders (some of whom were part of the project design and planning stage), including the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development, the Agency for Education and Culture [EEC] and the Agricultural and Rural Development Agency (ARDA); and the private sector. The AE will also provide training sessions in the eight target districts on the topics of sustainable forest management, forests and climate change; and other populations with a topic of interest to them. These activities are expected to engage academic institutions; and international organizations (e.g., multilateral organization development cooperation organizations).

23. Subsection 14.3.1 of the proposed project involves the development of an online knowledge management and training platform for the forest sector of Georgia. Training modules and information materials developed as part of the other project activities will be integrated into this platform, which will also include e-learning elements, videos and knowledge storage, and will ultimately strengthen the management capacity of the forest sector in Georgia. The project aims to ensure that those engaged in the forestry sector have adequate knowledge and skills to effectively implement the actions considered necessary for sustainable forest management. This will be achieved through improved knowledge management and dissemination, and through the institutionalization of key sector trainings. The knowledge materials on new forest management developed under previous interventions will also be integrated into this platform. These materials include information on sustainable forest management operations that will be developed as the project matures, as well as the development of a set of guidelines for the implementation of these operations.

It will also deliver comprehensive and innovative attributes that will make forest management more sustainable, while also enhancing replicability of the proof-of-concept to other forests in Georgia. In addition, the project will provide an enabling environment for the development of new attributes for the delivery of a paradigm shift in the forest sector of Georgia.
