### Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs)

- **SDG 3: Achieve gender equality and empower all women and girls.** The sustainable development benefits that the project will deliver under SDG 5 include:
  - Women-headed households constitute 15 per cent of all households in Georgia – over 10,000 women-headed households.
  - By providing alternative livelihoods to these women, their economic burden for water will be decreased as less fuelwood will be needed to source it.

- **SDG 6: Ensure availability and sustainable management of water and sanitation for all.** Sustainable development benefits that the project will deliver under SDG 5 include:
  - The construction of 100 km of irrigation canals in Georgia.
  - These canals are located on hilly and mountainous areas and thus carry out key soil-protecting, water-preserving functions.

- **SDG 7: Ensure access to affordable, reliable, sustainable and modern energy for all.** The sustainable development benefits that the project will deliver under SDG 5 include:
  - Facilities for the production and application of alternative fuels for 30,170 rural households.
  - Promote sustained, inclusive, and sustainable economic growth full and productive employment and decent work for all.

- **SDG 8: Decent work and economic growth.** The sustainable development benefits that the project will deliver under SDG 5 include:
  - The creation of 167 jobs in the forestry sector.
  - Improvement of road infrastructure, transportation and road building and maintaining activities in the project target regions.

- **SDG 9: Industry, innovation and infrastructure.** The sustainable development benefits that the project will deliver under SDG 5 include:
  - Implementation of new forest management practices and operating procedures.
  - Vocational education and training will further strengthen this employment sector.
  - Workers must hold formal qualifications for 2016.
  - Development of the alternative fuels sector will also create new employment opportunities in rural areas; currently, there are no products available on the market. The project will support smallholder market growth for these products.

- **SDG 12: Ensure sustainable consumption and production patterns.** The sustainable development benefits that the project will deliver under SDG 5 include:
  - Conservation of natural resources through the provision of sustainably harvested and dried fuelwood.
  - Through promotion of energy-efficient technologies as alternative fuels to close the fuelwood gap.

- **SDG 13: Take urgent action to combat climate change and its impacts.** The sustainable development benefits that the project will deliver under SDG 5 include:
  - Reduction in greenhouse gas emissions through improved forest management.


---
**Page 129**
---

### Economic co-benefits

The rural population living close to the target forests will benefit the most from the proposed project activities. Most of the economic co-benefits discussed in the funding proposal reviewed by TAP are tangible and can be delivered through successful implementation of the project. The independent TAP believes that the following key co-benefits will be tangibly delivered as a result of the project:

- Successful implementation and adoption of energy-efficient stoves will reduce the quantity of fuelwood required to provide energy for the same level of activities in the household sector. According to the information provided by GCF, this


---
**Page 130**
---

### Social and health co-benefits

In the baseline scenario of this project, combustion of inefficient cookstoves is a source of emissions of particulate matter (PM) 2.5 and carbon monoxide, which are internationally accepted sources of indoor pollution that the World Health Organization has estimated to be responsible for over 3 million premature deaths per year with effects such as respiratory and cardiovascular mortality and morbidity, in particular for children. It has been estimated that the introduction of energy-efficient cookstoves will result in reduced emissions of PM2.5 and carbon monoxide and thus over time will reduce the known serious health effects such as respiratory and cardiovascular mortality and morbidity associated with indoor air pollution.

### Environmental co-benefits

The independent TAP agrees with the view presented in the funding proposal that, if successfully implemented, it will deliver environmental co-benefits through the introduction of energy-efficient cookstoves. The project will reduce deforestation processes and will reduce the degradation of the forests. This will deliver a pathway for reversing forest degradation and improving forest health and biodiversity within the entire area of NTA managed forest land. Through the proposed activities targeting knowledge- and information-sharing, capacity building, and the development of an information management and training platform the project will also strengthen knowledge and information management and dissemination in Georgia’s forests. The independent TAP agrees that this will contribute to a high sustainable-development potential of the project.

### Gender-related co-benefits

A total of 650 jobs will be created at NTA and around 217 jobs at service contractors.


---
**Page 131**
---

30. Women living in rural areas in Georgia are heavily dependent on firewood to meet their domestic energy needs. Therefore, successful implementation and operation of the proposed project will benefit women, who will not only have a more transparent way of sourcing fuelwood but also will be able to reduce their time spent collecting firewood (which has a heavy dependence on illegal sources), but will also need to source only half of the current volume to deliver energy services. The rural population in Georgia is estimated at 5 million, and women constitute 33 per cent of rural households in Georgia; therefore, since it is estimated that over 18000 women are directly benefiting from the project, which will deliver 24 per cent of the power, which will deliver EE/alternative fuel products, the independent TAP concludes that the gender mainstreaming impact of the proposed project is significant.

31. In terms of the economic and financial needs of the recipient country as elements of sustainable development, the independent TAP concludes that the project will generate significant sustainable development potential; the social and environmental benefits of this project is expected to deliver benefits directly to 98,137 households and indirectly to 205,460 other beneficiaries. Therefore, the independent TAP concludes that the sustainable development potential of this proposed project is high.

### 1.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: High

#### 1.4.1 Needs of the country and the rural population

32. It is estimated that the population of Georgia in 2020 is about 3.99 million. Using the information provided by the independent TAP, it can be concluded that there are 5 million people living in rural areas in Georgia; therefore, the total population of Georgia is about 8.99 million, meaning that about 60 per cent of the population of Georgia live in rural areas of the country.

33. In terms of the needs of the recipient country as elements of sustainable development, the independent TAP concludes that over 90 per cent of the rural population (around 1.43 million people) depend on firewood to meet their end-use energy requirements, in particular cooking. Given that the average household size is estimated at four persons and considering the fuelwood commonly used in these rural areas, it is apparent that the project will deliver a need of the recipient country as elements of sustainable development, which is currently not met by the baseline woodstoves, for example. Among the rural population those most impacted by this effect are women, who have the primary responsibility to acquire and prepare firewood by the household.

34. There will also be a significant positive impact on forests from the introduction of a more organized and sustainable sourcing mechanism; will be introduced by this project in conjunction with the current high dependence on illegal sources. In terms of the needs of the country, the proposed project will contribute to reducing deforestation and forest degradation during the sustainable forest management processes, decrease the otherwise accelerating forest degradation, and improve the overall quality of forests that can claim to contribute to the international mitigation of global climate change. The proposed project will also deliver poverty alleviation benefits in recipients in the rural areas where this project is implemented; it is estimated that over 10 per cent of the population living under the funding proposal as averaging slightly above 24 per cent. The proposed project will contribute to reducing deforestation and forest degradation during the sustainable forest management processes, and improved income generation for some of the recipients via alternative livelihood ventures that will be introduced by this project in conjunction with the current high dependence on illegal sourcing conditions due to reduced pollution in living quarters. With these co-benefits, it can be considered that the proposed project will contribute significantly towards poverty reduction and sustainable implementation of this project. Therefore, the proposed project will satisfy the needs of the country, while also delivering various tangible co-benefits to the rural population.

#### 1.4.2 Economic and financial needs of the country


---
**Page 132**
---

10. The economic need of the country that will be met by successful implementation of the proposed project will be the promotion of economic growth in rural areas. Economic growth in rural areas will be fostered by the economic contribution of the proposed project activities to the local economy and the creation of new jobs in rural Georgia. According to information presented in the funding proposal, rural households in Georgia earn on average $100 per month less than urban households. The income levels of rural households in the project target areas will be improved owing to a combination of increased earnings from forest management activities, increased earnings from other sources as a result of the project activities, coupled with savings from reduced expenditures on fuelwood resulting from the project's EV/Alternative fuel schemes and improved living conditions that will be engendered by the project. The overall effect is expected to be an increase in household income through the use of more energy-efficient EV/alternative fuel equipment. These effects will not only contribute to poverty alleviation but also foster sustainable economic development and growth in rural areas beyond the level at the baseline period.

13. Strengthening the local capital market

13.5. The grant from GEF is expected to crowd-in financing from other sources, in addition to grant funding provided by the Government of Georgia. Strengthening the financial market will be essential for changing the baseline situation in which finding of this forest management intervention and other recent relevant interventions has depended on government budgetary allocation. According to information presented in the funding proposal, the Government's financing options are constrained by fiscal consolidation and are expected to be further constrained as a result of the current global economic crisis. The project is expected to crowd-in financing from other sources only for increased spending on education and investments in infrastructure at the expense of expenditures on social services. It should also be noted that the funding provided under this project will crowd in funds from other sources to reduce dependence on government-only funding. Furthermore, it is expected that the financial market will provide additional resources to finance the types of activities that will be implemented in this project and hence strengthen their financing capabilities for future expansion of the project concept to other forests not covered by the forest management intervention.

13.4. Institutional needs

13.4.1. Institutional needs

70. The baseline institutional capacities of both the public and private sectors of Georgia are insufficient to lay the foundation for an enabling environment for sustainable forest management in Georgia. According to information presented in the funding proposal, the performance of the Government's fund from the national budget and grant funding from bilateral and multilateral sources, including GEF, is not satisfactory. The implementation of this project will be essential not only for the success of the project concept but also for expansion to all other forests in the country, according to the aspirational plan of the Government of Georgia. According to information presented in the funding proposal, the institutional capacity for the implementation of this project whose capacity will be strengthened through learning-by-doing, capacity building and technical assistance provided by MoFA, MOPa, NPA, DIES and EEC. The funding proposal states that through the implementation of this project, the institutional capacities of MoFA, MOPa, NPA, DIES and EEC are expected to increase the overall management efficiency and effectiveness of the forest sector in the country. According to information presented in the funding proposal, a key observation is that there has been no systematic review and analysis of each institution's mandate and the adequacy of their capacities, as well as to identify key areas where gaps exist and where improvements are needed. A key observation according to the funding proposal is that there have not been any studies conducted related in relation to best practices for sustainable forest management (e.g., tree-cutting operations, low-impact logging, etc.). The lack of such information has resulted in a number of problems among others. Consequently, it is understood from the information presented in the funding proposal


---
**Page 133**
---

that the project has been designed to comprehensively address identified barriers and gaps, including the institutionalization of training and improved knowledge management. In the opinion of the independent TAP, this will ensure that the institutional needs for the success of the proposed project are met, even though these needs were not currently covered by the proposed project.

The various needs of the country, including the needs of rural populations, especially those who are not direct project recipients of the co-benefits of this project, will be positively promoted by the success of this project, as discussed above. In addition, successful implementation of this project will also have a positive impact on the institutional capacity whereby the proposed intervention will be enhanced as the enabling environment for planning, implementing and monitoring the project. The independent TAP concludes that the success of this intervention. This indicates that the needs of the recipient country, its people and institutions will be fully met by the successful implementation of this project. As a result, the independent TAP concludes that the proposed project has a high potential to meet the needs of the country and its people.

### 1.5 Country ownership Scale: High

#### 1.5.1 Evidence of country ownership through participation of government bodies

The first key evidence of country ownership of this project is the strong participation of the following five key government institutions in the implementation of this project: NFA, MoPA, GIZ, Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development (MARD), and the National Bank (commercial) legal entity of MoPA. It is also important to point out that MoPA is the GCF national focal point for Georgia. The second key evidence of country ownership is that an en-financees of this project has been active in Georgia’s forest sector since 2013, starting with the regional (South Caucasus) HES project, which ended in November 2019 and was funded by BMZ and the GCF. The third key evidence of country ownership is that the implementation of the project commenced in January 2019. Through this work, GIZ has had extensive interactions with all the above mentioned government institutions. This interaction has been crucial for the development of the concept of the proposed project. The design of the proposed project builds on the lessons learned from these interactions and is based on the needs identified by the government bodies involved and complement them. The independent TAP believes that this provides robust evidence of strong support from the Government of Georgia for this proposed project.

#### 1.5.2 Alignment of the project with relevant national strategies

Some of key national plans and policies that this project is aligned can be summarized as follows:

(a) National Climate Strategy: the first national climate strategy of Georgia that the government has adopted in line with its commitments under the Paris Agreement, which was submitted to the UNFCCC on 7 May 2017. According to the NCSC, Georgia is committed to reducing its GHG emissions by 15 per cent below the business as usual scenario by 2030. The conditional reduction target for Georgia in 2030 under the Paris Agreement is a 48 per cent cut of carbon intensity per unit of gross domestic product from 2013 to 2030. Georgia’s NCSC singled out forest sector as one of the key sectors that will contribute significantly towards achieving these targets with quantified unconditional and conditional commitments. Through domestic action and funding, Georgia GCF will deliver emissions reduction of at least 1 MTCO2e over a


---
**Page 134**
---

period of 10 years on 45,000 ha from forest land and, conditional to receiving external support, will also deliver an emissions reduction of up to 6 MtCO2e on 250,000 ha of forest land over the period 2020–2030. Current updates of the NDC and the draft climate change strategy of Georgia are in line with this commitment by increasing selected NDC by increasing the carbon sink capacity of the forest sector by 10 per cent, while the draft climate change strategy sets a target to increase the area under sustainable forest management to 436,000 ha by 2030. According to the information provided in the funding proposal, Georgia has already submitted its NDC commitments which need the submission of this proposed project to GCF for funding. The second national climate change strategy of Georgia with reference to the objectives and targets of the Paris Agreement includes National Adaptation Mitigation Actions (NAMAs), which were also submitted to the UNFCCC in 2019. Key NAMAs that the proposed project will support are:

(a) "Adaptive Sustainable Forest Management in Bjorj-Bakuriani Forest District", which was completed in 2016 and set the foundation for the Government's implementation of sustainable forest management practices in the Bjorj-Bakuriani region and provides the platform for the inclusion of the unconditional contribution to the GCF as a co-financing partner for the proposed project; and

(b) In the NDC document, emphasis is placed on the importance of reforestation and support for implementation of efficient use of biomass for equitable climate-proof and sustainable rural development, as set out in the proposed project.

Alignment with the existing GCF country programme, according to information provided in the funding proposal, Georgia's country programme, which serves as the strategic framework for the GCF operations in Georgia, includes four components: one under mitigation and four for adaptation, including "land use and forests" under mitigation; see "Technical Assistance for Sustainable Land Use and Forest Management". The country programme is strongly aligned with the core strategies included in the proposed project. A component of the proposed project that focuses on sustainable land management in Georgia is included in the Georgia's Country Programme as a cross-cutting action. This cross-cutting measure is also a strategic activity of the proposed project. It can be considered as an important element for the implementation of the proposed project.

### 15.3 Implementation capacity of the accredited entity for the proposed project

GIZ, as GCF AB, will also serve as a co-executing entity for the proposed project. Direct co-finance will flow from BMZ, SDC and GCF to the GIZ head office. GIZ will be responsible for transferring funds to the implementing entities (IEs) in Georgia. The implementing entities (NFA, DES, EAE and ARBA) as its role in AB, GIZ, as a co-executing entity, will be responsible for the overall implementation of the project. The GIZ head office will provide support through: (i) an Executive Committee; (ii) administrative, financial and technical oversight and supervision throughout project implementation; (iii) ensuring funds are effectively managed to deliver results and achieve outputs; (iv) providing guidance on the quality of project design, implementation and quality of reporting to GCF; (v) participating as a GCF project coordinator in the Project Implementation Committee; (vi) supporting the GIZ head office in the AB project closure and evaluation. The AB is well suited and prepared for these roles. The following information provided by GIZ demonstrates its capacity to support the readiness of GIZ to serve in the roles earmarked for it in this proposed project:

GIZ is one of the largest international providers of capacity development and technical assistance on climate change worldwide.

### Executing capacity of the executing entity

#### a1:
According to the information made available by the independent TAP in the funding proposal and some of the annexes thereto, the project has been developed with active engagement from the NDA. The GIZ IBS project is one of several projects that form part of the project idea was initiated by NFA, MoPAF and the GIZ IBS project in the summer of 2017 and the concept note was prepared in early 2018. A draft version of the concept note was presented to the NDA and the government stakeholders and received approval for submission to the GOP Secretariat in July 2018 in the form of an endorsement letter from the NDA. The project idea is part of the Forest Sector Reform Strategy programme. Representatives of the NDA have attended cross-sector multi-stakeholder workshops on forest sector reform, which were organized by the Ministry of Environment and led by the NFA (see annex 1 to the funding proposal), confirming that the project conforms to the country's national strategy for forest sector reform as well as relevant laws and regulations. It is important to point out that the involvement of some of the leading stakeholders in the development of this project idea has been a key factor in the earlier work of GIZ in this sector. This earlier activity of these entities, coupled with their involvement in the design of the proposed project, starting with the development of the concept note to the final document, will ensure that the project is well aligned and that the capacity for them earned for them. Furthermore, the project management capacities of each of these national executing entities have been assessed by GIZ and the identified gaps have been included in the capacity-building programme of the proposed project. Various workshops were organized with representatives from the proposed executing entities, which provides additional assurance that these executing entities will be able to effectively manage this project. The other two receiving entities are NPA, DIF, representing the State of Georgia, EERD and ARDA.

#### a2:
According to information included in the funding proposal, the GIZ project preparation team met with representatives from the National Forest Authority (NFA) and the civil society during several missions, including 25 planning workshops, a separate consultation workshop with national stakeholders, and meetings with key actors at the district level in the target regions. The independent TAP concludes that adequate stakeholder consultations, including with women's groups and relevant non-governmental organizations, have been carried out.

### 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

#### Scale: Moderately high

##### 1.6.3 Financial structure

The proposed project is requesting grant financing from GCF and other co-financing sources to support the Government of Georgia in implementing sustainable forest management in the three target regions of Kakheti, Mtskheta-Mtianeti and Guria covering 275,087 ha of NFA-registered forests. The funding for this project is expected to be provided by GCF grant funding which can substantially reduce demand for fuelwood in the household sector in these target regions. The funding structure also includes co-financing from other sources such as grants, in-kind contributions and loans. The breakdown of the funding contribution from these sources, the types of hand and the total that will be provided for the project is presented in table 2.

## Co-financing/leveraging

### a) The project’s rate of cumulative total finance leveraged is 1:6 (GCF = total leverage).

The effectiveness of co-financing can be measured by the fact that each euro (EUR 1) from GCF will crowd in about 6 euros (EUR 6) of co-financing. The co-financing that will be crowded in will include both public and private sector contributions, as well as other forms of financial support (e.g., grants, technical assistance, etc.) which terms were not provided in the funding proposal; and the rest as a combination of grant, in-kind contributions and guarantees (EUR 10.19 million), amounting to about 94 per cent of the total co-financing. The Government of Georgia constitutes the majority of the non-debt contribution (EUR 118.06 million), amounting to about 75 per cent of the total co-financing, and is a key partner in the project. Key financial leveraging volumes and performances presented in the funding proposal can be summarized as follows:

(a) Total finance leveraged = EUR 184.71 million;
(b) Requested GCF amount = EUR 12.79 million;
(c) Public source co-financed = EUR 614.61 million;

### Note
* Including co-financing from CRYSTAL for EE stoves and alternative fuel loans.


---
**Page 138**
---

(d) Private source finance leveraged<sup>18</sup>: EUR 123.57 million;

(e) Total leverage ratio ((e) = (b)/(a)): 1:6;

(f) Public source co-financing ratio ((f) = (c) / (b)): 1:2; and

(g) Private source leverage ratio ((g) = (d) / (b)): 1:4.

as The independent TAP is of the opinion that these financial leveraging ratios indicate sound cost-effectiveness of the project when compared with benchmark forestry projects recently funded by GCF.

Considering that the successful implementation of this proposed project will deliver concrete benefits to the environment and the local population, particularly in rural communities in the three target regions of Kalabett, Miskheta-Mtianeti and Goria, the independent TAP considers that the proposed project is an effective way of utilizing the GCF funding.

### 1.3 Cost efficiency

50. The project is expected to deliver about 16.1 MgCO<sub>2</sub>e of GHG emission reductions over a 20-year period. The efficiency metrics that will be delivered by this project intervention were estimated as follows:

(a) Total project financing*: EUR 179.6 million;

(b) Requested GCF amount: EUR 32.79 million;

(c) Expected lifetime emission reductions: 18.14 MgCO<sub>2</sub>e;

(d) Estimated cost per ton of carbon dioxide equivalent removed ((d) = (b)/(c)): EUR 11.06/CO<sub>2</sub>e; and

(e) Estimated GCF cost per tCO<sub>2</sub>e removed: EUR 23.0/CO<sub>2</sub>e.

51. The independent TAP agrees with the conclusion in the funding proposal that the estimated GCF mitigation cost of EUR 22/CO<sub>2</sub>e, as presented above, when added to the project's additional costs (EUR 9/CO<sub>2</sub>e) would result in a total cost per ton of carbon dioxide equivalent money. This was confirmed when the independent TAP compared this metric with the alternative metrics provided by the World Bank and the GCF in their respective sections of the funding proposal (FP019, Ecuador, titled “Priming Financial and Land-Use Financing Instruments to Reduce Emissions from Deforestation”), which has an assessment cost of about EUR 25/CO<sub>2</sub>e.

### 1.4.3 Economic and financial rate of return of the investment


---
**Page 139**
---

10. The economic internal rate of return (EIRR) calculated for this project is 34 per cent and the net present value of EUR 83 million, even at a discount rate of 10 per cent and a carbon price of EUR 24/t, is considered to demonstrate a project that will deliver economic efficiency in spite of the carbon price. The carbon price used for this analysis is based on the mid-2016 June price of carbon dioxide (CO₂) allowances in the European Union Emissions Trading System (EU ETS). This carbon price may be reassessed given the mid-June 2019 price of carbon dioxide (CO₂) allowances in the EU ETS. The carbon price used is also consistent with the European Commission’s recommended CO₂ price assumption for forest projects under the Clean Development Mechanism (CDM). For independent TAPs of the opinion that the robustness of the EIRR is also confirmed when a 60 per cent reduction in the carbon price used in the analysis delivers an EIRR that is slightly higher (1.5 per cent) than the one calculated, this should be taken into account for sensitivity analysis.

13. The financial analysis of the project that was reviewed by the independent TAP showed that the component 1 activities will yield a financial internal rate of return (FIRR) of about 2.4 per cent over the 20-year period conventionally used to evaluate the effectiveness of sustainable forest management projects. This is lower than the FIRR calculated for the project presented in the funding proposal, the reason for this low financial viability is due to the fact that component 1 activities will not generate any revenue (activities 1.1, 1.4 and 1.5), while several component 1 activities will generate financial returns after 20 years of implementation (activities 1.3 and 1.6). The independent TAP noted that the project component financially viable even at the lowest concessionary rate. Therefore, the independent TAP concluded that the project is financially viable in spite of the low FIRR for the financial viability of the component 1 activities. This is accounted by the fact when that GCF grant funding is considered for component 1, it would increase the FIRR to 9.7 per cent, just above the threshold level of 5 per cent.

34. For component 2, GCF financing will be utilized only for activity 2.2 implementing community-based forest management to replace conventional logging. According to the financial analysis for this project presented without GCF grant calculated a very high FIRR (about 165 per cent over the 10-year useful lifetime of the stoves), the independent TAP agrees with the conclusion that the adoption of improved cookstoves in Georgia is highly dependent on the adoption of EE stoves, is hindered by their higher price point, product novelty and lack of sizeable production capacity. The independent TAP also agreed with the conclusion that the financial viability of the AE of a GCF grant covering 30 per cent (up to GEL 300 or EUR 100) of the price of each EE stove, as proposed in the funding proposal.

56. The independent TAP therefore concludes that the efficiency and effectiveness of implementation of this proposed project will be high.

1. Introduction

This Gender Analysis and Gender Action Plan has been prepared for the submission to the Green Climate Fund with the proposal “Enabling implementation of forest sector reform in Georgia to reduce GHG emissions from forest degradation”. This assessment aims to provide an overview of the gender situation in Georgia, identify gender issues that may be relevant to the project, and to examine potential gender mainstreaming measures.

The project aims at reducing emissions from forest degradation through sustainable management of forests as a key strategy for climate change mitigation. Forests are recognized by the United Nations as a main driver of forest degradation. The project will result in the reduction of national GHG emissions, equivalent to approximately 5.2 million CO2 over seven years implementation period.

Forests play an important role in Georgia’s economy and environment. They provide timber and wood products that are essential for ensuring protection of water resources and providing clean air. Forests are sources of fuel wood and construction wood materials, provide space for grazing, housing, collection of berries, food and medicinal plants for the local population. Forests also play a significant role in the recreation value of forests.

Long-term effects that forest event besides the universal one also have a differential gender effect: impacts on land use and settlement reflect on women more severely than on men, as they are more involved in agriculture, and as primary care givers users with water and fuel more than men, are more hard hit when facing water scarcity. Women are more likely to be affected by forest fires, which is a common event that involves more women than man, who in many cases are owners and managers of guest rooms and small hotels. Women are also more involved in the collection of firewood for cooking, heating, drying and processing, selling of firewood as the main income generating activity of many men, living in the vicinity of forests. Men go and use forests more often than women not only for fellings trees, but also for hunting and fishing. Women are more involved in the collection of berries, mushrooms, fruits, nuts, manage and control space heating, cooking and water heating.

Effective measures targeting preservation of forest should rest on recognition of existing gender roles and capabilities, as well as the importance of women’s use of forest land. Therefore, before planning any policies for forest regeneration the needs of men and women would be recognized and taken into consideration.

Resting on biological features and traditional role differentiation, gender in many respects determines differing access to resources and opportunities. Gender differences in terms of roles, responsibilities should be acknowledged and addressed. Although, gender equality is understood as both sees having equal opportunities, it should stay at the centre of any policy making or decisions.


In 2015, the UN adopted a set of goals to end poverty, protect the planet, and ensure prosperity for all at this century’s end. The Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) has specific targets to be achieved over the next 15 year. Gender equality has been mainstreamed into each SDG. The goal on gender equality is Goal No. 5 which aims to achieve gender equality and empower all women and girls. It contains nine targets, including: ending all forms of discrimination against all women and girls everywhere; eliminating early and forced marriage; eliminating harmful practices, such as early, forced and child marriage; achieving women’s full and effective participation and equal opportunities for leadership at all levels of decision making in political, economic and public life; recognizing and valuing unpaid care work through the provision of social protection policies and the equal sharing of domestic and household responsibilities. The goal also aims to promote the gender equality and the empowerment of all women and girls at all levels, among others (UN Women).

The proposed project is in line with the aims outlined in the SDGs.


---
**Page 148**
---

2. GCF and GIZ Guidelines for the Promotion of Gender Equality

According to the GCF’s Gender Policy, “proposed projects or programmes submitted to the Fund are required to be aligned with national policies and priorities on gender and with the Fund’s gender policy.” In the GCF’s Action Plan 2019 – 2021 it is further described that core elements include:

- A mandatory social/economic and gender assessment, complementary to the environmental and financial assessments, which should be conducted at project/programme level in order to collect baseline data; and,
- (ii) The project/programme can respond to the needs of women and men in view of the specific climate change issue to be addressed;
- (iii) Identify and design the gender dynamics in order to achieve the objectives of the project/programme adaptation or mitigation goals;
- (iv) Identify and design the specific gender elements to be included in the project/programme activities;
- (v) Estimate the implementation budget;
- (vi) Select appropriate monitoring, evaluation indicators; and
- (vii) Design project/programme implementation and monitoring institutional arrangements.

2. Gender equitable stakeholders’ consultations with the gender parameters provided in the policy.
3. Inclusion of a gender perspective in the application of the mandatory project/programme social and environmental assessments, as well as in the project/programme specific requirements of the Fund’s ESS in accordance with decision B.07/02.
4. Project screening for gender sensitivity at the various stages of the project preparation, appraisal, approval and monitoring and evaluation (M&E) phases, including National Designated Authorities (NDAs), accredited entities, the Secretariat). The updated GCF Gender Policy and Action Plan 2019 – 2021 builds on the previous core elements. The updates and lessons learned can be summarized in five key aspects:

- The policy emphasizes gender responsiveness rather than gender sensitivity. Being gender responsive means that instead of only identifying gender issues or ensuring a “do no harm” approach, it is about taking into account women’s and men’s different needs and interests as well as the language used in UNFCCC decision COP09 (Line Work Programmes) and the Paris Agreement.
- In addition to the above, the policy includes a new requirement: a mandatory requirement during project-level gender action plans.
- The policy also emphasizes that all elements of the project implementation, monitoring and reporting stages as well as regarding roles and responsibilities among GCF, Accredited Entities, and National Designated Authorities/points focal.

The updated GCF Gender Policy is aligned with Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), which make explicit commitments to gender equality both as a stand-alone goal on gender equality and women’s empowerment or as an important element in other goals. The action plan provides portfolio-level gender-responsive indicators to the Action Plan of the updated GCF Gender Policy, including those related to knowledge management, capacity development, monitoring, evaluation and learning.

In line with the above mentioned GCF Gender Policy and respective update, GIZ’s Safeguards + Gender Management System and Gender Strategy require that a gender analysis is conducted at an early stage


---
**Page 149**
---

# Methodology of the Gender Assessment

The implementation of the Gender Assessment (GA) and the Gender Action Plan (GAP) used the following methodology and stages:

- Review all relevant project documentation, including regulatory documentation and the standards that are applicable to the project.
- Analysis of secondary data (existing documentation).
- Information gathering through workshops in Tbilisi and the three target regions in Georgia identified by the project and preparation of the stakeholder engagement report.
- Analysis of primary data from stakeholder engagement process.
- Processing of information.

Procedures of stakeholder engagement were pursued following the review of project documentation and analysis of secondary data. Stakeholder engagement is a tool for identifying and mobilizing project stakeholders (individuals, associations, groups) that are impacted or have an interest in the project. The purpose of establishing this dialogue is to identify the key issues, concerns, and opportunities; facilitate open communication/dialogue between the project and stakeholders; convey a strong commitment towards meeting the requirements of the GIZ, GCF and understand and manage the needs, interests, fears and motivations of all stakeholders involved in the project; ensure that women’s voices are heard by men and provide mitigation measures. The aims of stakeholder consultations were defined as follows:

1) Introducing the project.
2) Identifying issues, conflicts and mitigations from stakeholder perspective;
3) Understanding stakeholder suggestions and concerns;
4) Obtaining information on the current status of the pro-environment behavior (what drives the current behavior and how rural communities might be motivated to change behavior despite of the value of the felled tree) and understanding of socio-economic, cultural and gender barriers associated with the project.
5) Assessing the level of awareness among stakeholders about their role in participation on decision-making on community issues and envisaging effective ways for increasing it;
6) Estimating the potential impact of the project on women’s empowerment and participation in forest management.
7) Assessment of the gender composition of the three departments/institutions of the Ministry of Environmental Protection and Agriculture (MEPA) (National Forestry Agency, Department of Biological Forest Policy, Department of Environmental Supervision and State Forest Management).

Stakeholder consultations were carried out in the period of March to April 2018 in Tbilisi, Kutaisi, and the three Ministry regions. The final report was submitted on 4th May, 2018 to the ESS expert (see also Stakeholder Engagement Report, Annex 7a to the Funding Proposal for details). As a result of the stakeholder mapping process, the following stakeholders were identified and consulted.

# Gender Equality: Georgia by International Indices and National Framework Conditions

Georgia is not among the champions in gender equality. Its standing is rather low on the two most acknowledged international indices of gender inequality carried out annually by UNDP and the World Economic Forum.

According to UNDP Gender Inequality Index (GII), which is a composite measure of inequalities in reproductive health (maternal mortality rate and the adolescent birth ratio), empowerment (share of Parliamentarians, share of women in national parliaments, share of women in ministerial posts) and labor force participation (proportion of women employed among women in working age population). In 2017, Georgia scored 0.683 out of 100 on the GII.

Another composite measure of gender equality, Global Gender Gap Index of the World Economic Forum was introduced in 2006 to measure the magnitude of gender-based disparities on four sub-indexes with scoring from zero (no disparity) to one (total disparity). In Georgia, the score for education and health is 0.85 due to educational attainment (score 0.964), 85 for economic participation and opportunities (score 0.654), 119


---
**Page 151**
---

for political empowerment (score 0.093) and 123 for health and survival (score 0.967) (World Economic Forum, 2018).

Popular perception of gender equality in the country is in line with International Indices, pointing at the low level of equality. According to the results of one national survey, less than a quarter of Georgians (25 percent of men and 21 percent of women) think that there is gender equality in the country (CENIC 2015).

Article 11 of the Georgian Constitution warrants gender equality. Since Declaration of Independence in 1991, Georgia has been committed to the promotion and protection of gender equality and became signatory of all main International Conventions on gender equality:

- The Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW)
- Council of Europe Convention on preventing and combating violence against women and domestic violence
- UNDP 1235 resolution on women, peace and security
- Georgia’s National Strategy for Implementation of Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): 17 principles of sustainable development make explicit commitments to gender equality both as a stand-alone goal and in relation to the other SDG goals. The strategy includes 60 Indicators to gender equality and has 14 indicators, of which 11 have established methodology and standards.

A number of laws dealing with gender equality has been adopted, among them:

- Law of Georgia on Combating Trafficking
- Law of Georgia on the Elimination of Domestic Violence, Protection and Assistance to the Victims of Domestic Violence
- Law of Georgia on Gender Equality

Several institutional mechanisms were established for the achievement of gender equality, and they include:

- Gender Equality Advisory Council in the Parliament with the mandate to develop and monitor action plan for ensuring gender equality
- Gender Equality Department as a standing unit in the structure of the Public Defender’s Office
- The Interagency Commission on Gender Equality, Violence against Women and Domestic Violence Issues

Key findings for proposed project

Legal mechanisms warranting gender equality are in place, but they do not ensure equality. The interventions will focus on how popular perception points on low gender parity. While the project's primary goal is not achieving gender equality, it aims to promote gender empowerment through making existing legal mechanisms viable.

5. Gender Equality in the Public Space: Representation and Participation

Equality is linked with having power to make decisions. Presentation and participation are therefore necessary conditions.


---
**Page 152**
---

# Representation

In order to be adequately considered, women’s concerns have to be voiced and heard. Hence, representation of women in representative, executive bodies and at decision-making level at public and private institutions is necessary, although not sufficient prerequisite for reaching gender equality.

Representation of women in Parliament is low, having only 16 percent of women as MPs, while world average is 23%. In the country’s local self-government bodies, representation of women is even lower; one being a deputy chair and two as heads of committees. Lower than in Parliament is the representation of women in self-government bodies: they comprise only 13 percent of 2058 members of Sakrebulo* (City Councils) out of 64 municipalities.

In project regions, the representation of women in Sakrebulo is even lower than country average; women comprise only 12.7 percent of elected members.


| Municipality | Women | Men | Total |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Akhmeta | 3 | 59 | 62 |
| Khasheti | 4 | 23 | 27 |
| Akhmeta | 2 | 10 | 12 |
| Dedoplistsqaro | 8 | 26 | 34 |
| Ozurgeti | 7 | 38 | 45 |
| Lanchkhuti | 9 | 31 | 40 |
| Chokhatsuri | 6 | 30 | 36 |
| Total | 33 | 227 | 260 |
| % | 12.7 | 87.3 | 100 |

The situation is similar on executive power level. Among eleven ministers, only four are women. These are the Minister of Regional Development and Infrastructure, who at the same time is the Vice Prime Minister; Minister of Justice, Minister of Economy and State Minister for Reconciliation and Civic Equality.

The situation is better in judiciary, where 52 percent of judges are women. Among 58 ambassadors of Georgia only six are women (Women and Men, 2018).

Men form the majority of public servants. Among 74,532 employees of 169 public authorities of Georgia, majority (55%) are men. The same is true for law enforcement agencies. Gender imbalance is especially great in law enforcement agencies, where men comprise 88.2 percent of the staff. The situation is better in all other spheres of public administration, but still gender imbalance exists.

Gender imbalance is especially apparent at high-ranking positions, even excluding male-dominated law enforcement and military bodies: women occupy 70.1% and only 29.7 percent of decision-making positions. Inequality is more evident in self-government bodies where women comprise only 24 percent of high-ranked officials (EPI,2013).


* Sakrebulo - a representative body, which exercises self-governance in the municipalities as defined by the Organic Law of Georgia on Local Self-Government.


---
**Page 153**
---

Despite the little representation of women on decision-making positions, the attitude of the population towards female leadership both at national and local levels is positive. Many surveys demonstrated that both men and women favor women’s participation in public sphere. For example, Population’s Life Experiences Survey (2013) showed that 85 percent of men think that women make good leaders, while 94.7 percent of women think that women make good local leaders. Further, 89.5 percent of men and 93.4 percent of women consider women as good candidates for the position of mayor or governor, and even 60.2 percent of men and 61.8 percent of women would vote for a woman candidate to be prime minister of the country. According to an NDI poll Georgians believe that there are too few women in parliament and self-government, therefore 63 percent support quota legislation to increase women’s representation (NDC, 2015).

### Participation

Civil participation in the county is low. Like some other post-communist countries Georgia can be defined as in a state of “never ending transition to democracy”, with public space characterized by poverty and lack of transparency, and with a weak civil society and political culture (Lutzeneyer, 2013). The population is rarely involved in decision-making on important country issues.

Voting dynamics over years are stable and low. In Parliamentary elections’ turnout in 2008 was 55.2 percent, 59.5 percent in 2012 and 51.6 percent in 2016. The voting rate on presidential elections was 46.1 percent in 2007, 39.5 percent in 2011 and 48.5 percent in 2018.

The organic law of Georgia on Local Self-Government (2014) provides the legal framework for ensuring public participation.

Article 85 obliges self-government to ensure participation of citizen in the activities of municipality, including sessions of municipal bodies. The law lists five mechanisms for civil participation in local decision-making processes:

I. A general Assembly of settlement, which shall be duty constitute if it is attended by at least 20 percent of its members;

II. A public forum;

III. A Council of Civil Advisors; a deliberative body of a municipality (Gampelguli), or a Mayor or a deputy mayor, the council shall be composed of entrepreneurial legal entities, NGOs and of the municipality population, comprising at least ten members, with one third of representatives of one gender at least;

IV. Public hearings sessions of Municipality Sakrebulo and sessions of its commission; V. Hearing reports on the work performed by GampelguliMayor of the municipality and by a member of the Municipality Sakrebulo.

However, these legal provisions do not warrant participation. National Assessment of Georgian Municipalities points that only 7 percent of population is informed about the work of municipalities. Advisory Councils are established in only 10 out of 89 municipalities (National Assessment of Georgian Municipalities, 2015). According to the study of public policy Transparency International, 2014, only 4 percent of surveyed attended any session of Municipal council, and only 30 percent think that Local Government represents their interests of people like them. A gender difference in these perceptions was not indicated in this study.

### Council of Civil Advisors

Results of the consultations in the framework of the project are in line with above cited estimations. Councils of Civil Advisors have not been established in every municipality. Among eight municipalities which were consulted by the project, two Tavets and Akhvidze do not have a council, and in remaining 11


---
**Page 154**
---

six municipalities, councils have been established only recently and have not yet met (Talati municipality) or meet once in 3 or 4 months and are not effective. Women constitute 39 percent of Civil Advisory Councils in studied municipalities.

| Talati | 10 | 2 | 12 |

In fact, the only way a mayor communicates with the population is through the mayor's trustees. Such mechanism operates in each of the target regions. Each settlement or a group of small size settlements has a trustee who represents the mayor and acts as an intermediary between the mayor and the population and the municipality, spreads information and mobilizes population. During consultations, all interviewed members of the civil society agreed that it is more effective to communicate with the population, mainly through trustees, but also through meetings in villages, is the most effective way of public consultation.

In Kakheti regional office, the male representative evaluated the engagement of its population as very low. He accounted this to the lack of trust towards authorities.

"Generally speaking, people think that their ideas will not be considered."

The same reason for low level of public engagement was cited in Kvirila municipality.

Indirect indication of the validity of such explanation comes from a positive increase of participation and women's representation in local government bodies after the renewal of "The Village Development Program" by the Ministry of Infrastructure and Local Self-Government. The Ministry assigns a sum of money to each village for implementation of projects. The amount allocated to each municipality adds half of its amount from local budget. The decision on selecting the project to be implemented is made in a meeting organized by the mayor's trustee with the village assembly, which is deemed eligible if it consists at least of 30 persons of either the gender. In this project implementation phase, the local population will be hired as manual workers.

"Village Development Program serves as a perfect tool for the engagement of population in displaying their ideas and suggestions to the government or collecting information on problems in community." (Male regional administration representative of Kakheti)

### Mayor's advisors on gender

Special legal provisions are made for increasing women's participation in all governing bodies. Amendments to the Constitution of Georgia dated 2017 points out that a mayor can determine the civil servant responsible for gender equality issues. As no provision was made in the law regarding building capacity of the mayor's advisor on gender issues, it often is of formal nature and does not

# Women Rooms

In order to strengthen women’s participation, since 2012 municipalities are supposed to have women’s rooms. The idea is that a women's room according to the regional administration of Kakheti is to create a venue for a dialogue between stakeholders: women and men alike. All municipalities in Kakheti have Women’s Rooms. They were established in 2018 by Mercy Corps and EKSESPER, so that the region now has a total of 34 women's rooms. The rooms are located in various buildings such as schools or offices of lormugloko. The activities carried out in this space so far are limited to giving advice about municipally functions, organizing meetings with other stakeholders and providing information on gender equality. They do not have any strategic plans for using the rooms, although some, e.g. in Akhmeta are currently working on it. In addition, they organize various events such as training sessions, workshops, seminars and organizing language courses. Today, not having the overall strategy, the effectiveness of the rooms depends on activity and creativity of their managers. The rooms in all municipalities of Kakheti are equipped with a table and chairs, shelves, a table and chairs and tables and chairs for children, a copying machine and a computer.

"Now we are in the process of defining operational strategy of Women's Rooms in region in order to make them more effective and more vibrant (Female representatives of regional administration in Kakheti).

Note that Guria and Mtskheta-Mtianeti municipalities do not have Women’s Rooms.


## NGOs and CSOs

Voluntary organisations are considered as the most common venues of participation. The Non-governmental sector is developing, Georgia's approach to distribution is very uneven. The overwhelming majority is located in the capital Tbilisi. Presently there are 105 NGOs active in Georgia. There are more organisations present in the region where donors have been active.

About 50 NGOs are working on environmental issues and are considered to be active in the country. Most of them are involved with topics such as: forest management, forests and biodiversity (The Centre of Environmental Information and Education, 2014).

In the consulted regions, the NGO sector is regarded as underdeveloped. In total, ten NGOs are registered in Guria and eight in Mtskheta-Mtianeti. The most active ones are "Guria for Women" and "Women's Association 'Bazaezi'". Two NGOs are active in Akhmeta: "Women for Health" and "Women for Healthy Future". The consultations with the governmental sector as well as with the local population stated that the NGO


---
**Page 156**
---

sector is not active on regional or municipal level. The only exception where the NGO sector as prominently active is Parasi Gorge of Kakheti Region. Some NGOs based in Tbilisi are working on gender issues in the regions, e.g. Taso in Guria and "Women for Peace" in Dedoplistsqaro.

Community Based Organisations (CBOs) are also rare. In Mtskheta-Mtianeti there is only one in Kakheti, in Gori district. There are two CBOs active in Kakheti. Members of Kakheti regional administration pointed to their positive experience with CBOs.

"The decisions are made more easily and effectively in the villages which have CBOs."

The majority of existing community organisations in Kakheti are headed by women and are selected by majority vote.


Cooperatives

Despite repeated attempts to boost creation of business cooperatives, their number is rather limited. Within the country, there are 1,538 registered agricultural cooperatives with 13,977 persons of which 3,674 are women. Among them are 61 cooperatives in which all members are women, numbering 389 persons (10% of total).

The biggest number of cooperatives in largest regions is in Kakheti, where more than 100 cooperatives operate. Their members are mostly men. However, in donor-funded cooperatives, women's participation is considerable. For example, in the cooperative "Kakheti" (in Gori district) the leadership consists of women. Mostly female members form the association of Kakheti as honey producers.

Animal husbandry and viticulture cooperatives are predominately male.


In Guria, 45 cooperatives are registered: 22 in Lanchkhuti, 13 in Durguri and 10 in Chakhtauli. In Tskhinvali region, there is only one cooperative, "Kakheti", which was created with European Union's support within the framework of EU-funded ENPARD program in Mtskheta-Mtianeti zone; some fruit processing small factories were opened. Tskhinvali factory is a cooperative comprising eleven members, out of which seven are women. The factory produces 10 tons of apples per year and sells them to local markets. It is heated both by solar and electric power. During the season, the factory employs additional 200 persons.

While women head many NGOs and CBOs, business cooperatives are rather headed by men. However, some donors consider that the participation of women as a requirement. In these cases, women are usually among the leaders.


Although the majority of existing cooperatives in Georgia are formed in agriculture sector, there are two cooperatives in energy sector: "Saragari" and "Mtskheta". The cooperative's main goal is to install and promote renewable energy and energy-efficient solutions in a gender-sensitive way by replacing the use of firewood and fuel from animal manure with solar water heaters. The cooperative has 10 members, out of which two are women; but gender sensitive in the sense that its charter is established so that their local community can be involved in the process of choosing the most appropriate technology for them.

The main product of the energy cooperatives is solar water heaters, however due to the lack of demand on market and high cost of production, they have not been able to sell any products. The women who have committed themselves to spread awareness on energy-efficient technologies in rural areas.


Current activity status of gender-aware/women cooperatives in the EE/EEF sector is low; however strengthening their role is possible during UCP project implementation via encouraging the involvement

# Key findings for proposed project

* Representation of women in national and local representative and executive bodies is not adequate.
* The level of participation of the local population in decision-making on local issues is inadequate.
* The representation of women in cooperatives is low (10%).
* Self-governance provides a structure for citizens' participation, but it is not effective. Citizen's Advisory Committees are established at municipal and enterprises where they are established; they do not function effectively.
* Intergovernmental relations are fine and are far from being vibrant. Community organisations with elected heads are effective, but rare are few and far from being vibrant. Community organisations with elected heads are effective, but rare are few and far from being vibrant.

## 6. Gender Equality in the Private Space: Decision-Making and Time Poverty

Next to public space, gender inequality is also apparent in private space. Women own less material resources than men do; hence lose power in family decision-making process and experience time poverty. The role of women as a provider of care for children and elderly is very common.

Traditional gender role division in Georgia is still very strong, especially in rural areas. It is supported and practiced by the majority of Georgian society. Women are expected to be responsible for their children's education and household chores while men are expected to earn money for the family and provide for everyone if the man earns money and woman takes care of home and children (UNWOMEN; 2018).

### Household composition

There are more men than women-headed households, which account for 39 percent in Georgia. The share of single mother headed households is bigger than that of single father headed households (GEOSTAT, 2017). Around 54 percent of single member households with children comprise 28 percent. This tendency could be explained on the one hand by a higher life expectancy of women and higher share of unmarried women compared to unmarried men. In general, women-headed households are more vulnerable to poverty (UNDP; 2017).


---
**Page 158**
---

Households comprise on average 3.3 persons. In Kakheti and Guria the number is 3.2 and in Mtskheta-Mtianeti 2.1.

Ownership of family assets

The Law on Gender Equality (Article 10.1) ensures equal ownership rights to men and women. Although inheritance laws are not gender-neutral, in practice, women have the same right as men to inherit a share of parental house and land plots. In households with adult male members property is usually registered on man's name. This practice accounts for the generally disproportionate inheritance. It is more common among older generations. Women report owning less livestock than men do. The ownership of animal husbandry is higher among men. About 80 percent of men and 76 percent of women report owning cattle, while about 52 percent of men and 43 percent of women own sheep and goats. Ownership of large agricultural equipment (GEOSTAT, 2016). Land parcels owned by women are smaller than those owned by men. Women's land ownership is higher in rural areas.

In the project regions women report in Guide 43.1 percent of agricultural land plots, in Kakheti 39.1 percent and in Mtskheta-Mtianeti 38.1 percent (UNWOMEN 2016b).

Decision-making power

Obtaining objective information on who makes final decisions at home is not an easy task. As a rule, opinions differ among spouses and surveys often do not show similar results. E.g. According to the EBRD (2018) survey in Georgia, 54 percent of women consider that they make most joint decisions while the majority considers this right. Both women (56 percent) and men (76 percent) think that man should make decisions on spending income from agricultural production. Women are more likely to agree over spending agricultural income (EBRD, 2018). Another survey points to making joint decisions on spending income from agricultural production among agricultural producers: 73 percent women (41 percent), income from business activities: 67 percent) and income from government benefits (49 percent). Whenever decisions are made mainly by one spouse, in all cases except one, the decision is made by men. In Mtskheta-Mtianeti, 50 percent of women report that they make most joint decisions.

During project consultations, the consultative population declared that spouses or the whole family mostly make the decisions jointly, although some claimed that the household member with the higher income had more power, and this could be either man or woman. Although in reality most often it was the man


---
**Page 159**
---

# Time poverty and household tasks

One of the problems women face is time poverty. The amount of household tasks and caring activities most often coupled with employment and/or farming does not leave much time for women for rest, entertainment and personal development. The lack of access to kindergartens in many regions further aggravates the problem—the only exception is the situation among Bedouin women.

Women are more engaged in unpaid work than men. About 46.3 percent of all tasks performed solely by women, while only 27.3 percent of them are done solely by men; the remaining 30.3 percent are performed jointly (Gender and Generations Survey, 2015). Making repairs and buying bills are the only tasks where men outnumber women. Women spend more time on household chores than men, especially in relation to the care of the children or sick family members. According to survey, women spend 45 hours a week on household chores while men spend just 37 hours; for children, old and ill family members, women spend about 10 hours (UNWOMEN, 2018b).

Women are more engaged in unpaid work than men and agricultural works working as 32.9 percent of women versus only 16.5 percent per year, while men work 20.3 hours a week. Women also spend more days in animal husbandry. The largest gap, constituting 130 days is noted in Kaffeken region. Gender division is apparent not just among the rural population but also among urban women. In contrast to rural women, who usually are seen as one of the women’s duties, while men and women do husbandry of larger animals jointly if men are engaged more in income-generating agricultural and farming works; women usually handle smaller animals such as sheep, goats, chickens and ducks (UNWOMEN, 2016). Women also spend a lot of time working in crops value chains (UNWOMEN, 2016).

## Key findings for proposed project

Despite existing provisions ensuring equality in ownership, men own more material assets than women.
* Decisions at household levels are mostly done jointly by spouses, but in cases of disagreement men more often have a final say.

Women bear the main responsibility as they perform the majority of household chores. Besides, they bear the main responsibility for caring after children and sick family members and all this is often coupled with paid employment or land subsistence farming.


## 7. Human Capital

### Poverty

Poverty associated with high unemployment constitutes one of the main problem of the country. With Per Capita GDP in 2017 being 4,105 USD (World Bank), (2018) 32 percent of population lives under 2.5 USD per day. In 1988 a rural and 14.3 percent of urban dwellers are qualified as persistent poor (GSO, 2018). The number of people living in poverty has decreased from 67.5% in 2007 to 21.9% in 2017 (GSO, 2018). Georgia counts 258,595 Internally Displaced Persons (IDPs) out of a total population of about five million people. The majority of IDPs are women and children who are extremely disadvantaged regarding the access to basic services and social inclusion. Children are at a high risk of poverty and social exclusion. According to UNICEF, 2017, 35% of children in Georgia live with children under 15 is particularly vulnerable to poverty as well. Households with children comprise 78 percent of households living in extreme poverty; the higher the number of children in the household, the greater the poverty risk (UNICEF, 2017).

Poverty was cited as the most urgent problem during consultations. All prospective decisions upon purchasing, installation, maintenance, servicing, insulation and use of alternative fuels were considered mostly only in regard to the prices.


## Economy and Employment

As stated earlier, one of the main reasons for poverty is high unemployment. According to official statistics 12.7 percent of the population is unemployed, but real numbers are much higher, as those having jobs are often underemployed or working in informal employment. In 2015, only 36.3 percent of employed males and 28.6 percent of employed women are hired workers (Kwakwani & Sumbecha, 2016). The female labor force participation rate is 47.9 percent compared to the economically-active population (GEOSTAT, 2015). In 2018, the ratio of female to male labor force participation was 73.4 percent, higher than the world average of 53 (World Bank, 2018a).

More than 23 percent of the population is engaged in informal employment, with men and women equally involved in this type of work. In 2016, 9.5 percent of employed males were self-employed or working as business owners (50 percent) in agriculture; 23 percent provide different services; 8 percent, mostly men, are busy with repairs; 7 percent, mostly women, are doing domestic work and 7 percent are in sales (UNWOMEN, 2018b).

The gender wage gap can be considered as rather big, which amounted to 427 GEL in 2017. Men earned 1,197 GEL against women earning only 770 GEL. Differences in income are caused by both historical and cultural factors. Women are mostly engaged in agriculture, fishing and animal husbandry – traditionally female professions, which are the least remunerated. Women mostly can be found in such professional categories as teachers, nurses, midwives, secretaries, receptionists, administrative services clerks. Men are most occupied in the armed forces; they are legislators, general managers, physical, mathematical and engineering science professionals or associate professionals. In 2015, 47.2 percent of women were employed in the three sectors health, education and culture (World Bank 2018b).


---
**Page 161**
---

In all professions, vertical segregation is also present, with women occupying lower positions than men and correspondingly earning less. For example, women comprise 65 percent of medical staff, but are usually rarely found on high managerial and hence well-paid positions (GEOSTAT, 2018). Employed women often experience dual burden as they have to fulfill their obligations at work and at home. This is due to the fact that women in Georgia are responsible for household chores according to accepted standards. Compensated Maternity leave amounts to 126 days (Article 27 of Labor Code). Additional parental leave amounts to a minimum two weeks per year, maximum twelve weeks until the child turns five years old and up to six months if the mother is employed in a public institution or by an employer who employs pregnant women from being dismissed by the employer, pressing women to restrain from using maternal leave. The law does not provide for any compensation during this period except at the discretion of the Employer. This state warrants only one time-payment in the amount of 1,000 GEL.

Agriculture is the biggest sector of employment, as approximately 43.4 percent of country's territory is designated to agriculture and livestock production. The majority of agricultural workers are women. However, women are more engaged in low-value-added and a small-scale agricultural production (UNWOMEN, 2018). About 40 percent grant beneficiaries of the programmes "Produce in Georgia" and "Supporting Women Farmers" are women. In addition to this, there is no specific support for women among large-scale grant beneficiaries. Gender equality issues are not reflected in the Regional Development Strategies 2017-2021 and one not detailed in Georgia's Rural Development Strategy 2017–2020.

The stakeholder consultations demonstrated that although the proportion of economic activity of women is increasing, women still remain underrepresented. Women earn less than men and women still remain the principal earners. The main economic activities in the studied regions concern: agriculture, animal husbandry, services and employment in public institutions. Many women are employed as seasonal workers.

Small-scale agriculture and farming constitute the main income and subsistence sources for the local population. In Guria, seasonal work in Turkey also plays a significant role. In Kakheti wine production, grain and fruit processing, and cattle breeding are the most important activities. These activities mostly are associated with also development of touristic activities. The wine route – a network of vineyards, often with wineries, restaurants, hotels and guesthouses is a favourite touristic attraction, especially during autumn. While in wine production more men than women are engaged, the opposite can be said of hospitality business that is more in the hands of women's.

In all types of agricultural works and animal husbandry, both women and men are equally engaged. Nevertheless, women are mostly involved in post-harvest activities such as sorting, packaging, cleaning and production while, women are busy to production of milk products. In agriculture, mechanical works are more often performed by men than women. Women are mostly employed as seasonal workers or as daily hired labourers. Here, in most cases the work is also gender specific. In all regions, there are organized women's teams for post-harvest activities such as sorting and packaging of fruits and vegetables. The teams mostly mixed gender. The teams are well organized, ensure contracts and transportation. Women teams are also mostly led by men. Men and women get equal pay, the amount of course depends on the task. A female worker in a fruit processing factory in Guria, for example, is paid 10 lari per day (GEL) for sticking labels to bottles and for doing similar tasks.

In Guria, the main crop is maize. Hazelnuts have been the main import product of the region, but it is now replaced by wine production. Women are employed in a number of work in sewing factories in Poti where they commute daily. In total 200 women are employed in Ourguz in sewing factories of famous brands.


---
**Page 162**
---

Unemployment is the main obstacle for men and women equally throughout the country – the same applies to the targeted region. It stimulates both migration to other regions in Georgia as well as migration to other countries. Mountainous villages are especially threatened, e.g. in Mtskheta-Mtianeti region out of 293 villages, 50 are deserted, in addition in 50 villages the population does not exceed ten persons.

The division of labour in the concerned regions is evident as cited previously in this report. Generally, women in these regions are more involved with agricultural activities and men are more involved with monetized activities. There is a risk that the project will further accentuate the division of labour and benefits for women are not maximized if no actions are to be taken to minimize this risk. Section 3 therefore describes the proposed measures to ensure the positive benefits for women are maximized.

Health

Women's life expectancy in Georgia exceeds men by 14 years – 72.7 years for women and 58.6 years for men in 2014. Women constitute 62.2 percent among those over 65 years of age in Georgia. In total, 40 percent of men and 25 percent of women evaluate their health as good or very good in representative survey (CRRIC, 2016), although the share of those with registered disability status is higher among male adults compared to female adults.

As can be seen from Table 5 in the targeted project regions occurrence of disability is high. In Guri-3.4 percent of males and 3.1 percent of females; in Khasheti 3.1 percent of males and 2.5 percent of females and in Mtskheta-Mtianeti 3.1 percent of males and 2.6 percent of females have a registered disability status.

Maternal mortality remains a problem. In 2013, World Bank estimates show that it was 23 per 100,000 childbirths (Transparency International, 2016).

Education

Georgia has a good record concerning education. Girls constitute 47 percent of basic level and 49 percent of high school graduates. Girls outperform boys in national exams in almost all subjects including Science, Technology, Engineering and Mathematics (STEM). The share of girls and boys is


---
**Page 163**
---

similar among Vocational Education and Training (VET) graduates, but with 54 percent girls form the majority of University students (Sumatra, 2015).

## Domestic violence and early marriage

Domestic violence and early marriages are the barriers to health and education, concerning in much greater extent women than men. About 81 percent of population considers domestic violence and 72 percent consider early marriage as a serious problem. The number of registered cases of domestic violence since 2015 show 50 percent increase, though some experts account this not to higher incidence rates, but rather to higher reporting. Incidences of domestic and gender-based violence correspond to global and regional trends. In 2013, the total number of registered cases was 487 women and 69 men as victims of domestic violence and 303 women and 1,492 men as perpetrators (DIFR, 2019).

### Regions of Georgia differ by the reporting incidences of domestic violence

In years 2013-2015, the least number of registered cases was in Guria. The highest number of registered cases is in Kakheti. The number was one of the highest in the country. When the number of incidences are calculated per 100 thousand population, then the highest number of incidences is recorded in Gori (24 percent) by the share of registered incidences, while Guria has one of the smallest shares (5.86 percent).
