### Non-violent incidences of domestic violence are much higher than officially registered

A 2017 nationwide study conducted by the UN Women revealed that one of every seven women experienced sexual abuse during her lifetime. Study findings indicate that women and men show a high degree of tolerance to domestic violence, with 45 percent of women (38 percent) and 69 percent of men (22 percent) and one third of men (31 percent) believing that beating the wife is justified at least under some circumstances. The study also revealed that 70 percent of women and 50 percent of men (42 percent) believe that a wife should obey her husband even if she disagrees (UNWomen, 2018).

### A World Bank study on gender-based violence in Georgia (WB), identified vulnerable domestic violence groups

Compared to the others, women who got married before the age of 18 are much more likely to experience domestic violence. The study also shows that a woman's vulnerability demonstrates that respondents with stressed partners, who are unemployed, regularly drink alcohol and stay away from work, are more likely to be victims of domestic violence than those whose partner has personal income, compared to less than a quarter of men. Men also control most household assets. Even in the cases where women are the legal owners of the property, often they still control the wealth. Women's economic dependence on their husbands is another factor that contributes to their economic independence. This makes them dependent on their spouses. Therefore, they cannot easily report abuse or seek help from others. The study also shows that a woman's education level does not increase domestic violence. This has not been considered an impact nor risk during the assessment


---
**Page 164**
---

process and stakeholder consultations. On the contrary, the project will bring awareness regarding gender issues and will empower women through the implementation of the mitigation proposed measures. Women will not be forced to participate in the employment, business, and community opportunities that are offered. All activities will be identified and properly communicated to ensure that they are managed if they do occur.

Another problem impeding development of human capital is early marriage. According to information provided by the Georgian Ministry of Justice, in 2015, 611 child marriages were registered. In 2016, there decreased to 487 cases and in 2017 it was 390 cases. The number of married women under age of a child also declined from 1,449 in 2015 to 1,278 in 2016. However, the figures for the number of minor parents have increased from 1,000 in 2015 to 1,300 in 2016 and 1,400 in 2017. Although there is a decline in the phenomenon continues, but marriages are not registered due to constraints introduced by law. The legal minimum age for marriage is 18 years old according to Georgian Civil Code. Article 35 of the Georgian Civil Code in 2015 postulates that for the marriage of a person younger than 18 years a court decision is needed.

Early marriage negatively reflects on the mother's and neonate's health. It restricts women's education and hence her chances for employment and prosperity. Early marriage very often results in dropping out of school. The Georgian Ministry of Justice (2015) reports that in Georgia, 43 percent of married women is more common among girls than boys. Even when married girls want to continue school education they are not allowed by their parents or husband because they say they do not want their children to have classes together with married women (Sumbadze, 2015).

The surveys noted above demonstrate that early marriage often makes woman more vulnerable to domestic violence (World Bank, 2017). Early marriages are rooted in customs and traditions closely linked with the social structure of the society. According to the World Bank (2017), 43 percent of married women among the Azeri were married before the age of 18, while five percent got married at the age of 15 or younger. In the period of the age at 15-16. As can be seen from Table S. Khatiev (12.4 percent) and Guria (10.2 percent) have a high share of married women younger than 18 years (Vakass & Sumbadze, 2017).

Key findings for proposed project
- Poverty is a main problem of the country as one third of the population lives on less than 2.5 USD a day.
- Unemployment is the main reason of poverty; it is higher among men, than women.
- Gender division of labour is evident in the proposed project regions. Men are more involved in money-generating activities and women in subsistence farming.
- Agriculture is the biggest sector for employment.
- Women spend more time than men in agricultural works, although men are more occupied in value added activities such as processing and marketing of farm produce.
- The majority of agricultural tasks are gender-specific.
- Wage Gender Gap captures 427 GEL and is accounted for horizontal and vertical gender segregation.
- Women's life expectancy by 8.6 years exceeds men's. Women constitute 62.2 percent of persons over 60.
- Women have slightly better educational attainment than men.
- Domestic violence, sexual harassment and other forms of human capital – are more frequent and have heavier consequences for women than men. There is an opportunity to use the project to bring awareness regarding gender issues, including domestic violence and its impacts.

8. Gender-specific Patterns of Forest and Fuel Use

Forest use
The forests are used both by men and women, but with different intensity and purpose. Men go to forests much more frequently than women and they do this for more economic gain, e.g., for obtaining fuel wood and construction materials – not only for home consumption, but also for sale. In case of use of forests for non-economic purposes (e.g., hunting), men are more involved in hunting wild animals such as boars, chameleons, walnuts, bladder nuts and decorative plants. Women refrain from going to forests due to threat of being attacked by animals. However, in cases when they go, they collect mushrooms,


---
**Page 166**
---

raspberries, bilberries, cornelian cherries, "Garzuli"-wild garlic, dog rose and herbs mostly for household consumption, but also for commercial purposes.

Non-timber products are unavailable in wet forests, i.e., forests that stand on wetlands. In Guria, there are no plants to collect for consumption and even taking fuel wood out of such forests is very difficult and time-consuming.


Many people are using forests as pastures. Here, men are more involved with herding.

### Fuel use

Wood for fuel is used universally for heating. Everywhere, notwithstanding having or not having piped gas, people in Georgia use wood fuel for heating. The overwhelming majority of the population heats only one room irrespective of either the size of the house or the number of the household members. In rare cases, a family may heat more than one room. Most families use firewood cut from fuel wood or briquettes, only in rare cases large schools have a central gas heating system.

People most often pay for fuel wood, but many depend heavily on obtaining it out of the forest for free. As one of them stated: "Why do we buy fuel wood, when it is in abundance behind our houses?"

During consultations such opinions were mainly heard from the villagers living in mountainous areas where forests are close to their houses. They also expressed great discontent with the activities of logging enterprises that cut down trees.

In Guria, some villages have plots on which they grow trees for fuel. These are rapidly growing species: elms and acacias, that they cut and use as fuel wood.


Fuel wood is usually cut in the forest by men; this may be a household member or a hired person. However, further splitting of wood, carrying a home and putting it to stoves is performed by women. So far, women are not involved with cutting trees.

Besides heating, cooking and warming water, fuel wood is used for fireplaces, traditional baking bread, distilling "Chacha" - spirit beverage and making preserves.


Nowadays, in many kindergartens in Guria and Dedoplistsqaro, biopellets are used. Teachers, who are almost exclusively women, are responsible for the use of these stoves.

"We are very satisfied with briquettes; they are easy in use and depending on the type of tree they are made of, give much warmth" (Teacher of a kindergarten in Lesa).

The only problem in Guria is the storage where humidity is high, which negatively affects the quality of the briquettes.


### Use of stoves

Some portion of the population uses more-energy-efficient (the so called "Svanetian stoves", which last for about 5 years). However, majority uses the cheaper traditional ones, lasting for just one or two seasons. The choice is determined entirely due to restricted economic situation of the household. In five years period, the cost of fuel wood and briquettes is much higher than the cost of a stove. Therefore, those that more-energy-efficient stoves have a better effect, for many it is difficult to pay a big sum at once.


24


---
**Page 167**
---

"We will buy enhanced stoves if the price would be lower than Swedienst stoves or if it would be possible to pay gradually" (Female participant of consultation in Guriis).

This restriction is more prominent for households who are below the poverty line, single-women households and households comprising only of pensioners.

### Recreational use of forests

The use of forests for recreational purposes is rather rare and differs across regions. In Kalkhet, there are many beautiful waterfalls in the forest, but they are not easily accessible as most of them are steep mountains while there are very few sites such as Guriis and Taniisi. The common problem in Kakheti is their accessibility as they are situated deep up high in the forests. The religious sites are kept very well, but camping is not allowed.

Up to 15-20 km from Angkhri are beautiful waterfalls. Villagers go out, but as for tourists to visit it, the road is needed (Female participant in Angkhri).

The schools rarely take pupils to the forests; parents also seldom take children there.

### Ownership of forests

Ownership entails usage right, legal or customary, and protection. The feeling of ownership of forests differs across regions. In Kakheti, the feeling of ownership of forest by villagers is very high among men living in villages bordering forests. The ownership is expressed both in a practical usage of forests by private businesses, and also in understanding of the need for their protection.

"The forest is behind my house, and if I cut the tree today, tomorrow my house may be flooded." (Man in Kakheti)

Villagers living near forests claim a control over forest use for themselves. The population in Zol, an isolated village situated in highlands of Guria, proposed to form village committee, which will check the acquisition and distribution of wood materials for the construction or repair of their wooden houses, and will issue cutting permits.

### Key findings for proposed project

- Wood for fuel and as construction material is used frequently
- Mostly women use wood fuel and control their consumption
- Men use wood mostly for construction purposes. In some places, men do not go to the forest at all. Men use forest moorings for obtaining wood fuel and construction materials and collecting economic products such as mushrooms, berries, honey, etc., but they do not enter forests for collecting berries, fruit, and herbs mostly for home consumption, but for selling as well.
- The feeling of ownership of forest differs by the distance of settlement from the forest. It is much higher in villages situated close to the forest than those situated far away from it
- Attitude towards using briquettes is positive due to experience of their use in public institutions. As the economic situation improves, more and more employees are switching over; they are most effective proponents of briquette use.
- The attitude for recreational purposes is not widely spread. Children are rarely taken for camping into forests. A bit more frequent are sites of worship, but their remote location and lack of roads makes them difficult to access.

In the regional branches of the NFA, similar gender imbalance exists. In Kalineli, out of 44 employees, only five are women. They work in the sphere of analysis (three persons) and services (two persons). Gender stereotypes also influence the choice of education for young people. Women staff and heads of territorial departments of targeted regions as noted in sex differences, namely physical strength and endurance needed for fulfilling the tasks associated with being a ranger, in most cases an entry point to the profession is forestry school. The majority of women who pursue this profession are not engaged in actual inspection of forests.

### Education in forestry

There are not many possibilities for acquiring the profession of a forester. All three levels of education in forestry, Bachelor, Master and PhD are provided by only two Universities, namely the Agriculture University of Georgia and Georgian Technology University. Master's and bachelor's degrees are available at the Agricultural University of Georgia, while the Doctoral degree is offered by the Georgian Patricracy. Master’s degrees are provided at Ira University, Vocational education in forestry (III., IV. V levels) can be obtained from vocational schools: Tbilisi State Forestry College, Gori State Forestry College and Jawaharlal Nehru Private School. Vocational education programme - Forest Work Specialist (Level V) is available only at the Agricultural University of Georgia. The same applies to VET college "NEW WAVE" and in Mtskheta, VET college "Tsinandaliyovaniakva". For the period of 2010- 2014, the total number of Bachelor Forestry programs graduates was 357 people. Among them, 29 graduates were awarded a Master's degree and one received a PhD degree at Agricultural University of Georgia. The most problematic issue students are facing is a poor knowledge base, lack of motivation, insufficient access to information on forest resources, inadequate infrastructure and materials, and poor opportunities for on-the-job and mid-career training (Forest Education, 2015).

### General Conclusions and Implications for the Project

It has become obvious that women on the one hand are still significantly impacted due to gender stereotypes, but on the other hand also have the potential to be important change agents in achieving the overall goal of reducing GHG emissions from deforestation. However, to strengthen women in their agency capacities is only one pillar to address gender equality and therefore organizational structures also need to be supported in order to provide an enabling environment for gender-responsive governance.

Based on the assessment, five intervention areas are identified:

1) Strengthening of gender competencies in partner structures
2) Gender mainstreaming in project implementation
3) Women as key actors and target group
4) Access of women to resources and benefits of the project
5) Gender Mainstreaming and data collection


---
**Page 170**
---

10.1 Specific interventions for strengthening gender competencies in partner structures

As the analysis shows, gender is still in most partner institutions a concept either unknown or at a very nascent stage of being mainstreamed. Taking into account the specific role of women in the project concerning forest resources, forest products and their uses, it is necessary to consider the project and decision-making as it is crucial to create space for action, strengthen gender competencies and recognize gender aspects in the respective partner organisations. It will enable organizations to implement the Forest Sector Reform and promote development for energy efficient appliances and alternative fuels in a gender-sensitive manner.

The following interventions in the partner structures have to be considered to close specific gender gaps identified by the analysis:

• With the new mandate under the Forest Sector Reform of also supervising forest use, the Department of Environmental Supervision will in future engage even more with local population to collect data on forest resources and their uses. This is a crucial task for which both men and women alike are needed. It is therefore crucial to capacitate the Department on gender-differentiated aspects of forest, wood and non-timber forest products use. Further, it is essential to ensure gender-mainstreaming in all activities related to forest resources and their uses. This will be done by considering the gendered nature of patterns in forest land use. For the institutionalization of gender perspectives in the Department, a Gender Focal Point (GFP) needs to be identified within the institution (MoEPA, NFA, DES, ARDA). These focal points will ensure that gender aspects are considered in the planning and implementation of all activities related to forest resources and their uses institutions. Once respective persons are identified, training needs need to be addressed based on clear terms of reference of their role as gender resource persons. In addition, a network among them is needed for sharing experiences and lessons learned.

• Changes in cultural attitudes towards accepting women in non-traditional jobs do happen. However it is important that the project takes this into account by ensuring that all partners are aware of this. Partners will comply with the project's policy including a Human Resource Policy that will clearly describe gender equality principles. The policy will be extended to capacity building, skill development and training to ensure that specific efforts are designed so that women are included in the training programmes and any community project, such as soil conservation or water harvesting projects. This is also reflected in the Environmental Social Management Plan (ESMP). In addition, all job advertisements will specifically encourage women to apply for jobs. The ESMP will include a gender action plan which will be part of the specific process for the ESA that will be regularly monitored to determine progress on ensuring that gender-related issues are addressed.

• Decisions at household levels, including investment decisions, are mostly done jointly by spouses, but in cases of disagreement men more often have a final say. In addition, many women-led households do not have access to credit and other financial services. This is therefore crucial that in the implementation of consumer financing instruments for EE-AF solutions, the design of such programmes takes into account gender aspects. For example, when promoting fuels receive gender-specific advice on the design of loan programmes based on a need's assessment. This will ensure that gender aspects are considered by the financial institutions.

10.2 Specific interventions to establish gender-responsive framework conditions

Key for success and sustainability of project activities is a) the creation of a legislative and regulatory framework, which safeguards project interventions and b) the development of implementation plans and


---
**Page 171**
---

standard operating procedures, which provide guidance for a medium-term period to all involved stakeholders. Since regulations, plans and SOPs are strategic directions, it is critical that gender-aspects are considered properly when developing and implementing them. Gender-responsiveness and gender-sensitivity should be integrated into the planning process at every stage. The inclusion of women in decision-making processes is an important step for ensuring gender-responsive frameworks. Both intervention areas closely should be linked.

The following processes should be reviewed by a gender expert, guaranteeing that revised or new policies and regulations are gender-sensitive and responsive:

- Sustainable FMPs and business plans to ensure they are gender sensitive and -responsive, equally reflecting areas traditionally used by men and women.
- Standard operating procedures (SOPs) for the implementation of sustainably produced fuelwood and implementation of SFM by NFA / on forest supervisions by DES / on the financial investment institutions.
- Policies and regulations for the EEAF sector.

Any further regulations and policies that might take place and be advised by project staff will also have to be reviewed to ensure gender-responsiveness and gender-sensitivity.


### 10.3 Specific interventions to include women as key actors

The assessment shows that women face constraints to participate in decision making in their households and communities. In most cases, women are not involved in the decision-making process; men have more power in the private realm as they have a higher income and own and control more property than women do. Women’s economic activities are often informal and less remunerative, being affected by it on a larger scale than men are. Women-headed households, single member families, which hold a large share of the population, face additional constraints in accessing resources and are more vulnerable persons. Moreover, women are more prone to time poverty as they usually combine employment with care-giving responsibilities, leaving almost no time for leisure activities and personal development.

To achieve the overall project objective, it is critical to include women as key actors and change agents. By acknowledging women as key actors, their role and visibility within the society will be increased, which in turn will increase their influence on decision-making processes at all levels of society; even by including women more actively in planning and decision-making processes, the measures need to be identified for ensuring that they are implemented effectively. The overall project should focus on the following measures to integrate women as key actors and decision makers:

To address the aspect of time poverty, the project activities (such as trainings and consultations) should not take part in the evening hours, during weekends and over longer periods of time (more than two hours). In order to ensure that women can participate effectively in training sessions if possible, the provision of childcare services during the planned measures and activities should be made available. The project should also consider organizing separate training sessions for women held in the women rooms (in Kalahandi).

In the context of gender-sensitive and sustainable forest management plans for Guna, Khatra and Mishikuda-Mainali, women need to be included in the planning and decision-making processes regarding FMPs. Based on the minimum target of 30%, share of women, any consultation processes with regard to the development of FMPs should include at least one-third (30%) representation of the target municipality. Given the low number of women as elected representatives in municipal councils, it is recommended that women be included in the planning and decision-making council. In order to make the most of existing institutional mechanisms, the municipal Gender Focal 29


---
**Page 172**
---

Point should be informed and whenever possible included in processes. The employment of women for fuelwood and timber marketing should be encouraged. Women in general have a higher educational attainment and second, have better understanding and access to the main consumer target group (households). In case of fuelwood, it is essential that access to resources and benefits of the project is ensured and promoted. This includes access to fuelwood, stoves and respective support programmes, financial support programmes, forest products and relevant information.

The project has to ensure that gender gaps in access to resources and project benefits are addressed by the following measures:

Access to fuelwood, stoves and respective support programmes:


---
**Page 173**
---

Access to high-quality fuel is crucial for women, since they are the main users of fuelwood. The new fuelwood delivery mechanism (fuelwood harvesting, drying and storage plus delivery) by NFA ensures that only dry fuelwood of high quality reaches the households. At the same time, it is being used in a way that preserves the forest ecosystem and reduces environmental degradation patterns. Women as main users of fuelwood for heating and cooking benefit via less exposure to tin- house fumes and smoke, which leads to better health outcomes (time saving). In addition, women also save money and via need less re-fueling the appliance (time saving). In addition, men do not have to collect and harvest fuelwood by themselves anymore, which also frees their time for economic activities.

To ensure that quality fuelwood benefits women and reaches also women-led households. NFA should assess the access of women-led households to the new fuelwood delivery mechanism. Women-led households should establish a respective monitoring system to track accessibility.


Other barriers encountered by women include poor access to financial capital and bank loans due to a low level of education, lack of collateral security and limited business skills. To guarantee women's economic participation, measures to support women's access to finance and business development opportunities are essential. NFA should provide training on business management and entrepreneurship to relevant resources necessary for economic development and financial sustainability in Georgia, such as business planning, marketing strategies, record keeping, accounting practices, etc., and provide technical support and market information (Crystal, 2019). Since women are the main users of the new stoves and alternative fuels, access for women to the respective financial support programmes is therefore essential.


The project has to ensure uptake of EE and AF with women-led households and financial incentives are attractive and accessible to women-led households as well as women who are married. The project has to make sure that women-led households are a specific target group, being addressed via targeted awareness raising campaigns (e.g., door-to-door visits, radio, SMS, access to information). This will lead to a high rate of women-led households being specifically informed on the benefits of EE and AF and their respective financial incentives. In addition, capacity building and gender literacy workshops should be organised jointly with the gender councils and gender focal points of the municipalities. Third, a gender impact assessment should be done to assess the accessibility of loan programmes for women-led households and the project has to ensure that these are taken into account accordingly. Fourth, it is recommended that the project commits itself to have at certain percentage of beneficiaries in its respective programmes to be women-led households.


Access to forest products


The assessment has shown that women collect non-timber forest products (NTFP) mainly for non- commercial use. In principle, NTFP also provide a potential source of income for women via value chain development and marketing. The project should ensure that women are allowed to collect NTFPs from forest products for household consumption purposes. It will still be allowed to access the forest and to collect these products. For commercial use negotiations have to take place to include the commercial utilisation of NTFP in the respective business plan of the SFM programme (e.g., marketing, etc.) and women's participation in SFM development.


The project has to ensure that collection of NTFP for personal consumption by women is respected by DES in terms of forest management and proposed measures above). In addition, it has to enable rural communities and especially women to create additional income sources via commercial utilisation of NTFPs. The project should also consider the involvement of local NGOs and other stakeholders as international donor to add a livelihood component to the project. This component will focus on creating


---
**Page 174**
---

additional livelihood value chains for NTFPP products and agro-forestery value chains with a specific focus on women. The component is under development and will be integrated in the project at the end of 2020.

Access to information on forest use and alternative fuels and energy efficient stoves:

Access to information for women is a pre-condition to participate in discussions and to facilitate women's decision-making. Women need to be informed about the changes in forest use, the opportunities linked to it and the benefits that can be derived from it. The project will ensure that all awareness activities are family- and transparent information targeted towards women and women-led households will enable them to take informed decisions on forest use and alternative fuels and energy efficient stoves.

The foreseen awareness activities of the project (Action 2.3) should take these aspects into account by utilizing instruments and mechanisms, which directly target women and women-led households.

To facilitate access to information for this specific group the awareness activities should include the following:

• Include the institutional mechanism of the municipal gender focal point and gender council for advocating purposes.
• Establish collaborations between local information points and municipal gender focal points to reach women and to feed in diverse women's perspectives.
• Focus on the most vulnerable households, specifically on women-headed and vulnerable households in order to increase the information sovereignty among before mentioned households.
• To properly mainstream all awareness materials.

Lastly, household advisory services for EE-AF should specifically focus on women-headed and vulnerable households in order to increase the information sovereignty among before mentioned households.


### Specific interventions to ensure gender mainstreaming

Gender Mainstreaming should be ensured through either external experts or by building up internal capacities. This is essential to ensure that any campaign, knowledge management and compilation of information specific to the forest sector reform and fuel use includes implicitly and explicitly the gender dimension. The project will ensure that all awareness activities are family- and transparent in order to address the availability of gender disaggregated data and needs to be supported throughout the project.

# Annex 1: Legal provisions and institutional mechanisms for ensuring gender equality in Georgia

## 1994
- Accession of the Convention on the Elimination of all Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) without reservations.

## 2004
- Establishment of a Gender Equality Advisory Council in the Parliament with the mandate to develop and monitor action plan for ensuring gender equality.
- Nationalization of Millennium Development Goals (MDGs) MDG 3 – promotion of gender equality and empowerment of women, within the Georgian context through two key targets: (i) ensuring gender equality in employment and (ii) ensuring equal access to activity in the political domain and at all levels of management.

## 2006
- Adoption of the Law on Promotion of Gender Equality by the Parliament.
- Adoption of the Law of Georgia on Combating Trafficking.
- Adoption of the Law of Georgia on the Elimination of Domestic Violence, Protection and Assistance to the Victim of Domestic Violence.
- Adoption of the Law on Equalization of Women’s and Men’s Rights.

## 2013
- Appointment of Prime Minister's Assistant on Human Rights and Gender Equality Issues.
- Establishment of Gender Equality Department as a standing unit in the structure of the Public Defender's Office.

## 2014
- Adoption of the Law on Non-discrimination.
- Signature of Council of Europe Convention on preventing and combating violence against women and domestic violence (Istanbul Convention).

## 2016
- Presentation of joint plan for Nationalization of SDGs. Georgia is among 139 signatories of the Istanbul Convention.
- Approval of Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). 17 principles of sustainable development make explicit commitments to gender equality both as a stand-alone goal and as a cross-cutting theme across all 169 targets. The SDG framework has 203 goals and has 14 indicators.

## 2017
- Ratification of the Council of Europe Convention on Preventing and Combating Violence against Women and Domestic Violence (Istanbul Convention).
- Establishment of the Inter-agency Commission on Gender Equality, Violence against Women and Domestic Violence.

## 2018
- Establishment of Human Rights Department in the Ministry of Internal Affairs.
- Adoption of National Action Plan for 2018–2020 on the Human Rights.
- Adoption of National Action Plan for 2018–2020 on Women, Peace and Security (UN SCR 1325).
- Adoption of National Action Plan for 2018–2020 on the Measures for Combating Violence against Women and Domestic Violence and Protection of Victims/Survivors.

# Annex 8b

## Gender Action Plan (GAP)

of the Project

*Enabling implementation of forest sector reform in Georgia to reduce GHG emissions from forest degradation*

Submitted to the Green Climate Fund (GCF)


---
**Page 181**
---

# Introduction

The rural population of Georgia heavily depends on forests for livelihood. Forests are used for fuel wood, construction materials, hunting, food, collection of berries and medical herbs, honey-keeping, and entertainment.

Project planning often is based on male norm*, excluding women who often lack skills, resources and time for participation in decision-making. The participation of women ensures understanding of activities carried out by men, their needs and interests and enables to incorporate the knowledge they have (IUCN, 2016).

The Gender Assessment of the proposed project demonstrated that although the climate and use of natural resources reflects both at women and men, their impact often is different due to different needs and capabilities that men and women possess.

# The Gender Action Plan (GAP) is designed to ensure that the project:

- Lowers gender inequality;
- Addresses the needs and constraints of women, girls, men, and boys;
- Avoids perpetuating existing gender imbalances;
- Ensures women's participation, promotes their leadership qualities;
- Ensures women are included as planners, co-implementers and agents of change;
- Contributes to reducing the gender gap of climate change-exacerbated social, economic and environmental vulnerabilities.

# Target groups

Project activities should be directed to five main target groups: women, men, youth, educational institutions, project staff and staff of executing entities and implementing partners.

## Men and women

Men and women reveal different patterns of behavior in dealing with the forest and its products. While men go for forests much more often than women, and do so for felling trees, hunting, herding and collecting food products mostly for economic aims, women in forests collect non-timber products mainly for family consumption. Women are the main consumers of fuel wood as a household resource and are responsible for cooking and cleaning. Consequently, they are the best agents of change and multipliers as they are concerned with upbringing of children as in private settings and also at educational institutions, constituting overwhelming majority of its staff.

## Youth

Youth is an important target group as the long-term impact of the project depends on them as decision-makers in the future.

## Educational Institutions - Children and youth, as well as teaching and academic staff can be best reached in educational settings.

## Project Staff and Staff of Executing Entities - Gender sensitivity of the staff and gender mainstreaming in the projects are essential for the implementation of planned activities.


---
**Page 182**
---

# Resources

I. Legal provisions aim to increase women’s participation in decision-making. The law on self-governance lists a number of mechanisms for the civic engagement of the population, however many of these mechanisms are not utilized. These mechanisms could be utilized to strengthen the engagement of men and women within the framework of the project.

II. Women's rooms, which exist at all Municipalities of Kakheti region, can be used as Information centers for women’s participation in decision-making processes.

III. Community-Based Organizations (CBOs), Non-Governmental Organizations (NGOs) and voluntary associations provide a useful context of participation and should be supported by project activities.

IV. One of the partners is a full-time ESS/gender expert within the project to take care of the implementation of the Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) and the Gender Action Plan (GAP) as well as to screen documents, standard operating procedures (SOPs), policies and regulations on gender-sensitivity and responsiveness. The expert has a budget of 18,000 USD for the period from January 2019 until December 31st 2019 (inclusive). Additional budget is already integrated in various activities of the project (see details below in GAP).

# Partners

I. National Forestry Agency (NFA), Ministry of Environmental Protection and Agriculture (MuEPA), Ministry of Education, Science, Culture and Sports, Ministry of Internally Displaced Persons from the Occupied Territories, Labor, Health and Social Affairs, Ministry of Regional Development and Infrastructure, should be considered as the main partner organizations of the project in Georgia.

II. Piaterekh, as the institution enjoying high trust of population, having an interest and expertise on environmental issues, it is running Georgian Patriarchate Community Circle of Decorative Gardens (GCPDG).

III. NGOs working on environmental and gender issues, such as: CCENN, which has elaborated an institutional model and action plan for management of recreational forests in Georgia; has an experience in Kakhet region of organizing Rural Women’s Councils; different activities targeting women’s empowerment and gender equality; NACO implementing rural development action plan for Borjomi forests; TASO working on women's issues and running Rural Women's Groups in Guria. "Women for peace", Tolisa NGO with activities in Dedoplistskaro, aiming at combating Domestic Violence.

IV. Media campaigns, including social media, should be used for awareness raising campaigns. The use of social media is also to be supported.

The specific gender action plan of the project is presented below:

# Gender Action Plan

## Output 1: Sustainable Forest Management (SFM)

### Activity
#### 1.1. Development and implementation of sustainable forest management plans in Gurka, Kalikhet & Mithila-Mauntai

- **Actions and Targets**: Women are included in and own the planning and decision-making processes for developing forest management plans (FMPs). In consultation processes with local communities it is ensured that women's voices are heard and their participation reaches ideally 50%, reflecting the gender composition of the target municipalities. When working with municipal councils ensure that all elected local representatives, including women, participate in meetings as well as given the time and space to speak (given the already available resources). Gender expert will revise the FMPs and business plans to ensure they are gender-sensitive and inclusive, reflecting areas traditionally used by men and women but also ensuring that women's needs are addressed. This process needs assessment of gender focal points regarding gender-specific areas on SFM.

- **Indicators**: Maximum 50% of participants in the FMP and business plan development consultations are women. Baseline: 0% Target: 50% (Quarter 4, year 2) 100% of FMPs and business plans are at least gender-sensitive or inclusive, where applicable. Baseline: None Target: 100% (Quarter 4, year 2)

- **Responsible Institution and resources**: DES; GIZ ESST-Gender Expert

#### 1.2. Strengthening of forest supervision

- **Actions and Targets**: The overall training system for the Department of Environmental Supervision (DES) includes one training for all staff on gender equality in forest management.

- **Indicators**: One training module on gender equality in the DES plan is implemented.

- **Responsible Institution and resources**: DES

1.3. Provision of sustainably produced timber by the National Forestry Agency (NFA)

- Ensuring the forestwood delivery system is accessible for women, and that a woman-headed organization or a gender-responsive monitoring system to track accessibility.
- Gender-sensitive selection criteria for SOFs will be used with a gender expert to ensure they are gender-responsive and enable gender-responsive monitoring. Encourage NFA to employ women for fuelwood and timber marketing.

1.4. Enhancement of enabling environment policies for the nation-wide implementation of ecosystem-based SFM

- Gender expert to review revised policies and regulations so that they are gender-sensitive.
- All training materials at the knowledge management and training platform are revised by the gender expert to ensure they are gender-sensitive.
